Mahāyāna Scriptures › Treatise on the Stages of Yogic Practice › Fascicle 48
Fascicle 48 · 瑜伽師地論卷第四十八 · The Abode of Heightened Discipline, the Second Ground

Ten Minds Made Pure, and the Ground of Stainlessness

Continuing directly from fascicle 47's complete account of the abode of utmost joy, this fascicle opens with the second of the bodhisattva's thirteen abodes — heightened discipline, matching the Ten Grounds Sutra's own ground of stainlessness — examined through nine aspects: purified aspiration (the ten minds carried forward from utmost joy, now fully ripened), innate precept-fulfillment, freedom from every violation, thorough understanding of karma and its fruit, personally practicing and encouraging pure conduct, compassion for suffering beings, purified wholesome roots (likened to gold refined further in alum), rebirth as a gold-wheel king ruling four continents, and majestic power tenfold greater than the first ground's own.

Translator: Ru-Yi Meditation Center Status: in progress Source: Taishō T30, no. 1579, fascicle 48 Commentary guide: Master Cháng Bǎi's lecture notes

1. Milestone: Fascicle 48 Opens — The Ten Minds Carried Forward From Utmost Joy, Now Fully Ripened

Fascicle 48 opens still within the second yoga foundation of sustained practice, continuing the chapter on the bodhisattva's thirteen abodes exactly where fascicle 47 left off — fascicle 47 having covered the lineage-abode, the abode of resolute practice, and the complete eightfold account of the abode of utmost joy; this fascicle takes up the remaining abodes from the abode of heightened discipline (the second ground) through the abode of ultimate accomplishment (the tenth ground), together with the overall qualities, eon-spans, cultivation and severance, purity, and mutual inclusion shared across all the abodes, likened also to the hearers' own abodes — and, further ahead, the third yoga foundation of ultimate practice's own chapter on modes of rebirth and chapter on being received into buddhahood. The treatise opens by posing its now-familiar question: when a bodhisattva at the abode of heightened discipline turns, what conduct, what state, what mark should be understood? The answer begins by explaining that this abode is reached through the very same ten minds of aspiration already perfected — though not yet fully so — back at the abode of utmost joy: bodhisattvas, previously at utmost joy, having already attained purified aspiration through ten kinds of mind, now bring that same purified aspiration to its fullest, most excellent grade, and, listing all ten in turn, the treatise names: a mind free of all deception toward every teacher and elder, a genuine field of merit; a mind of patient forbearance and gentleness, easily dwelling together with fellow bodhisattvas in the same dharma; a mind sovereign over every affliction, secondary affliction, and demonic undertaking; a mind that sees deeply into the faults of all conditioned activity; a mind that sees deeply into great nirvana's own excellent benefit; a mind constantly, diligently cultivating the wondrously excellent factors of awakening; a mind, according with that very cultivation, delighting in solitude; a mind entirely without any longing for the world's own tainted status, gain, or reverence; a mind departed from the lesser vehicles, oriented toward realizing the great vehicle; and a mind wishing to accomplish every kind of benefit for every sentient being.

Chinese Source

本地分中菩薩地第二持隨法瑜伽處住品第四之二

辰三、增上戒住　巳一、問　問：增上戒住菩薩轉時，當知何行、何狀、何相？

巳二、答　午一、略說　未一、別辨相　申一、意樂清淨　酉一、出意樂　戌一、標　答：若諸菩薩，先於極歡喜住，由十種心意樂，已得意樂清淨。

戌二、徵　何等為十？

戌三、列　一者、於一切師長尊重福田，不行虛誑意樂；二者、於同法菩薩忍辱柔和，易可共住意樂；三者、勝伏一切煩惱及隨煩惱眾魔事業，心自在轉意樂；四者、於一切行深見過失意樂；五者、於大涅槃深見勝利意樂；六者、於諸妙善菩提分法，常勤修習意樂；七者、即於彼修為隨順故，樂處遠離意樂；八者、於諸世間有染尊位利養恭敬，無所顧戀意樂；九者、遠離下乘，趣證大乘意樂；十者、欲作一切有情一切義利意樂。

Ru-Yi Translation

Within the Basic Section, the Bodhisattva Stage, the second yoga foundation of sustained practice, the chapter on the abodes, part two.

Third abode, heightened discipline; first, the question. Question: when a bodhisattva at the abode of heightened discipline turns, what conduct, what state, what mark should be understood?

Second, the answer; first, the brief account; first, distinguishing each mark; first, purified aspiration; first, presenting the aspiration; first, naming it. Answer: if bodhisattvas, previously at the abode of utmost joy, through ten kinds of mind-aspiration, had already attained purified aspiration —

Second, posing the question. What are the ten?

Third, listing them. First, a mind free of all deception toward every teacher and elder, a genuine field of merit. Second, a mind of patient forbearance and gentleness, easily dwelling together with fellow bodhisattvas of the same dharma. Third, a mind sovereign over every affliction, secondary affliction, and the undertakings of every demonic force. Fourth, a mind that sees deeply into the faults of all conditioned activity. Fifth, a mind that sees deeply into great nirvana's own excellent benefit. Sixth, a mind constantly, diligently cultivating the wondrously excellent factors of awakening. Seventh, according with that very cultivation, a mind delighting in dwelling in solitude. Eighth, a mind entirely without longing toward the world's own tainted status, gain, and reverence. Ninth, a mind departed from the lesser vehicles, oriented toward realizing the great vehicle. Tenth, a mind wishing to accomplish every kind of benefit for every sentient being.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi opens by orienting readers to this fascicle's own scope, spelled out in the treatise's own introductory line: fascicle 47 covered the lineage-abode, the abode of resolute practice, and utmost joy in full; this fascicle will carry the remaining abodes from heightened discipline through ultimate accomplishment, plus an overall summary treatment of qualities, eon-spans, cultivation, purity, and mutual inclusion across all thirteen abodes — and, further ahead still, the third yoga foundation's own chapter on the modes of rebirth (calamity-averting birth, birth according to kind, birth of great power, birth of heightened rebirth, and final birth) together with the chapter on being received into buddhahood.

He explains why this second abode opens by simply naming the same ten minds already introduced, in outline, back at utmost joy: those ten minds were already present at the first ground, but only in a still-maturing form; here, having been cultivated to their most excellent, fullest grade, they themselves constitute entry into the second abode — continuity rather than replacement being the governing principle across the entire ten-ground path. He supplies, for reference, the alternate names each mind bears in Vasubandhu's Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra: straightforward mind, gentle mind, tamed mind, wholesome mind, quiescent mind, genuine mind, unmixed mind, undesiring mind, superior mind, and great mind — noting the treatise itself will cross-reference this exact list shortly. Having named all ten minds, he notes, the treatise turns next to explaining how, together, they constitute this abode's own purified aspiration.

2. Milestone: These Ten Undistorted Minds Together Are Called Purified Aspiration

The treatise now closes the first aspect: these ten kinds of undistorted mind, continuously turning and depending on the mind, are for this reason called purified aspiration. The commentary cross-references the Ten Grounds Sutra directly, quoting its own explanation: if a bodhisattva, having already fully accomplished the first ground, wishes to attain the second ground, ten kinds of straightforward mind should arise — straightforward mind, gentle mind, tamed mind, wholesome mind, quiescent mind, genuine mind, unmixed mind, undesiring mind, superior mind, and great mind; a bodhisattva giving rise to these ten minds is thereby able to enter the second bodhisattva ground, the ground free of stain — and these ten undistorted forms of aspiration in this treatise should be understood to correspond, in the very same order, to that sutra's own ten.

Chinese Source

酉二、明清淨　如是十種無倒意樂依心而轉，是故說為意樂清淨。

《披》如是十種無倒意樂等者：十地經說：若菩薩已具足初地，欲得第二地者，當生十種直心。何等為十？一、直心，二、柔軟心，三、調柔心，四、善心，五、寂滅心，六、真心，七、不雜心，八、不希望心，九、勝心，十、大心。菩薩生是十心，得入第二菩薩離垢地。此中十種無倒意樂，如次應知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, clarifying purity. These ten kinds of undistorted mind, continuously turning and depending on the mind, are for this reason called purified aspiration.

Commentary: Regarding "these ten kinds of undistorted mind" and the like — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: if a bodhisattva, having already fully accomplished the first ground, wishes to attain the second ground, ten kinds of straightforward mind should arise. What are the ten? First, straightforward mind. Second, gentle mind. Third, tamed mind. Fourth, wholesome mind. Fifth, quiescent mind. Sixth, genuine mind. Seventh, unmixed mind. Eighth, undesiring mind. Ninth, superior mind. Tenth, great mind. A bodhisattva giving rise to these ten minds is able to enter the second bodhisattva ground, the ground free of stain. The ten kinds of undistorted aspiration here should be understood to correspond in this very order.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through the direct correspondence between this treatise's ten aspirations and the Ten Grounds Sutra's own ten minds: freedom from deception toward teachers matches straightforward mind (a genuinely upright mind, free of twisted discrimination, being itself the very ground of practice); patient, gentle ease dwelling with fellow bodhisattvas matches gentle mind; sovereignty over affliction and demonic undertaking matches tamed mind (having already subdued the root afflictions of greed, hatred, delusion, conceit, doubt, and wrong view, together with the secondary afflictions, such a mind is no longer coarse and unruly); seeing conditioned activity's own faults matches wholesome mind; seeing nirvana's own benefit matches quiescent mind; diligent cultivation of the factors of awakening matches genuine mind (a mind genuinely, sincerely committed to practice); delight in solitude matches unmixed mind; freedom from worldly longing matches undesiring mind; orientation toward the great vehicle matches superior mind; and the wish to benefit every sentient being matches great mind.

He notes the sutra's own explicit naming of the resulting ground — "the ground free of stain" — as the treatise's own first direct confirmation that "heightened discipline" and "the ground of stainlessness" name one and the same reality, a point the treatise itself will make explicit again at this abode's very close. With purified aspiration now fully explained as the first of nine aspects distinguishing this abode, he notes, the treatise turns next to the second aspect: the bodhisattva's own innate fulfillment of precepts.

3. Milestone: Innate Precept-Fulfillment, and Freedom From Even the Slightest Violation

The treatise now explains the second and third of nine aspects. The second is innate precept-fulfillment: precisely because these same ten kinds of aspiration have now reached their most excellent grade, utterly complete, such bodhisattvas are thereby able to enter and be certified into the second abode of heightened discipline, and within this abode, the precepts belonging to their own inherent nature are now entirely fulfilled. The third is freedom from every violation: even the very slightest evil conduct subsumed within a wrongful course of karmic action, violating the precepts, no longer manifests at all — let alone conduct of a middling or superior grade — and, regarding the ten kinds of perfected course of karmic action, these now manifest spontaneously, of their own accord. The commentary specifies that this passage concerns specifically the purity of restraint-precepts: departing from killing all the way through departing from wrong view constitutes the ten perfected courses of karmic action, and manifesting purely in these courses of action without needing deliberate discrimination beforehand is exactly what "manifesting spontaneously" means.

Chinese Source

申二、性戒具足　即由如是十種意樂成上品故，極圓滿故，是諸菩薩入證第二增上戒住。於此住中，性戒具足。

申三、離一切犯　極少邪惡業道所攝諸惡犯戒尚不現行，況中上品。又於十種圓滿業道，自性顯現。

《披》性戒具足至自性顯現者：此說律儀戒淨應知。遠離殺生乃至遠離邪見，是名十種圓滿業道。於此業道清淨現行，不由思擇，是名自性顯現。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, innate precept-fulfillment. Precisely because these ten kinds of aspiration have become of the most excellent grade, utterly complete, such bodhisattvas enter and are certified into the second abode of heightened discipline. Within this abode, precepts of their own inherent nature are fulfilled.

Third, freedom from every violation. Even the very slightest evil conduct subsumed within a wrongful course of karmic action, violating the precepts, does not manifest at all — let alone conduct of a middling or superior grade. Furthermore, regarding the ten kinds of perfected course of karmic action, these manifest of their own inherent nature.

Commentary: Regarding "from innate precept-fulfillment to manifesting of their own inherent nature" — this concerns the purity of restraint-precepts, one should understand. Departing from killing all the way through departing from wrong view are called the ten kinds of perfected course of karmic action. Manifesting purely within these courses of karmic action, without recourse to deliberate discrimination, is called manifesting of their own inherent nature.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains "innate" precisely: a first-ground bodhisattva already keeps the precepts, but still through some measure of deliberate effort and vigilance; a second-ground bodhisattva's own precept-keeping has become so thoroughly internalized that body, speech, and mind simply never incline toward violation at all, the way a habit fully mastered no longer requires conscious attention — hence "the precepts belonging to their own inherent nature" rather than precepts merely maintained through discipline imposed from outside.

He notes the vivid comparative structure the treatise uses — not even the very slightest violation, "let alone" anything middling or severe — as deliberately setting a floor far below which ordinary moral effort operates: an ordinary practitioner might struggle daily against major transgressions, whereas a second-ground bodhisattva's own baseline is already free of even trivial lapses. He identifies the ten courses of karmic action by their classical content — refraining from killing, stealing, sexual misconduct, false speech, divisive speech, harsh speech, idle speech, covetousness, ill will, and wrong view — noting that "manifesting of their own inherent nature" is the technical mark distinguishing this ground's effortless purity from an ordinary being's own effortful, case-by-case moral deliberation. Having explained innate precept-fulfillment and freedom from violation, he notes, the treatise turns next to the fourth aspect: thoroughly understanding karma, its cause, and its fruit.

4. Milestone: Thorough Understanding of Cause and Fruit, Ripening-Fruit and Outflow-Fruit

The treatise now explains the fourth aspect in two parts. First, regarding cause and fruit: such bodhisattvas, precepts of their own inherent nature now fulfilled, are able, with wondrous wisdom, to genuinely know exactly as it is whatever pertains to tainted or untainted conduct, to unfortunate or fortunate rebirth, and to karmic action currently manifesting across every vehicle — hearer, solitary-realizer, and bodhisattva alike — together with these vehicles' own causes, fruits, and the way their cultivation and realization is established in teaching. Second, regarding the course of karmic action specifically: such bodhisattvas genuinely know exactly as it is the ripening-fruit and the outflow-fruit belonging to these very actions — ripening-fruit determining which realm of rebirth (human, divine, hell, hungry ghost, or animal) a given karma leads to, itself karmically neutral regardless of whether the originating action was wholesome or unwholesome; outflow-fruit instead resembling its own cause in kind, a wholesome cause yielding a pleasant result and an unwholesome cause yielding a painful one, cause and fruit of a piece and continuing in series.

Chinese Source

申四、了知因果業道　酉一、於因果　菩薩如是性戒具足，能以妙慧，於染不染、惡趣善趣，及諸乘中諸業現行、若因若果、修證安立，如實了知。

酉二、於業道　於異熟果及等流果如是諸業，如實了知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fourth, thoroughly understanding karma and its course; first, regarding cause and fruit. Bodhisattvas, precepts of their own inherent nature thus fulfilled, are able, with wondrous wisdom, to genuinely know exactly as it is whatever pertains to tainted or untainted conduct, unfortunate or fortunate rebirth, and karmic action currently manifesting across every vehicle, together with cause, fruit, and how cultivation and realization are established.

Second, regarding the course of karmic action. Regarding the ripening-fruit and the outflow-fruit belonging to these very actions, one genuinely knows exactly as it is.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi distinguishes ripening-fruit from outflow-fruit with care: ripening-fruit (the "true ripening," lodged in the store-consciousness itself) determines only the broad category of rebirth a being receives — human, divine, hellish, hungry-ghost, or animal — and this determination is itself karmically neutral, neither wholesome nor unwholesome, however wholesome or unwholesome the originating action was; outflow-fruit, by contrast, resembles its own originating cause directly and continues on in series — killing yielding a short, sickness-prone life as its outflow-fruit, generosity yielding wealth and nobility as its own, cause and fruit sharing a single continuous flavor.

He treats this pairing of aspects — cause-and-fruit across every vehicle, then the specific ripening-versus-outflow distinction within karmic action itself — as together constituting a second-ground bodhisattva's own thoroughgoing mastery of karmic causality, extending even to knowing precisely what rebirth and what subsequent circumstance a given present action will eventually produce. Having explained this thorough understanding, he notes, the treatise turns next to the fifth aspect: personally practicing pure karma and encouraging others to do the same.

5. Milestone: Personally Practicing Pure Karma, and Delighting in Encouraging Others to Do the Same

The treatise now explains the fifth aspect in two parts, corresponding to the purity of the precepts that gather wholesome dharma. First, personally practicing: such bodhisattvas are personally able to presently sever every unwholesome course of karmic action, and personally able to presently receive and undertake every wholesome one. Second, delighting in encouraging others: precisely within this very same practice, such bodhisattvas delight in guiding others toward it, and are able to guide them correctly — the commentary explaining that cause here means tainted or untainted, fruit means unfortunate or fortunate rebirth, cultivation and realization concerns the three vehicles, and the way this is established in teaching concerns specifically the distinctions among the four noble truths, all of this genuinely known; and, knowing as well precisely which karmic actions yield which ripening-fruit and outflow-fruit, such bodhisattvas are personally able to depart from unwholesome courses of karmic action and abide in wholesome ones, and are likewise able to lead others to correctly depart from the one and correctly abide in the other — and it is for this very reason that they delight in guiding others, and guide them correctly.

Chinese Source

申五、受行淨業　酉一、自能受　自能現斷諸不善業，自能現受一切善業。

酉二、樂勸他　即於其中樂勸導他，能正勸導。

《披》能以妙慧至能正勸導者：此說攝善戒淨應知。言若因者，謂染不染。言若果者，謂惡善趣。言修證者，謂於諸乘。言安立者，謂即於彼四諦差別。於此一切皆如實知。又於能與異熟、等流果業，亦如實知。自能遠離不善業道，安住諸善業道；亦令他人能正遠離，能正安住；是故於中樂勸導他，能正勸導。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fifth, receiving and practicing pure karma; first, personally able to receive it. Personally able to presently sever every unwholesome karma, personally able to presently receive every wholesome karma.

Second, delighting in encouraging others. Precisely within this same practice, delighting in guiding others toward it, able to guide them correctly.

Commentary: Regarding "with wondrous wisdom" through "able to guide correctly" — this concerns the purity of the precepts gathering wholesome dharma, one should understand. "Cause" means tainted or untainted. "Fruit" means unfortunate or fortunate rebirth. "Cultivation and realization" means regarding the vehicles. "Established in teaching" means the very distinctions of the four noble truths. All of this is genuinely known. Furthermore, regarding karma yielding ripening-fruit and outflow-fruit, this too is genuinely known. Personally able to depart from unwholesome courses of karmic action, abiding in wholesome courses; likewise causing others to correctly depart, correctly abide — this is why one delights in guiding others, able to guide them correctly.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this fifth aspect as the natural completion of the fourth: understanding cause, fruit, and karmic action thoroughly (aspect four) is not merely theoretical for a second-ground bodhisattva but immediately actionable — personally severing what should be severed, personally cultivating what should be cultivated, and, crucially, delighting in leading others to do exactly the same. He draws a deliberate contrast with ordinary beings: an ordinary person may also wish to encourage others toward good conduct, but without thoroughly penetrating cause and fruit, such encouragement is not reliably correct, sometimes even well-intentioned but misguided; a second-ground bodhisattva's own encouragement, grounded in genuine, thorough knowledge, is correct without exception.

He notes the commentary's identification of this passage with the second of the three bodhisattva precepts — the precepts gathering wholesome dharma (following restraint-precepts, already covered under aspects two and three) — as confirming the treatise's own systematic structure: each aspect of this abode corresponds to one of the three precept-categories in turn. Having now explained personally practicing and encouraging others, he notes, the treatise turns next to the sixth aspect: a bodhisattva's own compassionate regard for the suffering, difficulty-laden condition of all sentient beings.

6. Milestone: Great Compassion for Beings Steeped in Ultimate Suffering, Whatever Their Worldly Fortune

The treatise now explains the sixth aspect: compassionate regard for suffering beings. Toward the realm of sentient beings, defiled by the fault of manifesting every kind of unequal karmic action, whether flourishing or declining, all such beings, without distinction, fall entirely into the suffering of ultimate truth, abide amid genuine hardship, and are pressed upon by every kind of difficulty — a condition profoundly deserving of compassion. Toward such beings, such bodhisattvas arouse a vast, far-reaching compassion, observing this truly, exactly as it is. The commentary specifies this concerns the purity of the precepts benefiting sentient beings, and identifies "the suffering of ultimate truth" with the suffering inherent in the five aggregates of grasping themselves, cross-referencing the immeasurable distinctions among life's own hardships already detailed in the chapter with both examination and analysis.

Chinese Source

申六、照愍眾苦　於其種種不平等業現行過失之所染汙諸有情界，若興若衰，等無差別，一切皆墮第一義苦，并住艱辛，種種艱辛之所逼切，甚可哀愍。於彼獲得廣大哀愍，如實觀照。

《披》於其種種不平等業至如實觀照者：此說饒益有情戒淨應知。五取蘊苦，是名第一義苦。生艱辛苦無量差別，如有尋有伺地說。（陵本九卷十一頁677）依彼當知，種種艱辛之所逼切。

Ru-Yi Translation

Sixth, compassionate regard for suffering beings. Toward the realm of sentient beings, defiled by the fault of manifesting every kind of unequal karmic action, whether flourishing or declining, equally without distinction, all fall entirely into the suffering of ultimate truth, abiding amid hardship, pressed upon by every kind of difficulty — profoundly deserving of compassion. Toward them, one attains vast compassion, observing this truly, exactly as it is.

Commentary: Regarding "toward the realm of sentient beings" through "observing this truly, exactly as it is" — this concerns the purity of the precepts benefiting sentient beings, one should understand. The suffering of the five aggregates of grasping is called the suffering of ultimate truth. The immeasurable distinctions of hardship in being born are as explained in the stage with both examination and analysis (fascicle 9, page 677 of the Nanjing edition). One should understand "pressed upon by every kind of difficulty" accordingly.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains "unequal karmic action" precisely: because ordinary beings still harbor grasping at self and dharmas, even wholesome karma remains, in a subtle sense, unequal — genuinely equal, unconditioned action belongs only to the noble path itself. He unpacks "the suffering of ultimate truth" as the deepest layer of the classical three or eight kinds of suffering: however fortunate a being's worldly circumstance, birth in the higher heavens through deep meditative attainment included, so long as any of the five aggregates of grasping remains, the very fact of being caught in the three realms' own restless flow constitutes suffering in the ultimate sense — the pervasive suffering of formations, distinct from the more obvious suffering of pain or the suffering of change.

He cites the passage cross-referenced from the stage with both examination and analysis, describing beings' own tears, blood, and mother's milk consumed across countless past lives as exceeding the four great oceans combined, former kin now utterly forgotten and even become enemies — a second-ground bodhisattva's own compassion arising not from sentiment but from clearly, truly observing this condition exactly as it is, whether a given being's present circumstance looks fortunate or unfortunate by worldly standards. Having explained this compassionate regard, he notes, the treatise turns next to the seventh aspect: purified wholesome roots, and the simile distinguishing this ground's own purification from the first ground's.

7. Milestone: Purified Wholesome Roots — Gold Refined Further Still in Alum, Explaining the Name "Ground Free of Stain"

The treatise now explains the seventh aspect in two parts. First, pointing back to what was already said: bodhisattvas abiding in this abode of heightened discipline likewise behold the buddhas broadly, and their wholesome roots are purified, exactly as already explained at the abode of utmost joy — the same three pure conditions of offering to the three jewels, maturing sentient beings, and dedicating merit toward awakening continuing to operate here as well. Second, a simile marking the difference: just as, in the world, a skillful craftsman takes gold already refined and places it in alum, this gold, through repeated smelting, becoming ever more bright and pure still, so too should one understand a bodhisattva's own purified wholesome roots at this abode — the gold-in-fire simile from utmost joy now extended one stage further, gold already purified once being purified again through an additional agent, alum, precisely capturing this ground's own distinctive character: not first purification, but purification of what was already pure, made purer still. This distinguishing simile is exactly why the Ten Grounds Sutra itself names this ground "free of stain."

Chinese Source

申七、善根清淨　酉一、指同前　是諸菩薩安住如是增上戒住，廣見諸佛、善根清淨，如前應知。

酉二、喻差別　此差別者，謂如世間善巧工匠，以所鍊金置迦肆娑，置於火中數數燒鍊，轉更明淨。如是菩薩善根清淨，當知亦爾。

Ru-Yi Translation

Seventh, purified wholesome roots; first, pointing to what was already said. Bodhisattvas abiding in this abode of heightened discipline likewise broadly behold the buddhas, wholesome roots purified, exactly as already explained.

Second, a simile marking the difference. The difference is this: just as, in the world, a skillful craftsman takes gold already refined and places it in alum, and, placed in fire, smelted again and again, it becomes ever more bright and pure — so too should a bodhisattva's own purified wholesome roots be understood, just so.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through the precise progression across the two similes: at utmost joy, raw ore containing gold was placed directly in fire, external dross burning away to reveal the gold within for the very first time; here, at heightened discipline, the treatise specifies that the gold has already been refined once, and is now placed additionally in alum before further smelting — alum serving, in traditional metallurgy, to draw out even subtler impurities that plain fire alone cannot remove — the point being that this ground's own purification is a second, more exacting refinement of something already fundamentally pure, not a first purification of something still raw.

He connects this directly to the ground's own name, quoting the Ten Grounds Sutra's own gloss (already cited two milestones earlier): "the finished, genuine gold, once placed in alum-stone, has every last stain entirely removed" — precisely why this ground is called, in Sanskrit tradition, vimalā, "stainless," and in this treatise's own thirteen-abode framework, the abode of heightened discipline. He adds Cháng Bǎi's own further observation that at this ground the perfection of giving through kind speech (among the four means of attraction) becomes especially prominent, and that the perfection of moral discipline itself, among the ten perfections, becomes the ground's own especially heightened perfection — the other perfections and means of attraction continuing to be cultivated as capacity allows. Having explained purified wholesome roots and their governing simile, he notes, the treatise turns next to the eighth aspect: where such a bodhisattva typically takes rebirth.

8. Milestone: Rebirth as a Gold-Wheel King, and Majestic Power Tenfold Greater Than the First Ground's Own

The treatise now explains the eighth and ninth aspects. The eighth, rebirth: within this abode, purified aspiration having reached its own complete fulfillment, such a bodhisattva, in whatever place of rebirth, most often becomes a gold-wheel king, ruling all four great continents — a rank two full tiers above utmost joy's own iron-wheel kingship ruling a single continent — with sovereign power able to cause countless sentient beings to cease their own precept-violating, unwholesome courses of karmic action, and to encourage them to undertake wholesome ones instead. The ninth, majestic power: should such a bodhisattva likewise renounce the throne for monastic diligence, the resulting majestic power should be understood to exceed the first ground's own tenfold — the very same twelvefold catalogue of attainments already given for utmost joy (samādhis, buddhas beheld, worlds shaken, worlds visited, worlds illuminated, beings matured, eons of life sustained, eons past and future penetrated, dharma-gateways discerned, bodies manifested, and retinues displayed) now multiplied from a hundred to a full thousand of each.

Chinese Source

申八、受生　於此住中，淨心意樂成滿趣入。在所生處多作輪王，王四大洲。以自在力，令多有情止息犯戒不善業道，勸彼受行諸善業道。

申九、威力　當知威力過前十倍。

Ru-Yi Translation

Eighth, rebirth. Within this abode, purified aspiration having reached complete fulfillment, one attains entry. In whatever place of rebirth, one most often becomes a wheel-turning king, ruling four great continents. With sovereign power, causing many sentient beings to cease precept-violating, unwholesome courses of karmic action, encouraging them to undertake wholesome courses of karmic action.

Ninth, majestic power. One should understand that majestic power exceeds the previous ground's own tenfold.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi supplies the full text the treatise itself abbreviates here, cross-referencing back to the first ground's own twelvefold catalogue: in a single instant, such a renunciate bodhisattva realizes a thousand samādhis; with a pure divine eye beholds a thousand tathāgatas across every kind of buddha-land, understanding fully both their transformation-sustaining and bodhisattva-sustaining power; with spiritual power shakes a thousand worlds, the body likewise able to go there; emits great light illuminating a thousand worlds, causing all to see; transforms into a thousand kinds, maturing a thousand kinds of beings; if wishing to prolong life, remains for a thousand eons; penetrates, with wisdom and vision, matters spanning a hundred thousand eons past and future; discerns correctly a thousand dharma-gateways among the aggregates, elements, and sense-fields; manifests a thousand bodies; and displays, in each and every one of those thousand bodies, a retinue of a thousand bodhisattvas surrounding it — every single figure from the first ground's own "hundred" now multiplied to "a thousand," a tenfold increase applied uniformly across all twelve attainments.

He notes the parallel structure with the first ground once more: there too, kingship (iron-wheel, one continent) preceded renunciation and the resulting spiritual attainments; here, kingship has itself grown grander (gold-wheel, four continents) and the resulting attainments have grown grander in exact proportion, a consistent tenfold scaling the treatise will likely continue to apply at each successive ground. Having now explained all nine aspects distinguishing the abode of heightened discipline, he notes, the treatise turns next to a concluding summary naming all nine together, followed by a final cross-reference confirming this abode's identity with the Ten Grounds Sutra's own ground of stainlessness.

9. Milestone: The Summary Names All Nine Aspects, and the Treatise Confirms — Heightened Discipline Is the Ground Free of Stain

The treatise now closes the entire abode of heightened discipline with a concluding summary and a final cross-reference. In brief, this is called the bodhisattva's abode of heightened discipline: because of purified aspiration; because innate precepts are fulfilled; because every kind of precept-violating stain is departed from; because every course of karmic action and every cause and fruit are genuinely understood; because pure karma is personally practiced and others are delighted in being encouraged to undertake it as well; because vast compassion is attained, truly observing the many sufferings and hardships arising from karmic action across the realm of sentient beings, exactly as it is; because of purified wholesome roots; because of rebirth; and because of majestic power. Should one wish to hear this expounded at greater length, the treatise directs the reader to the Ten Grounds Sutra's own chapter on the ground free of stain. And, in a final clarifying correspondence, the treatise states plainly: because every stain of precept-violation is departed from entirely, it is named the ground free of stain; and it is this very same freedom from every stain of precept-violation that constitutes what this treatise calls the abode of heightened discipline — one should understand that ground free of stain and this abode of heightened discipline are exactly the same reality under two names.

Chinese Source

未二、結略義　當知是名略說菩薩增上戒住。謂意樂淨故；性戒具足故；離一切種毀犯戒垢故；一切業道、一切因果了知通達故；於諸淨業能自受行，亦樂勸他令其受行故；於有情界諸業所生眾苦艱辛，得大哀愍，如實觀照故；善根清淨故；受生故；威力故。

午二、廣指　未一、指　若廣宣說，如十地經離垢地說。

未二、配　遠離一切犯戒垢故，名離垢地。由離一切犯戒垢故，即此名為增上戒住。彼離垢地，當知即此增上戒住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, closing the brief meaning. One should understand this, in brief, as the bodhisattva's abode of heightened discipline — namely, because of purified aspiration; because innate precepts are fulfilled; because every kind of precept-violating stain is departed from; because every course of karmic action and every cause and fruit are genuinely understood and penetrated; because pure karma is personally practiced, and others are delighted in being encouraged to undertake it; because vast compassion is attained toward the many sufferings and hardships arising from karmic action across the realm of sentient beings, truly observed exactly as it is; because of purified wholesome roots; because of rebirth; because of majestic power.

Second, the broader pointer; first, pointing to it. Should one wish to hear it expounded at length, it is exactly as the Ten Grounds Sutra expounds it in its chapter on the ground free of stain.

Second, the correspondence. Because every stain of precept-violation is departed from entirely, it is named the ground free of stain. Because of this very freedom from every stain of precept-violation, this is called the abode of heightened discipline. One should understand that ground free of stain is exactly this abode of heightened discipline.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi reviews all nine aspects as a final capstone: purified aspiration (the ten minds carried forward, now fully ripened); innate precept-fulfillment; freedom from even the slightest violation (the restraint-precepts made pure); thorough understanding of cause, fruit, and karmic action (the precepts gathering wholesome dharma made pure); personally practicing and delighting in encouraging others; vast compassion for suffering beings (the precepts benefiting sentient beings made pure); purified wholesome roots (gold refined once more in alum); rebirth as a gold-wheel king; and majestic power tenfold greater than the first ground's own — the same nine-part structure, he notes, mapping cleanly onto utmost joy's own eight-part structure with one clear addition: this ground splits utmost joy's single treatment of precepts implicitly present throughout into the three explicit precept-categories (restraint, gathering wholesome dharma, benefiting sentient beings), each becoming its own dedicated aspect.

He treats the closing correspondence — ground free of stain and abode of heightened discipline being simply two names, one from the Ten Grounds Sutra's own Sanskrit-derived tradition and one from this treatise's own thirteen-abode framework, for a single underlying reality — as the treatise's own deliberate, recurring practice of reconciling its own terminology with the wider Mahāyāna tradition at the close of each ground, precisely as it already did at utmost joy's own close. With this correspondence confirmed, the entire abode of heightened discipline — the second ground, examined across all nine aspects — reaches its complete conclusion. The treatise turns next to the third of the eleven-part granular analysis's own third abode: heightened mind, the third ground, opening with its own fourfold question about the mind's own thorough purification.

10. Milestone: The Abode of Heightened Mind Opens — Ten Further Kinds of Purified-Mind Reflection, the First Five Named

The treatise now opens the third abode: heightened mind, also called the ground of shining light, marking a bodhisattva's meditative power growing especially excellent. The question is posed exactly as before: when a bodhisattva at the abode of heightened mind turns, what conduct, what state, what mark should be understood? The answer explains that bodhisattvas, previously at the abode of heightened discipline, having already attained the ten kinds of purified aspiration and thoroughly penetrated them through reflection, now further reflect on ten additional purified-mind aspirations, bringing these too to their most excellent, utterly complete grade — thereby passing beyond heightened discipline and entering heightened mind. Listing the first five of these ten reflections: first, reflecting that the prior ten purified aspirations have themselves already become pure; second, reflecting that, being pure, they are now incapable of regressing; third, reflecting that the mind no longer enters into any defiled or contaminated dharma whatsoever, abiding correctly amid what opposes it; fourth, reflecting that, within cultivating the remedy for such dharmas, consciousness abides correctly; and fifth, reflecting that, within this very remedy already cultivated, one no longer regresses from it.

Chinese Source

辰四、增上心住　巳一、問　問：增上心住菩薩轉時，當知何行、何狀、何相？

巳二、答　午一、略說　未一、別辨相　申一、淨心思惟成滿趣入　酉一、標　答：若諸菩薩，先於增上戒住，已得十種清淨意樂，作意思惟解了通達。復由餘十淨心意樂作意思惟成上品故，極圓滿故，過增上戒住，入增上心住。

酉二、徵　何等為十？

酉三、列　一者、作意思惟：我於十種淨心意樂已得清淨；二者、作意思惟：我於十種淨心意樂已清淨故，能不退失；三者、作意思惟：我於一切漏有漏法心不趣入，於違背中能正安住；四者、作意思惟：我能於彼修對治中，識正安住；五者、作意思惟：我能於彼所修對治，不復退失；

Ru-Yi Translation

Fourth abode, heightened mind; first, the question. Question: when a bodhisattva at the abode of heightened mind turns, what conduct, what state, what mark should be understood?

Second, the answer; first, the brief account; first, distinguishing each mark; first, purified-mind reflection bringing entry to fulfillment; first, naming it. Answer: if bodhisattvas, previously at the abode of heightened discipline, having already attained ten kinds of purified aspiration, reflected upon and thoroughly penetrated them — further, by ten other kinds of purified-mind aspiration and reflection becoming of the most excellent grade, utterly complete, one passes beyond the abode of heightened discipline and enters the abode of heightened mind.

Second, posing the question. What are the ten?

Third, listing them. First, reflecting: the ten kinds of purified-mind aspiration have themselves already become pure. Second, reflecting: because the ten kinds of purified-mind aspiration have already become pure, they are able not to regress. Third, reflecting: toward every contaminated and defiling dharma, the mind does not enter at all, abiding correctly amid what opposes it. Fourth, reflecting: within cultivating the remedy for these, consciousness abides correctly. Fifth, reflecting: within this remedy already cultivated, one does not regress from it.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through each of these first five reflections as a genuine, honest self-examination rather than mere recitation: the first asks whether one has truly become free of deception toward teachers, gentle toward fellow practitioners, and so on through all ten prior aspirations, checking each one individually; the second (called "unmoving mind" in the Ten Grounds Sutra) asks whether this purity, once attained, can be trusted not to backslide, confidence here resting on genuine confidence in the Mahāyāna itself; the third (called "aversion-mind") asks whether one's own mind still inclines at all toward any contaminated dharma, or instead rests correctly amid what is opposed to worldly grasping; the fourth (called "desire-freed mind") asks whether, in present cultivation of the remedy against affliction, awareness genuinely abides settled on its object, free of the three realms' sensory desire, afflictive desire, and view-attachment; and the fifth (called "non-regressing mind") asks whether this same remedial cultivation, once attained, is now secure against relapse.

He notes that this entire exercise is deliberately structured as self-examination against the prior ten aspirations named at heightened discipline — a bodhisattva does not simply assume prior attainments remain intact, but actively re-checks each one before advancing, precisely the kind of rigorous, ongoing verification that distinguishes genuine spiritual progress from complacent assumption. Having explained the first five of ten purified-mind reflections, he notes, the treatise turns next to the remaining five.

11. Milestone: The Remaining Five Reflections — Firm, Bright, Sincere, Joyful, Great — Closing the First Aspect

The treatise now completes the ten purified-mind reflections and closes the first aspect. Sixth, reflecting that, amid this very firm remedy, one is not overcome by any contaminated or defiling dharma, nor by any army of demonic forces. Seventh, reflecting that, toward every buddha-dharma, the mind now turns without any timidity or inferiority whatsoever. Eighth, reflecting that, toward every ascetic hardship, there is now no timidity at all. Ninth, reflecting that the mind is single-mindedly, deeply resolved toward and understanding of the great vehicle, never delighting in any lesser, inferior vehicle. And tenth, reflecting that, toward every undertaking that benefits sentient beings, the mind delights in this profoundly and deeply. By these ten kinds of purified-mind aspiration and reflection, one is able to enter the bodhisattva's abode of heightened mind — the commentary cross-referencing the Ten Grounds Sutra's own naming of these same ten as ten kinds of deep, mindful reflection: pure mind, unmoving mind, aversion-mind, desire-freed mind, non-regressing mind, firm mind, bright and flourishing mind, sincere and generous mind, joyful mind, and great mind.

Chinese Source

六者、作意思惟：我於如是堅固對治，不為一切漏有漏法、一切魔軍之所勝伏；七者、作意思惟：我今能於一切佛法，其心無有怯劣而轉；八者、作意思惟：我今能於一切苦行，無有怯弱；九者、作意思惟：我心一向於大乘中深生信解，終不愛樂餘下劣乘；十者、作意思惟：我於一切利有情事，深心愛樂。

酉四、結　由此十種淨心意樂作意思惟，能入菩薩增上心住。

《披》復由餘十淨心意樂等者：十地經說：菩薩善清淨心行第二地已，欲得第三菩薩地，當起十種深念心。何等為十？一、淨心，二、不動心，三、厭心，四、離欲心，五、不退心，六、堅心，七、明盛心，八、淳厚心，九、快心，十、大心。菩薩以是十種深念心，得入第三地，十地經論一一別釋。此說十種淨心意樂，如次應知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Sixth, reflecting: within this firm remedy, one is not overcome by any contaminated, defiling dharma, nor by any army of demonic forces. Seventh, reflecting: toward every buddha-dharma, the mind now turns without timidity or inferiority. Eighth, reflecting: toward every ascetic hardship, there is now no timidity at all. Ninth, reflecting: the mind is single-mindedly, deeply resolved toward and understanding of the great vehicle, never delighting in any lesser, inferior vehicle. Tenth, reflecting: toward every undertaking benefiting sentient beings, one delights in this profoundly and deeply.

Fourth, the conclusion. By these ten kinds of purified-mind aspiration and reflection, one is able to enter the bodhisattva's abode of heightened mind.

Commentary: Regarding "further, by ten other kinds of purified-mind aspiration" and the like — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: a bodhisattva, having genuinely purified the mind and practiced the second ground, wishing to attain the third bodhisattva ground, should give rise to ten kinds of deep, mindful reflection. What are the ten? First, pure mind. Second, unmoving mind. Third, aversion-mind. Fourth, desire-freed mind. Fifth, non-regressing mind. Sixth, firm mind. Seventh, bright and flourishing mind. Eighth, sincere and generous mind. Ninth, joyful mind. Tenth, great mind. A bodhisattva, by these ten kinds of deep, mindful reflection, attains entry into the third ground, each explained individually in the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra. The ten kinds of purified-mind aspiration spoken of here should be understood to correspond in this order.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi continues the correspondence: firm mind (sixth) checks whether one's remedial cultivation can withstand every affliction and every one of the classical four demonic forces — the demon of the five aggregates, the demon of affliction, the heavenly demon, and the demon of death; bright and flourishing mind (seventh) checks whether, with meditative sovereignty now attained, one genuinely believes oneself capable of the buddhas' own vast qualities — the ten powers, four fearlessnesses, eighteen exclusive qualities — rather than doubting one's own capacity the way a meditator who has merely touched access concentration might wrongly believe they have already severed affliction; sincere and generous mind (eighth) checks whether, with meditative sovereignty, one can willingly return to the desire realm to endure the hardship of teaching unruly beings there, rather than being drawn upward into form or formless realm rebirth the way an ordinary meditator inevitably is; joyful mind (ninth) checks continued, undiminished delight in the Mahāyāna specifically over any path that stops at merely personal liberation; and great mind (tenth) checks the vast wish to benefit beings without limit, refusing to sever all further rebirth in the three realms precisely so as to keep returning for their sake.

He treats this practice of methodically re-examining and re-affirming, one at a time, an ever-lengthening list of prior aspirations as the treatise's own model for how a bodhisattva's practice compounds across each successive ground — nothing is simply left behind as "already done"; everything already attained is continually reflected upon, verified, and built upon further. With the first aspect of nine now complete, he notes, the treatise turns next to the second aspect: penetrating thoroughly the nature of conditioned formations, the buddhas' own wisdom, and the realm of sentient beings.

12. Milestone: Deep Aversion Toward All Conditioned Formations, and Deep Longing for the Buddhas' Own Wisdom

The treatise now opens the second aspect in three parts. First, regarding conditioned formations: bodhisattvas abiding in the abode of heightened mind are able, through every kind of aspect displaying formations' own faults and calamities, to break down every formation entirely, giving rise to a deeply felt aversion toward them — the commentary cross-referencing the Ten Grounds Sutra's own instruction to correctly observe that every conditioned formation is impermanent, suffering, and impure, arising and perishing moment by moment, coming from no prior boundary and going to no later one, not even abiding in the present. Second, regarding the buddhas' own wisdom: through every kind of aspect displaying wisdom's own excellent benefit, such bodhisattvas behold its great excellence, and, with a single, purely undivided savor of longing, deeply give rise to love and admiration for it — the commentary explaining, via the Ten Grounds Sutra, that such a bodhisattva sees the tathāgatas' own wisdom as inconceivable, peerless, immeasurable, rare, unmixed, untroubled, and able to reach the great, fearless city of peace, never to turn back again, and able to rescue immeasurable, suffering sentient beings.

Chinese Source

申二、通達諸行佛智及有情界　酉一、正通達　戌一、於諸行　菩薩安住增上心住，能以種種過患行相壞一切行，於彼諸行深心厭離。

《披》種種過患行相者：十地經說：正觀有為法，一切行無常、苦、不淨，無常敗壞不久住，念念生滅，不從前際來，不去至後際，現在不住。如是正觀一切諸行，此應準釋。

戌二、於佛智　於佛妙智，能以種種勝利行相，見大勝利。又於其中，能以淳淨一味欲樂，深生愛慕。

《披》種種勝利行相者：十地經說：是菩薩見如來智慧不可思議，無等、無量、難得、無雜、無惱、無憂，能至無畏安隱大城，不復轉還，見能救無量苦惱眾生。此應準釋。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second aspect: thoroughly penetrating formations, the buddhas' own wisdom, and the realm of sentient beings; first, genuine penetration; first, regarding formations. Bodhisattvas abiding in the abode of heightened mind, through every kind of aspect displaying faults and calamities, are able to break down every formation entirely, giving rise to deeply felt aversion toward them.

Commentary: Regarding "every kind of aspect displaying faults and calamities" — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: correctly observe that conditioned dharmas, every formation, are impermanent, suffering, impure, impermanent and decaying, not abiding long, arising and perishing moment by moment, coming from no prior boundary, going to no later boundary, not abiding even in the present. One should correctly observe every formation in just this way, exactly as explained there.

Second, regarding the buddhas' own wisdom. Toward the buddhas' own wondrous wisdom, through every kind of aspect displaying excellent benefit, one beholds its great excellence. Furthermore, within this, with a single, purely undivided savor of longing, one deeply gives rise to love and admiration.

Commentary: Regarding "every kind of aspect displaying excellent benefit" — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: this bodhisattva sees the Tathāgata's own wisdom as inconceivable, peerless, immeasurable, rare, unmixed, untroubled, unafflicted with grief, able to reach the great, fearless city of peace, never to turn back again, and sees it able to rescue immeasurable, suffering sentient beings. This should be explained exactly as there.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through the Ten Grounds Sutra's own fivefold analysis of the buddhas' wisdom being of "great, gathered merit": inconceivability (its spiritual power exceeding all ordinary imagination); peerlessness (utterly without rival, having reached the ultimate); immeasurability (able to benefit immeasurable sentient beings); rarity (a merit so difficult to accomplish that it invites no reproach); and being unmixed (invariably joined with the twofold emptiness of self and dharmas, never contaminated by the mixed, unstable spiritual powers non-Buddhist paths sometimes display). He continues with the wisdom's own purity: untroubled (free of the afflictive suffering born of grasping at self and dharmas alike) and unafflicted with grief (having already severed all suffering, hence free of any accompanying sorrow) — culminating in the wisdom's own capacity to reach nirvana itself, "the great, fearless city of peace," a city unarising and unceasing, undefiled and unpurified, unincreasing and undiminished, from which one never again turns back, and from within which immeasurable suffering beings can genuinely be rescued.

He notes the deliberate pairing this aspect sets up: deep aversion toward every conditioned formation (drawing the mind away from saṃsāra) paired directly with deep longing for the buddhas' own wisdom (drawing the mind toward nirvana) — a bodhisattva's own version of the classical twin movements of renunciation and aspiration, here examined with far greater precision and depth than at either of the first two grounds. Having explained aversion toward formations and longing for buddha-wisdom, he notes, the treatise turns next to the third part of genuine penetration: how this same bodhisattva regards the realm of sentient beings.

13. Milestone: Ten Kinds of Superior Mind Toward Suffering Sentient Beings — Pitiable, Impoverished, Imprisoned, Overgrown, Lost

The treatise now explains the third part of genuine penetration: regarding sentient beings. Toward the realm of sentient beings, through every kind of aspect displaying suffering and hardship, such a bodhisattva observes that they are beset by suffering, and toward every sentient being arouses a heart of grief and compassion, giving rise to a mind reliant on genuine benefit. The commentary, cross-referencing the Ten Grounds Sutra at length, names ten specific grounds giving rise to this superior mind: sentient beings are pitiable, solitary, without rescue — this the general statement; they are perpetually impoverished; the fire of the three poisons blazes on unceasing; they are shut within the firm prison of the three realms of existence; they are perpetually covered by the dense thicket of affliction and evil; they lack the power of correct observation; departed from wholesome dharma, the mind knows no joy; they have lost the buddhas' own wondrous teaching; they perpetually follow the world's own current; and they have lost the very means to nirvana.

Chinese Source

戌三、於有情　於有情界，能以種種苦惱行相，觀為有苦；於諸有情興悲戀心，生依義心。

《披》種種苦惱行相等者：十地經說：見一切有為行無量苦惱，復於一切眾生轉生殊勝十心。所謂眾生可愍、孤獨、無救，生殊勝心；恆常貧窮，生殊勝心；三毒之火熾然不息，生殊勝心；閉在三有牢固之獄，生殊勝心；常為煩惱諸惡稠林所覆，生殊勝心；無正觀力，生殊勝心；遠離善法心無喜樂，生殊勝心；失諸佛妙法，生殊勝心；而常隨順世間水流，生殊勝心；失涅槃方便，生殊勝心；是名生殊勝十心。此應準釋。

Ru-Yi Translation

Third, regarding sentient beings. Toward the realm of sentient beings, through every kind of aspect displaying suffering and hardship, one observes that they have suffering; toward sentient beings, one arouses a heart of grief and compassion, giving rise to a mind reliant on genuine benefit.

Commentary: Regarding "every kind of aspect displaying suffering and hardship" — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: seeing that every conditioned formation holds immeasurable suffering and hardship, one further gives rise, toward all sentient beings, to ten kinds of superior mind. Namely: sentient beings are pitiable, solitary, without rescue — giving rise to superior mind; they are perpetually impoverished — giving rise to superior mind; the fire of the three poisons blazes unceasing — giving rise to superior mind; they are shut within the firm prison of the three realms of existence — giving rise to superior mind; they are perpetually covered by the dense thicket of affliction and evil — giving rise to superior mind; they lack the power of correct observation — giving rise to superior mind; departed from wholesome dharma, the mind knows no joy — giving rise to superior mind; they have lost the buddhas' own wondrous teaching — giving rise to superior mind; they perpetually follow the world's own current — giving rise to superior mind; they have lost the very means to nirvana — giving rise to superior mind. These are called the ten kinds of superior mind. This should be explained exactly as there.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi unpacks all ten in turn: the first, "pitiable, solitary, without rescue," is the general statement — every being, dying and being reborn utterly alone, following karma's own current with no true companion, is object of compassion; the remaining nine (items two through ten) elaborate this in relation to three kinds of seekers the commentary tradition distinguishes — desire-seekers, existence-seekers, and holy-life-seekers. Impoverishment and the three poisons' blazing fire (items two and three) describe desire-seekers, beings whose craving is never satisfied no matter how much they acquire, lacking the true wealth of the seven noble treasures — faith, discipline, conscientiousness, shame, learning, generosity, wisdom — however materially wealthy they may worldly appear. Imprisonment in the three realms and being overgrown by affliction's dense thicket, together with lacking correct observation (items four through six), describe existence-seekers, beings pursuing rebirth in the form or formless realms, trapped in a prison stronger than any physical jail, and, lacking wisdom regarding the four noble truths, liable to rebirth in one of the eight unfortunate conditions where dharma cannot be learned at all.

The final four (items seven through ten) describe holy-life-seekers — those who do seek liberation but go astray: departing from wholesome dharma without genuine joy describes practice that fails to connect with real liberation; losing the buddhas' own wondrous teaching describes the danger of spiritual overconfidence, mistaking mere concentration for the noble path itself; following the world's current describes being swept along by the four "floods" — desire, existence, views, and ignorance — even while seeking release; and losing the means to nirvana describes well-meaning seekers, such as certain naked ascetics, who lack the correct thirty-seven factors of awakening or the six perfections and so cannot actually reach the goal they seek. Having named all ten grounds for a bodhisattva's own compassionate concern, he notes, the treatise turns next to explaining the resulting action such penetrating understanding produces.

14. Milestone: Three Resulting Actions — No Laxity, Blazing Diligence, Vast Compassion — Closing the Second Aspect

The treatise now closes the second aspect by explaining the resulting action that follows from this threefold penetrating understanding. Toward every formation, there is no laxity whatsoever; for the sake of great awakening, diligence blazes forth; and toward every sentient being, one is able to arouse a vast heart of compassionate grief. The commentary explains precisely how each of the three prior insights produces its corresponding action: because of deep aversion toward formations, there is no laxity toward them; because of beholding the buddhas' wisdom's own excellence and deeply longing for it, diligence blazes forth for its sake; and because of observing that sentient beings suffer, one is thereby able to arouse a vast heart of compassionate grief toward them.

Chinese Source

酉二、明所作　於一切行無有放逸，為大菩提熾然精進，於諸有情能起廣大悲愍意樂。

《披》於一切行無有放逸等者：此顯正通達已，能正所作。由於諸行深厭離欲，是故於彼無有放逸。由於佛智見勝利故，深愛慕故，是故為彼熾然精進。由於有情觀有苦故，是故於彼能起廣大悲愍意樂。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, clarifying the resulting action. Toward every formation, there is no laxity. For the sake of great awakening, diligence blazes forth. Toward every sentient being, one is able to arouse a vast heart of compassionate grief.

Commentary: Regarding "toward every formation, there is no laxity" and the like — this displays that, genuine penetration now accomplished, one is able to correctly act. Because of deep aversion and freedom from desire toward formations, there is no laxity toward them. Because of beholding the buddha-wisdom's own excellence and deeply longing for it, diligence blazes forth for its sake. Because of observing that sentient beings suffer, one is thereby able to arouse a vast heart of compassionate grief toward them.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this closing passage as the treatise's own insistence that understanding alone is never the endpoint — genuine penetration of formations, buddha-wisdom, and sentient beings' own condition must issue in correspondingly appropriate action, or else remain merely theoretical. He traces the clean one-to-one correspondence: aversion toward saṃsāra's own emptiness naturally yields freedom from laxity (no further temptation to indulge in what one has clearly seen through); longing for the buddhas' wisdom naturally yields blazing diligence (genuine desire for a goal always produces energy toward it); and clear-eyed observation of beings' own suffering naturally yields vast compassion (understanding suffering precisely, rather than sentimentally, is what makes compassion genuinely vast rather than merely sympathetic).

With the second aspect — thoroughly penetrating formations, buddha-wisdom, and sentient beings, together with the resulting threefold action — now complete, he notes, the treatise turns next to the third aspect: seeking out the ultimate means by which sentient beings can actually be liberated from suffering.

15. Milestone: Seeking the Ultimate Means of Liberation — Non-Discriminating Wisdom, Resting on Supreme Absorption, Resting on Hearing the True Dharma

The treatise now explains the third aspect: having penetrated formations, buddha-wisdom, and sentient beings' own suffering, such a bodhisattva must further seek out the actual means by which suffering beings can be liberated. Observing that the ultimate means for sentient beings' liberation from every suffering is nothing other than an unobstructed wisdom regarding every entanglement of affliction; observing that what allows this liberation to be fully realized is nothing other than a non-discriminating wisdom giving rise to the remedy for every kind of defiling discrimination currently manifesting within the dharma-realm; observing that what accomplishes this wisdom's own illumination is nothing other than an undistorted, excellent state of absorption; and observing that every meditative state, concentration, and attainment thus brought forth is, without exception, preceded first by hearing what is proclaimed in the bodhisattva canon, and arises altogether through hearing the true dharma as its own condition. The chain of reasoning runs, step by step, from liberation itself, back through the wisdom that accomplishes it, back through the absorption that illumines that wisdom, back to hearing the true dharma as the ultimate starting point of the entire chain.

Chinese Source

申三、推求有情脫苦方便　觀諸有情解脫眾苦究竟方便，唯是一切煩惱諸纏無障礙智；觀彼解脫能圓證者，唯於法界一切分別現行雜染生起對治無分別慧；觀能成辦彼智光明，唯是無倒勝三摩地；觀所引發一切靜慮、等持、等至，皆菩薩藏聽聞為先，皆聞正法以為緣起。

Ru-Yi Translation

Third aspect: seeking out the means for sentient beings' liberation from suffering. Observing that the ultimate means for sentient beings' liberation from every suffering is nothing other than an unobstructed wisdom regarding every entanglement of affliction; observing that what allows this liberation to be fully realized is nothing other than a non-discriminating wisdom that gives rise to the remedy for every defiling discrimination currently manifesting within the dharma-realm; observing that what accomplishes this wisdom's own illumination is nothing other than an undistorted, excellent state of absorption; observing that every meditative state, concentration, and attainment thus brought forth is, without exception, preceded by hearing what is proclaimed in the bodhisattva canon, arising altogether through hearing the true dharma as its condition.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi traces this entire causal chain in reverse, from effect back to ultimate cause: liberation from affliction's every entanglement requires an unobstructed liberating wisdom; that wisdom's own full realization requires a further, non-discriminating wisdom capable of remedying every discrimination that arises regarding the dharma-realm — discrimination the Bodhisattva Stage's own chapter 36 groups into eight kinds producing three underlying afflictive patterns: self-nature, differentiation, and totalizing discrimination (together generating the discriminating consciousness, its basis, and its object); self- and mine-discrimination (self-view and self-conceit); and desire-, aversion-, and neither-discrimination (greed, hatred, and delusion) — genuine non-discriminating wisdom sees through all such discrimination via the four kinds of investigation and four kinds of genuine knowledge already outlined in earlier fascicles.

He continues down the chain: this non-discriminating wisdom's own clarity depends on genuine, undistorted, excellent absorption — meditative stillness alone insufficient without the discernment built upon it, and discernment alone insufficient without the stillness that steadies it; and every such absorption, whether called meditative concentration, even-holding, or even-attainment (each term emphasizing a different facet of the same deepening stability), is itself said to arise nowhere except through first hearing the true dharma as proclaimed specifically in the bodhisattva canon. He notes the Ten Grounds Sutra's own further list of ten related activities this hearing gives rise to — hearing, rejoicing in, delighting in, relying upon, according with, remembering, contemplating, ultimately realizing, taking refuge in, and practicing in accordance with the dharma — the treatise itself here compressing this list rather than spelling it out in full. With the entire chain traced back to hearing the true dharma, he notes, the treatise turns next to the fourth aspect: the resulting reverence for the true dharma and tireless seeking after it.

16. Milestone: Reverence for the True Dharma — Willing to Leap Into a Blazing Pit for a Single Verse

The treatise now explains the fourth aspect in three parts, dramatizing just how far this reverence for the true dharma extends. First, arousing great diligence: having observed this chain of reasoning, such a bodhisattva arouses great diligence, seeking out extensive learning — for the sake of hearing the true dharma, not begrudging even life itself, with no wealth, no object of attachment inner or outer that cannot be relinquished, no teacher one would not vow to serve, no instruction one would not vow to uphold, and no bodily suffering one would not vow to endure. Second, joyful exuberance: upon hearing even a single four-line verse of buddha-dharma, such a bodhisattva's joyful exuberance surpasses even receiving a great trichiliocosm entirely filled with treasure, since hearing even one verse spoken by a buddha, capable of drawing forth correct, complete awakening and purifying bodhisattva conduct, surpasses attaining the most exalted worldly rank of Śakra, Brahmā, or a universal monarch. Third, enduring great suffering: should someone declare that a single verse of buddha-dharma will be taught only if the bodhisattva first leaps into a great pit of fire and endures great suffering, the bodhisattva, hearing this, joyfully exuberant, replies: I am able! Even were that fire-pit equal in size to an entire great trichiliocosm ablaze, I would leap in from the Brahma heaven itself rather than forgo it — how much more so a small pit of fire; for the sake of seeking the dharma, I should be willing to dwell long in the great hell itself, how much more so any lesser suffering.

Chinese Source

申四、恭敬正法訪求無倦　酉一、發大精進　觀見是已，發大精進訪求多聞。為聞正法，不惜身命，無有資財內外愛物而不能捨，無有師長不誓承事，無有尊教不誓奉行，無有身苦而不誓受。

酉二、歡喜踊躍　若聞佛法一四句頌，歡喜踊躍，勝得三千大千世界充滿其中大珍寶聚。聞一句法是佛所說，能引正等覺，能淨菩薩行，歡喜踊躍，勝得一切釋、梵、護世轉輪王等極尊貴位。

酉三、堪忍大苦　戌一、設告言　設有告言：善男子聽！我有一句佛所說法，能引正等覺，能淨菩薩行，汝欲聞不？汝今若能投大火坑受大苦者，當為汝說。

戌二、如應答　菩薩聞已，歡喜踊躍，答言：我能！我若得聞如前所說一句法義，正使火坑量等三千大千世界滿中熾火，我從梵天尚投身入，況小火坑。為求佛法，尚應久處大那落迦受大苦惱，況餘小苦而不應受。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fourth aspect: reverence for the true dharma, seeking it tirelessly; first, arousing great diligence. Having observed this, one arouses great diligence, seeking out extensive learning. For the sake of hearing the true dharma, not begrudging life itself; no wealth, no object of attachment inner or outer, that cannot be relinquished; no teacher one would not vow to serve; no instruction one would not vow to uphold; no bodily suffering one would not vow to endure.

Second, joyful exuberance. Upon hearing even a single four-line verse of buddha-dharma, one's joyful exuberance surpasses receiving a great trichiliocosm entirely filled with a great mass of treasure. Hearing a single verse spoken by a buddha, able to draw forth correct, complete awakening, able to purify bodhisattva conduct, one's joyful exuberance surpasses attaining every exalted rank of Śakra, Brahmā, a world-protector, or a universal monarch.

Third, enduring great suffering; first, the hypothetical declaration. Suppose someone declares: good son, listen! I have a single verse of buddha-dharma, able to draw forth correct, complete awakening, able to purify bodhisattva conduct — do you wish to hear it? If you are now able to leap into a great pit of fire and endure great suffering, I shall then teach it to you.

Second, the fitting reply. The bodhisattva, having heard this, joyfully exuberant, replies: I am able! If I may hear the meaning of the verse spoken of before, even were the fire-pit's size equal to an entire great trichiliocosm filled with blazing fire, I would leap in bodily from the Brahma heaven itself — how much more so a small pit of fire. For the sake of seeking buddha-dharma, I should be willing to dwell long in the great hell itself, enduring great suffering — how much more so should I not endure any lesser suffering.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi cites the Ten Grounds Sutra's own commentary describing this same reverence in ten further behaviors: day and night constantly seeking dharma, rejoicing in it, delighting in it, relying on it, according with it, remembering it, contemplating it, realizing it ultimately, taking refuge in it, and practicing in accordance with it — the treatise itself compressing all ten into the briefer account given here. He recalls Śākyamuni's own past-life account of trading an eye, or a leap from a cliff, for a single verse of dharma, treating this not as mere legend but as a genuine mark of what a third-ground bodhisattva's own reverence for the dharma actually feels like from within.

He relates, at some length, the story from the Mount Wutai gazetteer of a pilgrim who, after an arduous wartime journey past countless warlord checkpoints in search of the "Mañjuśrī who can speak," eventually found an ordinary-seeming monk serving as fire-tender during an ordination retreat at Mount Baohua near Nanjing — the very bodhisattva he sought — and served him faithfully throughout the retreat, only to fail, three times in succession, to follow that same bodhisattva's invitation to leap into the cooking-stove's own flame when the retreat ended and Mañjuśrī prepared to depart; Mañjuśrī vanished into the fire without him. Cháng Bǎi treats this story as illustrating exactly the gap between a third-ground bodhisattva's own genuine willingness to leap into fire for the dharma's sake, and an ordinary practitioner's own hesitation even after having recognized, served, and directly been invited by a genuine bodhisattva — a caution, he notes, against the complacency ("the smoothness sickness") that can settle over long practice, robbing it of the freshness and urgency this passage is meant to rekindle. Having explained arousing diligence, joyful exuberance, and willingness to endure great suffering for the dharma's sake, he notes, the treatise turns next to the fifth of nine aspects distinguishing the abode of heightened mind.

17. Milestone: True Practice Means Actually Practicing — Not Just Hearing Words and Sounds — Cultivating Mundane Absorption to Tame Beings' Pride

The treatise now explains the fifth aspect in two parts. First, genuine reflection: such a bodhisattva, having aroused this diligence and sought out the true dharma, is further able to reflect on it genuinely and rationally, understanding that correct practice means actually practicing dharma in accordance with dharma — this alone is what "according with buddha-dharma" truly means, not merely hearing its words and sounds and supposing oneself thereby purified. Second, cultivating what was heard: knowing this, such a bodhisattva, relying on what has been heard, correctly attends to the characteristics of phenomena, departs from sensory desires and unwholesome dharmas, and, in stages too lengthy to detail in full, is able to attain the four mundane meditative absorptions, the four formless attainments, the four immeasurables, and the five spiritual powers, dwelling fully equipped in all of them. The commentary explains, via the Ten Grounds Sutra, why a bodhisattva bothers to cultivate merely mundane absorption at all, despite already possessing wisdom transcending it: to break the pride of beings who cling to meditative bliss, entering these same absorptions in order to teach them, from a position of demonstrated superiority, that dwelling and clinging even in refined meditative states is not itself the final goal.

Chinese Source

申五、能正修行及得安住　酉一、如實思惟　菩薩發起如是精進，求正法已；復能如實如理思惟，要正修行法隨法行，方得名為隨順佛法，非但聽聞文字、音聲而得清淨。

酉二、依聞修習　如是知已，即依所聞正緣法相，遠離諸欲惡不善法，廣說乃至能得世俗四種靜慮、四無色定，及四無量、五種神通具足安住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fifth aspect: correctly practicing and attaining abiding stability; first, genuine reflection. A bodhisattva, having aroused such diligence and sought the true dharma, is further able to reflect genuinely and rationally: correct practice means practicing dharma in accordance with dharma; only this is called according with buddha-dharma — not merely hearing its words and sounds and thereby attaining purity.

Second, cultivating in reliance on what was heard. Knowing this, relying on what has been heard, correctly attending to the characteristics of phenomena, departing from sensory desires and unwholesome dharmas — in stages explained at length — one is able to attain the four mundane meditative absorptions, the four formless attainments, the four immeasurables, and the five spiritual powers, dwelling fully equipped in all of them.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this fifth aspect's opening line as one of the treatise's most quietly pointed corrections: hearing dharma, however diligently and reverently (as just dramatized in the previous aspect), is not itself the practice — only genuinely practicing dharma in accordance with dharma constitutes real accordance with the buddha's teaching. He explains why a bodhisattva who has already realized non-discriminating wisdom would bother cultivating merely "mundane" absorption at all, quoting the Ten Grounds Sutra's own fourfold rationale: entering these same absorptions in order to break the pride of beings who cling proudly to meditative bliss (since ordinary meditators who attain deep concentration easily grow arrogant, resistant to any teaching from someone who appears less accomplished); entering formless attainments to break the pride of beings attached to formless liberation; cultivating the four immeasurables — loving-kindness, compassion, sympathetic joy, and equanimity — precisely to address beings' own need for happiness, relief from suffering, rejoicing in others' good fortune, and freedom from the three poisons respectively; and cultivating spiritual powers to reach beings who have taken wrong refuge, establishing genuine faith through demonstrated capability non-Buddhist paths cannot match.

He notes this passage's own honesty about spiritual pride as a real obstacle even among sincere practitioners — attainment in meditation can become, paradoxically, an obstacle to being taught further, which is precisely why a bodhisattva must be capable of meeting practitioners at their own level of attainment and beyond, rather than relying solely on doctrinal argument. Having explained the purpose behind cultivating mundane absorption, he notes, the treatise turns next to detailing the five spiritual powers themselves, each arising from one of the three purified karmic doors.

18. Milestone: The Five Spiritual Powers in Full — Sovereignty Over the Body, Ear, Mind, Past Lives, and the Divine Eye

The treatise now details the five spiritual powers this abode's meditative attainment brings, each arising from one of the three purified karmic doors. Spiritual feet, arising from purified bodily karma, bring threefold sovereignty: sovereignty over the world (manifesting immeasurable spiritual power, able to shake the great earth at will); sovereignty over the body (one body becoming many, many becoming one, appearing and vanishing at will); and sovereignty over action, itself eightfold — passing unobstructed through stone walls and cliffs as though walking through open space; rising cross-legged into the sky like a bird in flight; entering and emerging from the earth as easily as water; walking on water as though on solid ground; the body itself blazing like a great mass of fire; the body itself producing water like a great cloud, able to relieve drought; the body's own hand able to touch the sun and moon, mighty as they are; and sovereign power over the very body's own realm of rebirth, up to and including the Brahmā heaven. The divine ear, arising from purified verbal karma, hears without obstruction across every distance, from the joyful sounds of heaven to the suffering cries of the lowest hell. Mind-reading, knowing precisely another's present affliction or its absence, the scope and quality of their mind, and their meditative attainment or lack of it. And, arising from purified mental karma, recollection of past lives, knowing one's own and others' countless past births in full particular detail, together with the divine eye, seeing exactly what karma leads a dying being to whatever rebirth follows.

Chinese Source

（節錄）一、神足通：是身業清淨的結果……世界自在……身自在……作業自在，有八種……二、天耳通，是語業清淨的結果……三、他心通……四、宿命通……五、天眼通，所得的天眼是清淨的，能仔細的觀察，知道這眾生死了是造什麼業，隨業到哪裡去受果報，都明明白白的。

Ru-Yi Translation

First, spiritual feet, the result of purified bodily karma — sovereignty over the world, sovereignty over the body, and eightfold sovereignty over action, including passing unobstructed through solid rock, rising into the sky cross-legged, entering and emerging from the earth as through water, walking on water, the body blazing like fire, the body producing water like a great cloud, the hand touching sun and moon, and bodily sovereignty extending up to the Brahmā heaven. Second, the divine ear, the result of purified verbal karma. Third, mind-reading. Fourth, recollection of past lives. Fifth, the divine eye, purely obtained, able to observe in detail exactly what karma a dying sentient being has created, and exactly where that karma leads them to be reborn — all of this understood with complete clarity.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi
