Master Cháng Bǎi walks through all eight sub-abilities of bodily sovereignty in vivid detail, each drawn directly from the Ten Grounds Sutra's own phrasing: unobstructed passage through stone walls and mountain cliffs, as easily as walking through open space; ascending and sitting cross-legged in mid-air like a bird in flight; entering and emerging from solid earth as though it were water; walking on water's surface as though on solid ground; the body itself emitting smoke and flame like a great blazing mass; the body itself producing water like a great rain-cloud, able to relieve a drought-stricken land; the hand itself able to touch the sun and moon, mighty and powerful as they are; and bodily sovereignty extending as far as the Brahmā heaven itself, able to shift the very physical basis of rebirth at will.

He explains the three-door structure organizing all five powers: spiritual feet result from purified bodily karma; the divine ear from purified verbal karma; and mind-reading, recollection of past lives, and the divine eye together from purified mental karma — recollection of past lives working through the mental factors of mindfulness and wisdom together, recalling not just the fact of past births but their full particulars: names, parents, social station, appearance, even what kind of food one ate and how long one lived. He notes the treatise's own citation from a further source distinguishing "fully equipped" (each of the four absorptions, four formless states, four immeasurables, and five powers brought to full completion) from "abiding" (able to enter, dwell in, and emerge from each at will) — together constituting genuine mastery rather than occasional, unstable access. Having detailed all five spiritual powers, closing the fifth aspect, he notes, the treatise turns next to the sixth aspect: taking rebirth according to one's own vow.

19. Milestone: Rebirth According to Vow — Relinquishing the Higher Realms, Severing the Fetters of Desire, Existence, and View

The treatise now explains the sixth aspect in two parts. First, observing genuine benefit: having long abided in these attainments, such a bodhisattva then relinquishes the very meditative absorptions, concentrations, and attainments just described, and, through the sovereign power of vow, returns instead to the desire realm — observing, place by place, wherever genuine benefit for sentient beings can be accomplished, or wherever the factors of awakening can be brought to fulfillment, and taking rebirth precisely there, not merely exercising sovereign power for its own sake but genuinely going to such places in order to benefit beings. Second, clarifying the severed fetters: such a bodhisattva, having thus departed from craving for sensory desire, is said to have severed the fetter of desire; having relinquished absorption, concentration, and attainment themselves, is said to have severed the fetter of existence; and, having already cultivated resolute understanding regarding suchness back at the stage of resolute practice, has already severed the fetter of view, such that wrongful craving, hatred, and delusion never again manifest at all.

Chinese Source

申六、隨願受生　酉一、觀為義利　既多住已，復還棄捨諸靜慮等、等持、等至，願自在力還來欲界。觀彼彼處，若為有情能作義利，若能圓滿菩提分法，即便往生。非但自在而生彼處。

酉二、明斷諸縛　如是菩薩離欲貪故，名斷欲縛；棄捨靜慮、等持、等至故，名斷有縛。菩薩先從勝解行地，於法真如修勝解故，已斷見縛，邪貪恚癡畢竟不轉。

《披》如是菩薩離欲貪故至畢竟不轉者：此釋前說願自在力因緣應知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Sixth aspect: rebirth according to vow; first, observing genuine benefit. Having long abided in this way, one further relinquishes these meditative absorptions, concentrations, and attainments, and, through the sovereign power of vow, returns to the desire realm. Observing, in this place or that, wherever genuine benefit for sentient beings can be accomplished, or wherever the factors of awakening can be brought to fulfillment, one takes rebirth precisely there — not merely exercising sovereign power while being reborn in such places.

Second, clarifying the severed fetters. Such a bodhisattva, having departed from craving for sensory desire, is said to have severed the fetter of desire. Having relinquished absorption, concentration, and attainment, is said to have severed the fetter of existence. Such a bodhisattva, previously at the stage of resolute practice, having cultivated resolute understanding regarding the suchness of dharmas, has already severed the fetter of view — wrongful craving, hatred, and delusion never again manifesting at all.

Commentary: Regarding "such a bodhisattva, having departed from craving for sensory desire" through "never again manifesting at all" — this explains the earlier cause and condition of the sovereign power of vow, one should understand.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains why this sixth aspect matters so much: an ordinary meditator who attains the four immeasurables and mundane absorptions is naturally drawn upward into form or formless-realm rebirth as its karmic result, unable to choose otherwise; a third-ground bodhisattva, by contrast, deliberately relinquishes this pull and, through vow-power alone, returns specifically to the desire realm — the very realm where suffering beings most need help — precisely because vow, not karmic momentum, now governs where rebirth occurs. He cites the Ten Grounds Sutra's own parallel phrasing: no longer accumulating wrongful craving, wrongful hatred, or wrongful delusion, these three roots of affliction no longer able to gather and drive continued wandering in birth-and-death.

He traces the three fetters back through the entire path so far: the fetter of view was already severed back at the first ground, when discriminated grasping at self and dharmas was cut through upon first realizing suchness directly (the text here noting this achievement traces back even further, to resolute understanding first cultivated at the stage of resolute practice, prior to the first ground itself); the fetter of desire and the fetter of existence are severed specifically here, at the third ground, through the deliberate relinquishing of both sensory craving and meditative attachment described in this same aspect. Having explained rebirth according to vow and the severing of these three fetters, he notes, the treatise turns next to the seventh aspect: purified wholesome roots, continuing the gold-refining simile now escalated once more.

20. Milestone: Purified Wholesome Roots — Gold Entirely Free of Impurity, Weighed and Found Exactly Equal

The treatise now explains the seventh aspect in two parts. First, pointing back to what was already said: such a bodhisattva likewise beholds the buddhas broadly, and wholesome roots are purified, exactly as already explained at the first and second grounds. Second, a simile marking the difference: just as, in the world, a skillful craftsman's own already-smelted, genuine gold, held in hand, has every last impurity entirely exhausted, weighed and found to abide exactly equal — so too should a bodhisattva's own purified wholesome roots be understood, just so. The simile has now progressed a third step: raw ore purified by fire at the first ground, that same gold purified further still in alum at the second, and now, at the third, gold already entirely free of impurity, its weight before and after smelting found to be exactly the same — nothing further to lose, nothing further gained, a settled, stable purity rather than a purity still in the process of being achieved.

Chinese Source

申七、善根清淨　酉一、指同前　廣見諸佛、善根清淨，如前應知。

酉二、喻差別　此差別者，謂如世間善巧工匠，先所燒鍊手中真金垢穢斯盡，稱量等住。如是菩薩善根清淨，當知亦爾。

Ru-Yi Translation

Seventh aspect: purified wholesome roots; first, pointing to what was already said. Broadly beholding the buddhas, wholesome roots purified, exactly as already explained.

Second, a simile marking the difference. The difference is this: just as, in the world, a skillful craftsman's own already-smelted, genuine gold, held in hand, has every impurity entirely exhausted, weighed and found to abide exactly equal — so too should a bodhisattva's own purified wholesome roots be understood, just so.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi traces the full three-stage progression of this recurring simile: at the first ground, raw gold-bearing ore was placed directly in fire, external dross burning away for the very first time; at the second, gold already refined once was placed additionally in alum for a further, more exacting purification; and now, at the third, the gold is entirely free of impurity, weighed before and after smelting and found to weigh exactly the same — "ore exhausted, gold pure" is how the Discourse Record glosses this same phrase, "weighed and found to abide exactly equal" naming the settled stability of a purity that has nothing further to lose or gain.

He adds the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra's own further gloss, drawn from this ground's own characteristic wisdom: observing that every dharma neither truly arises nor truly ceases, all arising through causes and conditions alone, one sees no increase in pure dharmas nor decrease in afflictive, deluded ones — the fetters severed at this ground being, ultimately, themselves empty and ungraspable, so that even their severing constitutes no substantial gain, exactly as a purified mind, illusory in nature like all dharmas, constitutes no substantial increase; defiled and pure alike are unarisen, unceasing, unincreased, undiminished, untainted, unpurified — a third-ground bodhisattva's own wisdom having reached precisely this "weighed and found equal" understanding. Having explained purified wholesome roots and their now-completed simile, he notes, the treatise turns next to the eighth and ninth aspects: rebirth and majestic power.

21. Milestone: Rebirth as Śakra, Lord of the Thirty-Three Heavens, and Majestic Power a Hundredfold Greater Still

The treatise now explains the eighth and ninth aspects. The eighth, rebirth: such a bodhisattva, taking rebirth as a householder, most often becomes Śakra, lord of the thirty-three heavens, skillfully transforming sentient beings and causing them to depart from craving and desire — a further step upward from the wheel-turning kingships of the first two grounds, now a rebirth among the gods themselves, specifically as their ruling lord. The ninth, majestic power: whatever majestic power was already described at the previous abode as reaching a thousand of each kind, one should understand that here, at this abode, it reaches a hundred thousand of each kind instead — a hundredfold increase over the second ground's own tenfold increase over the first, the same twelvefold catalogue of attainments (samādhis, buddhas beheld, worlds shaken and visited and illuminated, beings matured, eons of life sustained, eons past and future penetrated, dharma-gateways discerned, bodies manifested, and retinues displayed) continuing to scale upward at each successive ground.

Chinese Source

申八、受生　受生多分作釋天帝，善化有情，令離欲貪。

申九、威力　所有威力，於前住中已說千數，當知此中有百千數。

Ru-Yi Translation

Eighth, rebirth. Taking rebirth, one most often becomes Śakra, lord of the thirty-three heavens, skillfully transforming sentient beings, causing them to depart from craving and desire.

Ninth, majestic power. Whatever majestic power was already described in the previous abode as a thousand of each kind, one should understand that here it reaches a hundred thousand of each kind.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi notes the progression of rebirth across the first three grounds: iron-wheel king ruling one continent (first ground), gold-wheel king ruling four continents (second ground), and now Śakra himself, ruler of the thirty-three heavens — a rebirth many popular accounts mistakenly associate only with a stream-enterer's own attainment, whereas this passage makes clear that a third-ground bodhisattva may equally take this same exalted rebirth, precisely in order to use that position's own influence to turn beings away from craving and desire from a position of unmatched worldly authority.

He confirms the consistent scaling pattern now fully established across three grounds: a hundred at the first ground, a thousand (tenfold) at the second, and a hundred thousand (a further hundredfold) at the third — majestic power compounding far more steeply than rebirth's own worldly rank, suggesting that spiritual capability grows at a categorically different rate than worldly position as the grounds advance. Having now explained all nine aspects distinguishing the abode of heightened mind, he notes, the treatise turns next to a concluding summary naming all nine together, followed by the cross-reference explaining why this ground is called, in the wider tradition, the ground of shining light.

22. Milestone: The Summary Names All Nine Aspects, and the Treatise Explains — Heightened Mind Is the Ground of Shining Light

The treatise now closes the entire abode of heightened mind with a concluding summary and a final cross-reference. In brief, this is called the bodhisattva's abode of heightened mind: because purified-mind reflection brings entry to fulfillment; because formations, the realm of sentient beings, and great awakening are correctly penetrated; because the means for sentient beings' liberation from suffering is correctly sought out; because great reverence arises toward the true dharma, sought tirelessly; because dharma in accordance with dharma is correctly practiced, able to bring forth and abide in the mundane absorptions, concentrations, attainments, immeasurables, and spiritual powers; because, relinquishing these, one takes rebirth as one wishes through the sovereign power of vow; because of purified wholesome roots; because of rebirth; and because of majestic power. Should one wish to hear this expounded at greater length, the treatise directs the reader to the Ten Grounds Sutra's own chapter on the ground of shining light — explaining that this name derives from two sources of light: light arising from hearing and practicing the true dharma (through the ten dharma-conducts: upholding, reciting, teaching, and the rest), and light arising from meditative concentration itself — and it is this very same inner purity giving rise to light that this treatise, for its own part, calls the abode of heightened mind.

Chinese Source

未二、結略義　當知是名略說菩薩增上心住。謂心意樂作意思惟成滿趣入故；於一切行諸有情界及大菩提，能正通達故；於諸有情脫苦方便，能正推求故；於正法中起大恭敬，訪求無倦故；能正修行法隨法行，於其世俗諸靜慮等、等持、等至、無量神通，能引能住故；棄捨於彼，願自在力隨樂受生故；善根清淨故；受生故；威力故。

午二、廣指　未一、指配　若廣宣說，如十地經發光地說。由發聞行正法光明、等持光明之所顯示，是故此地名發光地。由內心淨能發光明，是故說名增上心住。

未二、釋名　由此義故，名發光地；即由此義，當知復名增上心住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, closing the brief meaning. One should understand this, in brief, as the bodhisattva's abode of heightened mind — namely, because purified-mind reflection brings entry to fulfillment; because formations, the realm of sentient beings, and great awakening are correctly penetrated; because the means for sentient beings' liberation from suffering is correctly sought out; because great reverence arises toward the true dharma, sought tirelessly; because dharma in accordance with dharma is correctly practiced, able to bring forth and abide in mundane absorptions, concentrations, attainments, and immeasurable spiritual powers; because, relinquishing these, one takes rebirth as one wishes through the sovereign power of vow; because of purified wholesome roots; because of rebirth; because of majestic power.

Second, the broader pointer; first, pointing to the correspondence. Should one wish to hear it expounded at length, it is exactly as the Ten Grounds Sutra expounds it in its chapter on the ground of shining light. Because it is displayed through the light of hearing and practicing the true dharma, and the light of meditative concentration, this ground is named the ground of shining light. Because inner purity of mind is able to give rise to light, this is why it is called the abode of heightened mind.

Second, explaining the name. Because of this meaning, it is named the ground of shining light; precisely because of this same meaning, one should understand it is also named the abode of heightened mind.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi reviews all nine aspects together as a final capstone: purified-mind reflection (ten further minds re-examining the prior ten); thorough penetration of formations, buddha-wisdom, and sentient beings, yielding freedom from laxity, blazing diligence, and vast compassion; seeking the ultimate means of liberation back through non-discriminating wisdom, supreme absorption, and hearing the true dharma; reverence for the dharma dramatized through willingness to endure the great pit of fire; correctly practicing dharma in accordance with dharma, mastering the mundane absorptions and five spiritual powers in order to tame beings' pride; relinquishing the higher realms to take rebirth by vow in the desire realm, severing the fetters of desire, existence, and view; purified wholesome roots (gold now weighed and found exactly equal); rebirth as Śakra; and majestic power a hundredfold greater than the ground before.

He explains the two sources of "light" the name commemorates: the light of hearing and practicing the true dharma — the ten dharma-conducts of writing out, making offerings to, giving to others, listening to, receiving and upholding, expounding, reciting, thinking over, cultivating, and reciting scripture, all radiating a kind of luminosity from genuine engagement with the teaching — and the light of meditative concentration itself, the mundane absorptions, immeasurables, and spiritual powers mastered in the fifth aspect all themselves possessing a radiant, illuminating quality. Both sources of light, he notes, trace back to this ground's own inner purity of mind — precisely why the treatise's own name, "heightened mind," and the Ten Grounds Sutra's own name, "shining light," name one and the same underlying reality. With this correspondence confirmed, the entire abode of heightened mind — the third ground, examined across all nine aspects — reaches its complete conclusion. The treatise turns next to the fourth of the eleven-part granular analysis's own next abode: the abode joined with the factors of awakening, corresponding to the fourth ground, opening with a notably larger structure of thirteen aspects rather than nine.

23. Milestone: The Abode Joined With the Factors of Awakening Opens — Ten Dharma-Lights of Entry, the Fourth Ground

The treatise now opens the fifth abode: joined with the factors of awakening, corresponding to the fourth ground, where a bodhisattva begins cultivating the thirty-seven factors of awakening in full, wisdom now blazing forth like flame — hence its wider name, the ground of blazing wisdom. The question is posed: what is a bodhisattva's abode joined with the factors of awakening? The answer opens with the first of a notably larger set of thirteen aspects: entry brought to fulfillment through the light of dharma. Bodhisattvas, previously at the abode of heightened mind, through the heightened power of seeking extensive learning, have already attained ten dharma-lights of entry; by these ten dharma-lights of entry becoming of the most excellent grade, utterly complete, one passes beyond the abode of heightened mind and enters the first of the three abodes classified as heightened wisdom (the fourth, fifth, and sixth grounds together forming this threefold group). The treatise then states plainly that the wording of these ten dharma-lights of entry follows exactly as given in the Ten Grounds Sutra itself, directing the reader there for their precise mark.

Chinese Source

辰五、覺分相應增上慧住　巳一、問　云何菩薩覺分相應增上慧住？

巳二、答　午一、略說　未一、別辨相　申一、由法明入成滿得入　酉一、明趣入　謂諸菩薩，先於增上心住，以求多聞增上力故，已得十法明入。由此十法明入成上品故，極圓滿故，超過增上心住，入初增上慧住。

酉二、釋略義　如是十法明入文辭，如契經說，應知其相。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fifth abode, joined with the factors of awakening; first, the question. What is a bodhisattva's abode joined with the factors of awakening?

Second, the answer; first, the brief account; first, distinguishing each mark; first, entry brought to fulfillment through the light of dharma; first, clarifying entry. Namely, bodhisattvas, previously at the abode of heightened mind, through the heightened power of seeking extensive learning, have already attained ten dharma-lights of entry. By these ten dharma-lights of entry becoming of the most excellent grade, utterly complete, one passes beyond the abode of heightened mind and enters the first abode of heightened wisdom.

Second, explaining the brief meaning. The wording of these ten dharma-lights of entry is exactly as the sutra explains; one should understand their mark accordingly.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi flags the significant jump in structure here: where the second and third grounds each unfolded across nine aspects, the fourth ground unfolds across thirteen — a marked increase in granularity that will recur, he notes, at several later grounds as well, tracking the treatise's own sense that later grounds require correspondingly richer description even though the underlying pattern (purified aspiration or its equivalent, penetrating understanding, purified wholesome roots, rebirth, majestic power) remains recognizable throughout.

He explains the grouping this ground belongs to: the fourth, fifth, and sixth grounds are together classified as the three abodes of "heightened wisdom" (paralleling heightened discipline at the second ground and heightened mind at the third), this fourth ground specifically named "joined with the factors of awakening" because it is here that the thirty-seven factors of awakening — the four foundations of mindfulness, four right efforts, four bases of spiritual power, five faculties, five powers, seven factors of awakening, and eightfold noble path — are cultivated in their fullest, most systematic form for the first time. Having named that ten dharma-lights of entry exist and that their wording follows the Ten Grounds Sutra directly, he notes, the treatise turns next to detailing all ten in full.

24. Milestone: All Ten Dharma-Lights Detailed — From the Realm of Beings to the Great, Resolved Mind — Closing the First Aspect

The treatise now details all ten dharma-lights of entry, cross-referencing the Ten Grounds Sutra's own exact list: contemplating the light of entry into the realm of sentient beings, the realm of worlds, the realm of dharmas, the realm of space, the realm of consciousness, the desire realm, the form realm, the formless realm, the realm of resolved faith in the superior mind, and the realm of resolved faith in the great mind. The treatise's own brief restatement maps each in turn: contemplating that this-and-that is merely provisional designation; that within this there is likewise provisional designation; that by this very designation, other designations follow; that ultimate truth is everywhere equal; that, through affliction or through purity, defilement or purity results; that one is bound and defiled through affliction's own fetters; and that one is purified through unsurpassed purity. Contemplating sentient beings, worlds, and dharmas addresses the merely provisional, nominal character of all such distinctions; contemplating space addresses the equal, ultimate truth underlying self and dharmas alike; contemplating consciousness addresses how defilement and purity both arise from mind alone; contemplating the three realms addresses how differing afflictions bind beings to differing rebirths; and contemplating the superior mind and the great mind addresses, respectively, a mind no longer defiled by affliction and a mind that, precisely because it is not defiled, still refuses ever to abandon sentient beings.

Chinese Source

《披》如是十法明入文辭等者：十地經說：若菩薩得第三菩薩地，具足清淨明已，欲得第四菩薩地者，當以十法明入，得入第四地。何等為十？一、思量眾生界明入，二、思量世界明入，三、思量法界明入，四、思量虛空界明入，五、思量識界明入，六、思量欲界明入，七、思量色界明入，八、思量無色界明入，九、思量勝心決定信界明入，十、思量大心決定信界明入。菩薩以此十法明入，得入第四地。

謂若彼假設；若於中假設；若由此假設；若平等勝義；若染惱故、清淨故，成染成淨；若由繫縛煩惱所染；若由無上清淨所淨。當知是名十法明入略所說義。

Ru-Yi Translation

Commentary: Regarding "the wording of these ten dharma-lights of entry" and the like — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: if a bodhisattva, having attained the third bodhisattva ground and its complete, pure light, wishes to attain the fourth bodhisattva ground, should enter by ten dharma-lights, thereby attaining entry into the fourth ground. What are the ten? First, the light of entry contemplating the realm of sentient beings. Second, contemplating the realm of worlds. Third, contemplating the realm of dharmas. Fourth, contemplating the realm of space. Fifth, contemplating the realm of consciousness. Sixth, contemplating the desire realm. Seventh, contemplating the form realm. Eighth, contemplating the formless realm. Ninth, contemplating the realm of resolved faith in the superior mind. Tenth, contemplating the realm of resolved faith in the great mind. A bodhisattva, by these ten dharma-lights of entry, attains entry into the fourth ground.

Namely: that this-and-that is merely provisional designation; that within this there is likewise provisional designation; that by this very designation, other designations follow; that ultimate truth is everywhere equal; that, through affliction or through purity, defilement or purity results; that one is bound and defiled through affliction's own fetters; that one is purified through unsurpassed purity. One should understand this as the brief meaning of the ten dharma-lights of entry.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi maps the correspondence precisely: the realm of sentient beings, worlds, and dharmas (the first three) all concern merely provisional, nominal designation — "Zhang" or "Li" as names imposed on a temporary aggregation of the five aggregates, the world one inhabits as a name imposed on one's dependent basis, and specific dharmas (wholesome or unwholesome) as the underlying reason particular beings occupy particular worlds in the first place, each designation building on the one before; the realm of space (the fourth) points to the equally empty, ultimate-truth nature of both self and dharmas, this equality itself as boundless as space; the realm of consciousness (the fifth) points to the properly Yogācāra insight that defilement and purity alike arise from mind, consciousness alone the basis on which both depend; the desire, form, and formless realms (the sixth through eighth) point to how differing kinds of affliction bind beings to correspondingly differing rebirths; and resolved faith in the superior mind and the great mind (the ninth and tenth) point respectively to a mind no longer touched by affliction and, precisely because of that same freedom, a mind resolved never to abandon sentient beings regardless.

He treats this tenfold contemplation as a bodhisattva's own systematic mapping of reality from the most provisional (mere names) through the most ultimate (empty, equal suchness) and back out again into compassionate engagement (the great mind that will not abandon beings) — a complete circuit rather than a mere list. With all ten dharma-lights of entry now detailed, closing the first of thirteen aspects, he notes, the treatise turns next to the second aspect: the wisdom that matures both oneself and other sentient beings.

25. Milestone: Ten Kinds of Maturing Wisdom, Gathered Into Four — Growing Ever More Powerful Within the Tathāgata's Family

The treatise now explains the second aspect: attaining maturing wisdom and its corresponding dharma. Bodhisattvas abiding in this abode, exactly as the sutra explains, take unwavering aspiration as foremost, and fully accomplish all ten kinds of maturing wisdom and the dharma matured by that wisdom — growing ever more established within the Tathāgata's own family, attaining that very substance itself. The commentary lists the Ten Grounds Sutra's own ten items in full: an unwavering mind; decisive, ultimate reverence toward the three jewels; discerning observation of arising and ceasing formations; discerning observation that all dharmas are, by their own nature, unarisen; discerning observation of the world's own formation and destruction; discerning observation that karmic action itself gives rise to birth; discerning observation of the world and of nirvana; discerning observation of the distinct karma shaping different worlds of sentient beings; discerning observation of the distinction between the prior and later boundary of time; and discerning observation that everything, ultimately, has nothing whatsoever that could be exhausted. The commentary further groups these ten into four underlying kinds of wisdom: the wisdom of one's own ultimate abode (unwavering commitment to the Mahāyāna itself); the wisdom of equally, ultimately revering the three jewels; the wisdom of suchness (observing both the selflessness of persons and the selflessness of dharmas); and the wisdom that discerns what is taught (the remaining six items, ranging across worldly and ultimate truth alike).

Chinese Source

申二、得熟智法　是諸菩薩住此住中，如契經說：不壞意樂而為上首，所有十種能成熟智、智成熟法皆悉成就。長如來家，得彼體法。

《披》如契經說不壞意樂而為上首等者：十地經說：以十種法智教化成熟。一、不退轉心故；二、於三寶中決定恭敬，畢竟盡故；三、分別觀生滅行故；四、分別觀諸法自性不生故；五、分別觀世間成壞故；六、分別觀業有生故；七、分別觀世間涅槃故；八、分別觀眾生世界業差別故；九、分別觀前際後際差別故；十、分別觀無所有盡故。諸佛子！菩薩如是十種法智教化成熟，即於如來家轉有勢力，得彼內法故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second aspect: attaining maturing wisdom and its dharma. Bodhisattvas abiding in this abode, exactly as the sutra explains, take unwavering aspiration as foremost, and all ten kinds of maturing wisdom and the dharma matured thereby are fully accomplished. Growing established within the Tathāgata's own family, one attains that very substance.

Commentary: Regarding "unwavering aspiration as foremost" and the like — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: by ten kinds of dharma-wisdom, teaching and maturing beings. First, an unwavering mind. Second, decisive, ultimate reverence toward the three jewels. Third, discerning observation of arising and ceasing formations. Fourth, discerning observation that dharmas are, by their own nature, unarisen. Fifth, discerning observation of the world's formation and destruction. Sixth, discerning observation that karmic action gives rise to birth. Seventh, discerning observation of the world and of nirvana. Eighth, discerning observation of the distinct karma of different worlds of sentient beings. Ninth, discerning observation of the distinction between the prior and later boundary. Tenth, discerning observation that everything has nothing whatsoever that could be exhausted. O sons of the buddhas! A bodhisattva, by these ten kinds of dharma-wisdom teaching and maturing beings, thereby grows in power within the Tathāgata's own family, attaining that inner dharma itself.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the fourfold grouping the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra itself proposes for these same ten items: the first (unwavering mind) is the wisdom of one's own ultimate abode — unshakeable commitment to the Mahāyāna as one's own home ground; the second (reverence toward the three jewels) is the wisdom of equally, ultimately revering them, whether encountering ordinary or noble members of the sangha alike; the third and fourth (observing arising-ceasing formations, and dharmas' own unarisen nature) together constitute the wisdom of suchness — the third observing selflessness of persons, the fourth observing selflessness of dharmas; and the remaining six (fifth through tenth) together constitute the wisdom that discerns what is taught, ranging from the physical world's own cycles of formation and destruction, through karmic causation, the distinction between saṃsāra and nirvana, the differing karma shaping different worlds, the movement from defiled past to potentially purified future, and the ultimate emptiness underlying every prior distinction — in the ultimate truth, nothing whatsoever is actually diminished among defiled dharmas nor increased among pure ones.

He treats "growing established within the Tathāgata's family, attaining that very substance" as marking real, measurable spiritual growth — a fourth-ground bodhisattva is not merely accumulating separate insights but is thereby gaining genuine power and standing within the lineage that will eventually flower into buddhahood itself, gradually acquiring facets of what will become the buddha's own body of qualities: the ten powers, four fearlessnesses, and the rest. Having explained the wisdom that matures self and others, he notes, the treatise turns next to the third aspect: cultivating the factors of awakening themselves in full.

26. Milestone: Cultivating the Thirty-Seven Factors of Awakening, the Four Foundations of Mindfulness Foremost

The treatise now explains the third aspect: cultivating the factors of awakening. Observing every kind of heightened power belonging to awakening itself and to sentient beings, such a bodhisattva cultivates the four foundations of mindfulness as foremost among the thirty-seven factors of awakening in their entirety — the four foundations of mindfulness, four right efforts, four bases of spiritual power, five faculties, five powers, seven factors of awakening, and the eightfold noble path all now cultivated together. The commentary explains "heightened power" precisely: observing every kind of awakening together with sentient beings themselves is what allows the very practice that remedies affliction to increase and grow — as the sutra itself puts it, observing the wisdom of every kind of wisdom, and never abandoning the mind set on every sentient being; this same observation, uniting great wisdom (delighting in buddha-wisdom) with great compassion (refusing to abandon beings), is precisely what causes diligence in cultivation to increase, since only sustained practice itself can actually bring both this wisdom and this compassion to their genuine fulfillment.

Chinese Source

申三、修習菩提分法　觀一切種菩提薩埵增上力故，修四念住而為上首三十七種菩提分法。

《披》觀一切種菩提薩埵增上力故者：謂觀一切種菩提及與薩埵，能令對治修行增長，名增上力。如經觀一切智智故，不捨一切眾生心故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Third aspect: cultivating the factors of awakening. Observing every kind of heightened power belonging to awakening and to sentient beings, one cultivates the four foundations of mindfulness as foremost among the thirty-seven kinds of factors of awakening.

Commentary: Regarding "observing every kind of heightened power belonging to awakening and sentient beings" — this means that observing every kind of awakening together with sentient beings themselves is able to cause the practice remedying affliction to increase; this is called heightened power. As the sutra says, observing the wisdom of every kind of wisdom, never abandoning the mind set on every sentient being.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this aspect as the natural practical consequence of the second: having gained maturing wisdom regarding oneself and others, a fourth-ground bodhisattva now takes up the thirty-seven factors of awakening as the concrete method for actually cultivating that wisdom to completion, the four foundations of mindfulness (contemplating body as impure, feeling as suffering, mind as impermanent, and dharmas as without self) leading the entire set as the foundational, most frequently emphasized practice.

He explains "heightened power" as arising from the union of two motivations working together: delighting in the buddhas' own comprehensive wisdom (a form of great wisdom, drawing the mind forward toward awakening) and refusing ever to abandon sentient beings (a form of great compassion, keeping practice tethered to genuine benefit rather than private attainment) — this combination is what actually accelerates diligence, since wisdom alone can turn abstract and compassion alone can turn merely sentimental, but together they generate sustained, effective practice. Having explained the cultivation of the thirty-seven factors of awakening, he notes, the treatise turns next to the fourth aspect: severing, at long last, the most subtle, innate form of the view of self.

27. Milestone: The Most Subtle, Innate View of Self Finally Severed — Root of Every Other View and of Pride Itself

The treatise now explains the fourth aspect: severing subtle views, attachment, and disturbance. Exactly as the sutra explains, through the skillful means gathered in these very factors of awakening, diligently cultivated, the most extremely subtle view of a substantial self — the attachment grasping at the aggregates, elements, and sense-fields as constituting a self, together with every disturbance this generates — is now, once and for all, severed from ever manifesting again. The commentary specifies that this innate view of a substantial self, born together with the body itself rather than adopted through mistaken reasoning, is called "most extremely subtle" precisely because it is the root from which every other wrong view and self-conceit itself springs forth; all such disturbance is now entirely extinguished — not the underlying seed itself, which a bodhisattva will not sever until the very last instant of the tenth ground, since continued rebirth is still required, but its actual manifestation, which is genuinely and permanently ended here. This, the commentary notes, is precisely the fruit that increased diligence in remedying affliction was always meant to produce.

Chinese Source

申四、斷微細見執著動亂　如契經說：由於此法方便攝受勤修習故，最極微細薩迦耶見，執著一切蘊、界、處等一切動亂，皆得畢竟不現行斷。

《披》最極微細薩迦耶見等者：此中薩迦耶見與身俱生，由是說言最極微細。此為根本，能生一切餘見及與我慢，是名執著一切蘊、界、處等一切亂動。如是一切皆悉斷滅，是名畢竟不現行斷。當知此即煩惱對治修行增長之果。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fourth aspect: severing subtle views, attachment, and disturbance. As the sutra explains: through the skillful means gathered in this dharma, diligently cultivated, the most extremely subtle view of a substantial self, the attachment grasping at the aggregates, elements, and sense-fields, and every disturbance this generates, are now entirely severed from manifesting again.

Commentary: Regarding "the most extremely subtle view of a substantial self" and the like — this view of a substantial self is born together with the body itself, and this is why it is called most extremely subtle. This is the root, able to give rise to every other wrong view and to self-conceit as well; this is called the attachment grasping at the aggregates, elements, and sense-fields, and every disturbance this generates. All of this is entirely extinguished; this is called permanently ended from manifesting again. One should understand this is exactly the fruit of increased diligence in remedying affliction.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi distinguishes carefully between the coarser, discriminated view of self already severed back at the very first ground (arising from mistaken intellectual views, adopted through wrong teachers or wrong reasoning, and hence relatively easy to cut through) and this far more stubborn, innate view of self — present from birth without any teaching required, the same view an infant already has before ever learning a single wrong doctrine — only now, at the fourth ground, finally prevented from manifesting at all. He explains why this view is called the root of every other wrong view and of pride itself: the extreme view, nihilism, eternalism, view-attachment, precept-attachment, and wrong view generally all ultimately trace back to some prior sense of "I" that such views are then built around, and pride itself (the sense of comparing oneself favorably or unfavorably to others) likewise presupposes this same underlying "I" being compared.

He carefully notes the precise scope of what is severed here: only the view's actual manifestation, not its underlying seed — a bodhisattva still needs to take further rebirth in order to continue benefiting beings, and the seed of self-view is not finally eliminated until the very last instant of the tenth ground, at the vajra-like concentration immediately preceding buddhahood itself — this fourth-ground achievement being genuinely complete freedom from the view's actual arising, even while its dormant seed remains present a while longer. Having explained the severing of this deepest, most subtle self-view, he notes, the treatise turns next to the fifth aspect: restraining certain conduct while permitting other conduct, and dwelling apart from over-familiarity.

28. Milestone: Restrained Conduct Ended, Praised Conduct Followed — The Fruit of Increased Karmic Remedy

The treatise now explains the fifth aspect: because this innate self-view's manifestation has now been severed, every conduct the Tathāgatas themselves condemn no longer manifests at all, while every conduct the Tathāgatas themselves praise is genuinely, correctly followed. The commentary specifies that where the fourth aspect displayed the fruit of increased remedy against affliction, this fifth aspect displays the corresponding fruit of increased remedy against karmic action itself: whatever action remains tainted by affliction, carrying the residue of grasping at self or dharmas, should not be done at all, and this is exactly what the Tathāgatas condemn; whatever accords with bodhisattva conduct — generosity, discipline, patience, diligence, meditative absorption, and wisdom — should indeed be done, and this is exactly what the Tathāgatas praise, such a bodhisattva now genuinely, correctly following it as a matter of course.

Chinese Source

申五、制業開業遠離習近　由彼斷故，一切如來所訶毀業皆不現行，一切如來所讚美業如實隨轉。

《披》一切如來所訶毀業等者：此說業對治修行增長之果。若業為諸煩惱所染，是不應作，此即如來所訶毀業。若順諸菩薩行，是所應作，此即如來所讚美業。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fifth aspect: restrained and permitted conduct, dwelling apart from over-familiarity. Because that view is severed, every conduct the Tathāgatas condemn no longer manifests at all, and every conduct the Tathāgatas praise is genuinely, correctly followed.

Commentary: Regarding "every conduct the Tathāgatas condemn" and the like — this displays the fruit of increased remedy against karmic action itself. Whatever action is tainted by affliction should not be done — this is exactly what the Tathāgatas condemn. Whatever accords with bodhisattva conduct should be done — this is exactly what the Tathāgatas praise.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this fifth aspect as the direct karmic consequence of the fourth: once the root view generating every affliction no longer manifests, the karmic actions that affliction used to produce simply stop arising on their own, without requiring separate, effortful restraint — while wholesome conduct, no longer competing against afflictive impulses, now flows forward as a matter of course. He notes the parallel the treatise itself draws between "increased remedy against affliction" (the fourth aspect's own fruit) and "increased remedy against karmic action" (this fifth aspect's own fruit) — affliction and the karma it produces are treated as two distinct, sequential objects of remedy, severing the root cause first and its karmic expression second.

Having explained restrained and permitted conduct as the fruit of this dual remedy, he notes, the treatise turns next to the sixth aspect: the mind itself growing supple, gentle, and thoroughly purified as these prior four aspects together mature into visible inner transformation.

29. Milestone: The Mind Grows Supple, Gentle, and Purified — Nine Kinds of Nourished Mind From the Ten Grounds Sutra

The treatise now explains the sixth aspect: having accomplished all of this, such a bodhisattva's mind grows nourished and gentle, genuinely capable, and every kind of its own activity becomes wholesome and pure. The commentary cross-references the Ten Grounds Sutra's own ninefold description of this "nourished mind": a soft mind, a tamed mind, a mind of peaceful ease, an undefiled mind, a mind ever seeking superior and higher conduct, a mind ever seeking superior wisdom, a mind that rescues the entire world, a mind reverent toward every teacher and compliant in receiving instruction, and a mind that practices in accordance with whatever dharma is heard — grouped into three: gladness in practice (the soft mind, growing ever happier the more one practices); sovereignty in absorption (the tamed mind, meditative mastery making the noble path ever more workable); and excellence in remedying faults (the mind of peaceful ease and the six further items following it, each remedying one specific fault — craving tied to food, jealousy toward others' praise, contentment that stops short of further progress, complacency that stops seeking superior wisdom, laziness in teaching others, clinging to one's own views against a teacher's instruction, and treating mere listening as sufficient without actual practice).

Chinese Source

申六、心轉調柔及善清淨　既如是已，其心轉復滋潤、柔和，有所堪能；其心轉復種種行相皆善清淨。

《披》其心轉復滋潤柔和等者：十地經說：如是如是成潤益心、軟心、調心、安隱樂心、不染心、轉求上上勝行心、轉求勝智心、救一切世間心、恭敬諸師隨順受教心、隨所聞法修行心。此說轉復滋潤，即彼成潤益心。此說柔和，即彼軟心。此說有所堪能，即彼調心。此說種種行相皆善清淨，即彼安隱樂心，乃至隨所聞法修行心。以彼諸心皆是離過對治所顯，故此總說種種行相。

Ru-Yi Translation

Sixth aspect: the mind grows supple and thoroughly purified. Having accomplished this, such a bodhisattva's mind grows nourished and gentle, genuinely capable; every kind of its own activity becomes wholesome and pure.

Commentary: Regarding "the mind grows nourished and gentle" and the like — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: in this way, one accomplishes a nourished mind, a soft mind, a tamed mind, a mind of peaceful ease, an undefiled mind, a mind ever seeking superior and higher conduct, a mind ever seeking superior wisdom, a mind that rescues the entire world, a mind reverent toward every teacher and compliant in receiving instruction, and a mind that practices in accordance with whatever dharma is heard. What is said here as "nourished" is exactly that nourished mind. What is said as "gentle" is exactly the soft mind. What is said as "genuinely capable" is exactly the tamed mind. What is said as "every kind of activity wholesome and pure" is exactly the mind of peaceful ease through the mind practicing in accordance with what is heard. Since all these minds are displayed through remedying faults, this is why it is said in total as "every kind of activity."

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi unpacks the nine minds carefully, grouped as the text itself suggests into three marks: the first mark, gladness in practice, is the soft mind — practice growing genuinely more joyful, not more burdensome, the deeper it goes. The second mark, sovereignty in absorption, is the tamed mind — sustained cultivation of the thirty-seven factors of awakening making meditative mastery ever more workable and available. The third mark, excellence in remedying faults, unpacks into seven specific remedies: the undefiled mind remedies both craving tied to food (freed from the affliction that arises around eating, clothing, and bodily maintenance) and jealousy (no longer resenting others' own praise or good fortune, since a bodhisattva whose own merit is genuinely full has nothing left to envy); the mind ever seeking superior conduct remedies complacent contentment (the fault of assuming one's current attainment is already sufficient); the mind ever seeking superior wisdom remedies intellectual complacency (assuming one need not seek wisdom beyond what one already has); the mind that rescues the entire world remedies laziness in teaching others; the mind reverent and compliant toward teachers remedies clinging stubbornly to one's own views against instruction; and the mind that practices in accordance with what is heard remedies the fault of treating mere listening as if it were itself sufficient practice.

He treats this ninefold nourished mind as the natural, visible flowering of everything already accomplished in the first five aspects — not a separate achievement but the mind's own felt transformation once affliction's root view and its karmic expression have both been addressed. Having explained the mind's own growing suppleness and purity, he notes, the treatise turns next to the seventh aspect: knowing kindness and repaying it, an abundance of wholesome qualities following naturally from genuinely purified aspiration.

30. Milestone: Knowing Kindness and Repaying It — Nine Forms of Gratitude, From Reverence to Never Misconstruing the Teacher's Own Meaning

The treatise now explains the seventh aspect: such a bodhisattva, skillfully knowing kindness received and skillfully knowing how to repay it, and every other aspiration according with this same spirit, thereby accomplishes every kind of wholesome dharma in abundance. The commentary cross-references the Ten Grounds Sutra's own ninefold elaboration of this gratitude, beginning from a mind that knows kindness and repays it: gratitude toward the three jewels' own kindness; a mind of mutual protectiveness toward fellow practitioners of the same dharma, dwelling together in peace and ease; a straightforward mind that follows the dharma exactly as taught; a gentle mind, able to endure whatever arises; a mind free of any dense thicket of hidden conduct, neither concealing faults nor advertising merits when receiving offerings from a benefactor; a mind free of flattery or crookedness, never falsely claiming attainment; a mind free of pride, never presuming on one's own superior qualities; a mind that correctly receives instruction; and a mind that grasps precisely what a teacher means, never misconstruing the teaching received.

Chinese Source

申七、隨順意樂功德隆盛　又善知恩、知報恩等隨順意樂，種種白法皆悉成就。

《披》又善知恩等者：十地經說：是菩薩如是成知恩心，知報恩心，轉柔和，同止安樂，直心、軟心，無稠林行、無有諂曲、無有我慢，善受教誨，得說者意，如是善心成就，如是寂滅心成就，如是善寂滅心成就。此應準釋。彼說善心、寂滅心，及善寂滅心，即此種種白法應知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Seventh aspect: aspiration accords, wholesome qualities abound. Furthermore, skillfully knowing kindness received, skillfully knowing how to repay it, and every aspiration according with this — every kind of wholesome dharma is thereby accomplished.

Commentary: Regarding "skillfully knowing kindness" and the like — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: this bodhisattva thus accomplishes a mind that knows kindness, a mind that repays it, growing gentle, dwelling together in peace; a straightforward mind, a gentle mind; a mind free of any dense thicket of conduct, free of flattery or crookedness, free of pride; skillfully receiving instruction, grasping the speaker's own meaning. In this way a wholesome mind is accomplished, a quiescent mind is accomplished, a wholesome, quiescent mind is accomplished. This should be explained exactly as there. That wholesome mind, quiescent mind, and wholesome quiescent mind are exactly this text's own "every kind of wholesome dharma," one should understand.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through all nine forms of gratitude the sutra names: knowing and repaying kindness toward the three jewels forms the foundation; mutual protectiveness toward fellow practitioners keeps the bodhisattva community itself harmonious, dwelling together in the classical six kinds of concord; the straightforward mind follows the teaching exactly as given, without private distortion; the gentle mind endures whatever hardship arises without complaint; freedom from any "dense thicket" of hidden conduct means receiving a benefactor's own offerings without either concealing one's own faults or advertising one's own merits to seem more worthy of the gift; freedom from flattery or crookedness means never claiming attainment one does not genuinely have, however tempting the resulting gain and reverence might be; freedom from pride means never presuming on one's own status as, say, a fourth-ground bodhisattva; skillfully receiving instruction means genuinely taking a teacher's guidance to heart; and grasping the teacher's own meaning — perhaps the subtlest of all — means never misconstruing what was actually said, a failure Cháng Bǎi notes is depressingly common even among sincere practitioners in the ordinary world, where speaker's intent and listener's understanding routinely diverge.

He treats the resulting "wholesome mind, quiescent mind, wholesome quiescent mind" as the fruit of these nine gratitudes taken together — quiescent mind marking abiding in ultimate truth free of all conceptual marks, wholesome quiescent mind marking this same quiescence as itself the fruit of genuine gratitude fully expressed. Having explained gratitude's own ninefold expression, he notes, the treatise turns next to the eighth aspect: seeking out ever-higher undertakings through great, unflagging diligence.

31. Milestone: Great, Unflagging Diligence — the Lute String Neither Too Taut Nor Too Slack

The treatise now explains the eighth aspect: seeking out ever-higher undertakings, such a bodhisattva arouses great diligence, thereby attaining genuine, abiding stability. Diligently seeking the fifth ground and beyond, continuing to cultivate the remedies against affliction and karmic action through the thirty-seven factors of awakening, such a bodhisattva accomplishes what the sutra calls unflagging diligence — not meaning never resting at all, but meaning, among its nine specified qualities, above all an evenly balanced diligence free of three kinds of contamination: laziness, being mixed with affliction, and being either excessive or insufficient. Excessive effort, pushed too hard too soon, risks precisely the kind of collapse and regression described in the classical story of the monk Śroṇa Koṭikarṇa, whose intense effort toward liberation, unrelieved and unmodulated, nearly drove him to abandon monastic life altogether — until the Buddha likened correct diligence to a lute string: too taut, and the string snaps or sounds poorly; too slack, and no music comes forth at all; only the string tuned exactly right produces its proper tone.

Chinese Source

申八、尋求上業發大精進　尋求上地能修治業，發大精進，逮得安住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Eighth aspect: seeking higher undertakings, arousing great diligence. Seeking out the higher ground and the practice able to remedy karmic action there, one arouses great diligence, thereby attaining genuine, abiding stability.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi tells the classical story behind this passage's own caution against excess: the monk Śroṇa Koṭikarṇa, pushing himself so hard toward liberation that his feet bled and yet still failed to sever affliction, grew so discouraged that he considered abandoning the robe to return to lay life and support the sangha instead as a donor. The Buddha, learning of this, asked Śroṇa whether he had once been skilled at playing the lute before ordaining, and whether the music came out well when the strings were pulled too tight, or when left too slack — Śroṇa answering that neither extreme produced good music, only strings tuned to just the right tension. The Buddha then drew the direct parallel to spiritual practice itself: neither excessive striving nor complacent slackness produces genuine progress, only patient, evenly sustained diligence, calibrated neither too tight nor too loose.

He treats this ancient teaching-story as illuminating exactly what the sutra's own nine qualities of unflagging diligence are driving at: a fourth-ground bodhisattva's own diligence is genuinely great, yet never contaminated by the extremes an eager but unskillful ordinary practitioner might fall into — not because the goal matters less, but because sustainable, well-calibrated effort actually reaches the goal more reliably than effort pushed to the point of collapse. Having explained this evenly balanced, great diligence, he notes, the treatise turns next to the ninth aspect: purifying aspiration into a genuinely settled disposition of resolute understanding.

32. Milestone: Purified Aspiration Becomes Settled Disposition — Cross-Referencing the Seven Aspects of Compassion Back in Fascicle 47

The treatise now explains the ninth aspect: through all these prior causes and conditions, every kind of aspiration, heightened aspiration, and resolute-understanding disposition is now brought to complete fulfillment. The commentary cross-references directly back to fascicle 47's own chapter on excellent aspiration: aspiration corresponds to the seven aspects of compassion a bodhisattva arouses deeply toward sentient beings there, called excellent aspiration and utterly excellent aspiration; heightened aspiration corresponds to that same chapter's account of pure faith taking the lead, discerning wisdom taking the lead, resolute understanding and settled confidence in the buddha-dharmas — called a bodhisattva's own heightened aspiration; and this aspiration and heightened aspiration, through the power of resolute understanding, becomes a habituated, settled disposition — precisely what "resolute-understanding disposition" names — the entire fulfillment tracing directly back to the great diligence just described in the eighth aspect as its own governing cause.

Chinese Source

申九、修淨意樂勝解界性　由此因緣，所有意樂、增上意樂勝解界性，皆得圓滿。

《披》所有意樂增上意樂等者：增上意樂品說：菩薩於諸有情深心發起七相憐愍，名善意樂、極善意樂。（陵本四十七卷九頁3760）是即此中所有意樂。又說：當知此中，淨信為先、擇法為先，於諸佛法所有勝解信解決定，是名菩薩增上意樂。（陵本四十七卷十頁3763）如是所有意樂、增上意樂，由勝解故，習所成性，是名勝解界性。此由前說發大精進為其因緣，故得圓滿。

Ru-Yi Translation

Ninth aspect: cultivating pure aspiration into a resolute-understanding disposition. By these causes and conditions, every kind of aspiration, heightened aspiration, and resolute-understanding disposition is brought to complete fulfillment.

Commentary: Regarding "every kind of aspiration, heightened aspiration" and the like — the chapter on excellent aspiration says: a bodhisattva, toward sentient beings, deeply arouses seven aspects of compassion, called excellent aspiration and utterly excellent aspiration (fascicle 47, page 3760 of the Nanjing edition). This is exactly this text's own "aspiration." It further says: one should understand that here, pure faith taking the lead, discerning choice of dharma taking the lead, resolute understanding and settled confidence regarding the buddha-dharmas, this is called a bodhisattva's own heightened aspiration (fascicle 47, page 3763 of the Nanjing edition). Such aspiration and heightened aspiration, through the power of resolute understanding, become a habituated, settled disposition — this is called resolute-understanding disposition. This is brought to fulfillment through the great diligence already described as its own cause and condition.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi welcomes this direct cross-reference back to fascicle 47's own third chapter as confirming just how continuous this entire treatise's structure actually is — the seven aspects of compassion and fifteen kinds of aspiration explained there in such careful detail, many milestones ago, are not superseded by later grounds but are precisely what matures, ground by ground, into the settled dispositions a fourth-ground bodhisattva now genuinely possesses. He explains "resolute-understanding disposition" as marking the difference between an aspiration one must consciously reaffirm each time and an aspiration so thoroughly habituated through repeated resolute understanding that it has become, in effect, second nature — no longer requiring effortful re-arousal, the way the treatise earlier distinguished effortful from spontaneous practice at the transition from resolute practice into the first ground.

He notes the causal chain the commentary makes explicit: this ninth aspect's fulfillment depends directly on the eighth aspect's own great, evenly balanced diligence — settled disposition is not simply given but earned through sustained practice precisely calibrated as the lute-string simile described. Having explained aspiration's maturation into settled disposition, he notes, the treatise turns next to the tenth aspect: demonic forces and hostile powers now entirely unable to shake this same settled resolve.

33. Milestone: Demonic Forces and Rival Teachings Unable to Shake This Resolve

The treatise now explains the tenth aspect: by these same causes and conditions, every non-Buddhist path, every kind of demonic force, and every hostile power opposed to the noble teaching are entirely unable to obscure or overturn this bodhisattva's own resolve, entirely unable to shake it. The commentary explains simply that this security follows directly from the ninth aspect's own fulfillment of resolute-understanding disposition — precisely because that disposition has now been brought to genuine completion, nothing external, whether non-Buddhist teaching or demonic obstruction of any kind (the five aggregates, affliction, the heavenly demon, or death itself), retains any power to obscure or overturn it.

Chinese Source

申十、魔怨無動　由是因緣，一切外道、種種魔軍、聖教怨敵，不能映奪、不能傾動。

《披》一切外道種種魔軍等者：此由前說勝解界性圓滿為其因緣，故彼一切不能映奪、不能傾動。

Ru-Yi Translation

Tenth aspect: demonic and hostile forces unable to shake this resolve. By these causes and conditions, every non-Buddhist path, every kind of demonic force, and every hostile power opposed to the noble teaching are entirely unable to obscure or overturn it, entirely unable to shake it.

Commentary: Regarding "every non-Buddhist path, every kind of demonic force" and the like — this follows from the previous fulfillment of resolute-understanding disposition as its own cause and condition; this is why all such forces are entirely unable to obscure, overturn, or shake it.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this tenth aspect as brief almost by design — having spent nine aspects building up genuine, settled resolve through purified wisdom, mind, gratitude, diligence, and disposition, the treatise simply states the natural consequence: such resolve, once genuinely established, needs no further elaborate defense against outside challenge, since the four classical demons and every non-Buddhist teaching alike have simply lost any purchase on a mind this settled.

He notes how this short aspect functions as a hinge, turning from the interior qualities just cultivated (aspects one through nine) toward the treatise's now-familiar closing sequence shared with every prior ground: purified wholesome roots, rebirth, and majestic power. Having explained the bodhisattva's own imperviousness to demonic and rival forces, he notes, the treatise turns next to the eleventh aspect: purified wholesome roots, given here in unusually elaborate form with two distinct similes.

34. Milestone: Gold Wrought Into an Ornament, and Wisdom Blazing Like a Jewel No Storm Can Extinguish

The treatise now explains the eleventh aspect in two parts, each with its own governing simile — this ground's treatment of purified wholesome roots notably richer than any prior ground's. First, the general account, pointing back to what was already explained at the first three grounds: broadly beholding the buddhas, wholesome roots purified, exactly as before, but now with a simile marking the difference: just as a skillful craftsman's own smelted gold, wrought into an ornament, can no longer be outshone by any gold not yet so wrought, so too this bodhisattva's own wholesome roots can no longer be outshone by any bodhisattva still abiding at an ordinary, unenlightened stage — this ground's gold no longer merely pure but actively fashioned into something of manifest, superior worth. Second, a further simile specifically for wisdom, this ground's own especially heightened perfection: just as light radiating from a wish-fulfilling jewel cannot be outshone by any lesser jewel, and cannot be extinguished by any worldly wind, water, or rain, so too this bodhisattva's own wisdom-light cannot be outshone by any hearer or solitary-realizer, and cannot be extinguished by any demonic, hostile force whatsoever — precisely the imagery behind this ground's own wider name, the ground of blazing wisdom.

Chinese Source

申十一、善根清淨　酉一、總辨善根　戌一、指同前　廣見諸佛、善根清淨，廣說如前，應知其相。

戌二、喻差別　此差別者，謂如世間善巧工匠，以所鍊金作莊嚴具，非餘未作莊嚴具金之所映奪。如是此中菩薩善根，非餘安住凡住菩薩所有善根所能映奪。

酉二、別喻慧根　如末尼寶所放光明，非餘寶珠所能映奪，一切世間風水雨等，不能斷滅所放光明。如是此中菩薩所有智慧光明，一切聲聞及獨覺等不能映奪，一切魔怨不能斷滅。

Ru-Yi Translation

Eleventh aspect: purified wholesome roots; first, the general account; first, pointing to what was already said. Broadly beholding the buddhas, wholesome roots purified, explained at length exactly as before; one should understand their mark accordingly.

Second, a simile marking the difference. The difference is this: just as, in the world, a skillful craftsman's own smelted gold, wrought into an ornament, cannot be outshone by gold not yet so wrought — so too this bodhisattva's own wholesome roots cannot be outshone by any bodhisattva still abiding at an ordinary, unenlightened stage.

Second, a further simile specifically for the faculty of wisdom. Just as light radiating from a wish-fulfilling jewel cannot be outshone by any other jewel, and every worldly wind, water, and rain cannot extinguish the light it radiates — so too this bodhisattva's own wisdom-light cannot be outshone by any hearer or solitary-realizer, and no demonic, hostile force whatsoever can extinguish it.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi highlights the escalation this simile represents over the first three grounds' own gold-refining imagery: raw ore purified by fire (first ground), refined gold purified further in alum (second ground), gold weighed and found exactly equal (third ground) — and now, at the fourth ground, that same pure gold actively wrought into a finished ornament, its worth no longer merely a matter of purity but of manifest, superior craftsmanship compared to gold not yet worked at all. He explains why this ground receives a second, dedicated simile specifically for wisdom (rather than lumping wisdom together with the other wholesome roots as before): the fourth ground is precisely where the thirty-seven factors of awakening are cultivated in full for the first time, making wisdom this ground's own especially heightened perfection, deserving its own illustration.

He connects the wish-fulfilling jewel's own light — outshining every lesser jewel, unquenchable by any worldly wind, water, or rain — directly to the ground's own name, "blazing wisdom" (or, in the Ten Grounds Sutra's own terms, the ground hard to conquer through fire): a fourth-ground bodhisattva's own wisdom cannot be outshone even by the wisdom of hearers or solitary-realizers, and cannot be extinguished by any demonic, hostile obstruction whatsoever, precisely the same imperviousness described in more general terms two milestones earlier. Having explained purified wholesome roots through both similes, he notes, the treatise turns next to the twelfth aspect: where this bodhisattva typically takes rebirth.

35. Milestone: Rebirth as the Lord of the Suyāma Heaven, Teaching Beings to Uproot the View of Self

The treatise now explains the twelfth aspect: should such a bodhisattva take rebirth as a householder, most often becoming the lord of the Suyāma heaven — the third of the six desire-realm heavens, situated above the Heaven of the Thirty-Three and below the Tuṣita heaven — skillfully transforming sentient beings there, causing them to eliminate the view of a substantial self.

Chinese Source

申十二、受生　受生多作蘇夜摩天王，善化有情，令其除滅薩迦耶見。

Ru-Yi Translation

Twelfth aspect: rebirth. Taking rebirth, one most often becomes the lord of the Suyāma heaven, skillfully transforming sentient beings, causing them to eliminate the view of a substantial self.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi traces the progression of rebirth now across four grounds: iron-wheel king (first ground, one continent), gold-wheel king (second ground, four continents), Śakra, lord of the thirty-three heavens (third ground), and now the lord of the Suyāma heaven, a rank higher still among the six heavens of the desire realm — each successive ground's own worldly rebirth climbing the desire realm's own hierarchy in step with the ground's own deepening spiritual attainment.

He notes the fitting correspondence between this ground's own signature achievement — severing the most subtle, innate view of self from manifesting, as explained in the fourth aspect many milestones earlier — and the specific teaching mission this rebirth is given: as lord of the Suyāma heaven, this bodhisattva specifically guides its inhabitants toward eliminating that very same view of self, teaching directly out of personal realization rather than secondhand instruction. Having explained rebirth as lord of the Suyāma heaven, he notes, the treatise turns next to the thirteenth and final aspect distinguishing the abode joined with the factors of awakening: majestic power.

36. Milestone: Majestic Power Now Reaching a Koṭi of Each Kind — Closing the Thirteenth and Final Aspect

The treatise now explains the thirteenth and final aspect: whatever majestic power was already described in the previous abode as reaching a hundred thousand of each kind, one should understand that here, at this abode, it reaches a full koṭi of each kind instead — koṭi denoting, in the classical Indian reckoning, ten million, the same twelvefold catalogue of attainments (samādhis realized, buddhas beheld, worlds shaken and visited and illuminated, beings matured, eons of life sustained, eons past and future penetrated, dharma-gateways discerned, bodies manifested, and retinues displayed) now scaled to this vastly greater measure, should such a bodhisattva likewise renounce the household life for monastic diligence.

Chinese Source

申十三、威力　所有威力，於前住中說百千數，當知此中說俱胝數。

Ru-Yi Translation

Thirteenth aspect: majestic power. Whatever majestic power was already described in the previous abode as a hundred thousand of each kind, one should understand that here it is described as a koṭi of each kind.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi confirms the scaling now fully established across four grounds: a hundred at the first, a thousand at the second, a hundred thousand at the third, and now a koṭi — ten million — at the fourth, a scale of increase that has itself been accelerating from ground to ground rather than holding to any single fixed multiplier. He notes that a koṭi bodhisattva-samādhis realized in a single instant, koṭi buddhas beheld directly, is a scale genuinely difficult to hold in the mind at all — which he suggests is rather the point, majestic power at this stage having outpaced anything ordinary comprehension could concretely picture, echoing the treatise's own earlier remark, at the abode of utmost joy, about spans of time no listener could concretely visualize.

With all thirteen aspects distinguishing the abode joined with the factors of awakening now complete, he notes, the treatise turns next to a concluding summary naming all thirteen together, followed by the cross-reference explaining why this ground is called, in the wider tradition, the ground of blazing wisdom.

37. Milestone: The Summary Names All Thirteen Aspects, and the Treatise Confirms — This Is the Ground of Blazing Wisdom

The treatise now closes the entire abode joined with the factors of awakening with a concluding summary and final cross-reference. In brief, this is called the bodhisattva's abode joined with the factors of awakening: because entry is brought to fulfillment through the light of dharma; because of maturing wisdom; because the factors of awakening are cultivated; because the view of a substantial self and every attachment and disturbance it generates are severed; because of restrained and permitted conduct, dwelling apart from over-familiarity; because, through these causes, the mind grows supple; because wholesome qualities, in accordance with this, all abound; because, seeking out the practice that remedies the higher ground, great diligence is aroused; because, through these causes, aspiration, heightened aspiration, and resolute-understanding disposition are purified and cultivated; because, through these causes, every hostile power opposed to the noble teaching is unable to obscure or shake it; because of purified wholesome roots; because of rebirth; because of majestic power. Should one wish to hear this expounded at greater length, the treatise directs the reader to the Ten Grounds Sutra's own chapter on the ground of blazing wisdom — explaining that, within this ground, the blazing flame of genuine wisdom regarding the factors of awakening is able to accomplish teaching sentient beings the true dharma with an illuminating wisdom, and this is precisely why this ground is named the ground of blazing wisdom; and, in a final correspondence, the treatise confirms that this very same ground is exactly what this treatise, for its own part, calls the abode joined with the factors of awakening.

Chinese Source

未二、結略義　當知是名略說菩薩覺分相應增上慧住。謂法明入成滿得入故；成熟智故；修習菩提分法故；薩迦耶見等一切執著動亂斷故；制業、開業遠離習近故；由是因緣心調柔故；隨順功德皆隆盛故；依所尋求修治地業發大精進故；由是因緣，所有意樂、增上意樂勝解界性淨修治故；由是因緣，一切聖教所有怨敵不能映奪及傾動故；善根清淨故；受生故；威力故。

午二、廣指　未一、指　若廣宣說，如十地經焰慧地說。

未二、釋　於此地中，菩提分法如實智焰，能成正法教慧照明，是故此地名焰慧地。

未三、配　又即彼地，此中說名覺分相應增上慧住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, closing the brief meaning. One should understand this, in brief, as the bodhisattva's abode joined with the factors of awakening — namely, because entry is brought to fulfillment through the light of dharma; because of maturing wisdom; because the factors of awakening are cultivated; because the view of a substantial self and every attachment and disturbance it generates are severed; because of restrained and permitted conduct, dwelling apart from over-familiarity; because, through these causes, the mind grows supple; because wholesome qualities, according with this, all abound; because, seeking out the practice remedying the higher ground, great diligence is aroused; because, through these causes, aspiration, heightened aspiration, and resolute-understanding disposition are purified and cultivated; because, through these causes, every hostile power opposed to the noble teaching is unable to obscure or shake it; because of purified wholesome roots; because of rebirth; because of majestic power.

Second, the broader pointer; first, pointing to it. Should one wish to hear it expounded at length, it is exactly as the Ten Grounds Sutra expounds it in its chapter on the ground of blazing wisdom.

Second, explaining. Within this ground, the blazing flame of genuine wisdom regarding the factors of awakening is able to accomplish teaching the true dharma with an illuminating wisdom; this is why this ground is named the ground of blazing wisdom.

Third, the correspondence. This very same ground is exactly what is here called the abode joined with the factors of awakening.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi reviews all thirteen aspects as a final capstone, noting how naturally they fall into three movements: an opening movement of entry and foundational wisdom (the ten dharma-lights, maturing wisdom, cultivating the factors of awakening, and severing the innate self-view — aspects one through four); a middle movement of interior transformation flowing from that foundation (restrained and permitted conduct, the mind's own suppleness, gratitude's abundance, great balanced diligence, aspiration's maturation into settled disposition, and imperviousness to hostile forces — aspects five through ten); and a closing movement matching every prior ground's own familiar triad (purified wholesome roots, rebirth, and majestic power — aspects eleven through thirteen) — a structure, he notes, that will likely recur, in some form, at the remaining grounds still to come, however their own aspect-counts may vary.

He treats the closing three-part naming — ground of blazing wisdom (from the Ten Grounds Sutra), explained via the blazing flame of genuine wisdom regarding the factors of awakening, and abode joined with the factors of awakening (from this treatise's own thirteen-abode framework) — as the treatise's now-familiar practice of reconciling its own terminology with the wider tradition at each ground's close. With this correspondence confirmed, the entire abode joined with the factors of awakening — the fourth ground, examined across all thirteen aspects — reaches its complete conclusion. The treatise turns next to the fifth of the eleven-part granular analysis's own next abode: the abode joined with the truths, the fifth ground and second of the three heightened-wisdom grounds, opening with its own question.

38. Milestone: The Abode Joined With the Truths Opens — the Fifth Ground

The treatise now opens the sixth abode: joined with the truths, corresponding to the fifth ground, the second of the three grounds classified together as heightened wisdom (following the abode joined with the factors of awakening at the fourth ground, and preceding a sixth ground still to come) — each of these three grounds sharing wisdom as their governing theme, yet each unfolding that theme through a different specific content, this ground's own content centered on the four noble truths themselves. The question is posed exactly as before: what is a bodhisattva's abode joined with the truths?

Chinese Source

辰六、諸諦相應增上慧住　巳一、問　（云何菩薩諸諦相應增上慧住？）

Ru-Yi Translation

Sixth abode, joined with the truths; first, the question. What is a bodhisattva's abode joined with the truths?

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the grouping this ground belongs to: the fourth, fifth, and sixth grounds are together called the abodes of heightened wisdom, each ground progressively deepening a bodhisattva's own wisdom through a different specific lens — the fourth ground through the thirty-seven factors of awakening, this fifth ground now through the four noble truths themselves (suffering, its origin, its cessation, and the path leading to cessation), and a sixth ground still to come through a yet further lens. He notes that each successive ground within this same threefold group tends to go deeper and more subtly into territory the previous ground has already opened, rather than starting on entirely new ground each time — wisdom itself deepening through concentric widening circles rather than through unrelated, separate topics.

Having posed the opening question, he notes, the treatise turns next to answering it, beginning, as at every previous ground, with the first of this ground's own aspects: an abode entered through the fulfillment of an aspiration of genuine equality.

39. Milestone: Ten Kinds of Equally Purified Aspiration — Past, Present, and Future Buddha-Dharma All Equal

The treatise now explains the first aspect: entry brought to fulfillment through equally purified aspiration — corresponding, in the Ten Grounds Sutra's own terms, to remedying pride connected with the purity of one's own body. Bodhisattvas, previously at the abode joined with the factors of awakening, have already attained ten kinds of equally purified aspiration; by this same aspiration becoming of the most excellent grade, utterly complete, one passes beyond the first abode of heightened wisdom and enters the second. The commentary supplies the Ten Grounds Sutra's own full list of these ten: a deeply pure mind of equality regarding past buddha-dharma; regarding future buddha-dharma; regarding present buddha-dharma; regarding purity of precept; regarding purity of mind; regarding purity that removes view, doubt, and regret; regarding purity of the wisdom distinguishing the path from what is not the path; regarding purity of the wisdom of conduct and its severance; regarding an ever-higher purity in contemplating every factor of awakening; and regarding purity in transforming and liberating every sentient being.

Chinese Source

申一、平等意樂成滿得入　酉一、明證入　謂諸菩薩，先於覺分相應增上慧住，已得十種平等清淨意樂。由彼平等清淨意樂成上品故，極圓滿故，超過第一增上慧住，證入第二增上慧住。

酉二、指經說　十種平等清淨意樂所有文辭，如契經說，應知其相。

《披》十種平等清淨意樂所有文辭等者：十地經說：若菩薩得第四菩薩地，善滿諸行已，欲入第五菩薩地，當以十平等深淨心，得入第五地。何等為十？一、過去佛法平等深淨心故；二、未來佛法平等深淨心故；三、現在佛法平等深淨心故；四、戒淨平等深淨心故；五、心淨平等深淨心故；六、除見疑悔淨平等深淨心故；七、道非道智淨平等深淨心故；八、行斷智淨平等深淨心故；九、思量一切菩提分法上上淨平等深淨心故；十、化度一切眾生淨平等深淨心故。

Ru-Yi Translation

First aspect: entry brought to fulfillment through equally purified aspiration; first, clarifying entry.
