Namely, bodhisattvas, previously at the abode joined with the factors of awakening, have already attained ten kinds of equally purified aspiration. By this equally purified aspiration becoming of the most excellent grade, utterly complete, one passes beyond the first abode of heightened wisdom and enters the second.

Second, pointing to what the sutra says. The wording of these ten kinds of equally purified aspiration is exactly as the sutra explains; one should understand their mark accordingly.

Commentary: Regarding "the wording of these ten kinds" and the like — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: if a bodhisattva, having attained the fourth bodhisattva ground and its well-fulfilled conduct, wishes to enter the fifth bodhisattva ground, should enter by ten kinds of deeply pure, equal mind. What are the ten? First, a deeply pure mind of equality regarding past buddha-dharma. Second, regarding future buddha-dharma. Third, regarding present buddha-dharma. Fourth, regarding purity of precept. Fifth, regarding purity of mind. Sixth, regarding purity removing view, doubt, and regret. Seventh, regarding purity of the wisdom distinguishing path from non-path. Eighth, regarding purity of the wisdom of conduct and its severance. Ninth, regarding ever-higher purity in contemplating every factor of awakening. Tenth, regarding purity in transforming and liberating every sentient being.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi groups these ten as the sutra itself suggests: the first three concern the equality of buddha-dharma across all three times — whichever buddha, past, present, or future, the dharma awakened to is exactly the same, deep reflection on this sameness itself purifying the mind; the fourth through sixth concern discipline, meditation, and wisdom respectively (precept purity, mental purity through absorption, and wisdom purity that removes wrong view, doubt about the three jewels and four truths, and regret over past conduct); and the seventh through tenth concern skillfully applying these three trainings — correctly distinguishing the noble path from every non-path, understanding the four kinds of practitioner distinguished by whether progress toward severing affliction is painful-slow, painful-swift, pleasant-slow, or pleasant-swift (depending on whether one has attained fundamental absorption and how sharp one's discerning wisdom has become), contemplating the thirty-seven factors of awakening ever more deeply, and transforming sentient beings while grasping no sign of any being transformed at all.

He treats this ground's own signature remedy — addressed in the Ten Grounds Sutra as overcoming pride connected with bodily purity — as pointing to a subtle danger: even genuine purity, whether of body, precept, or attainment, can become an occasion for comparing oneself favorably to others if not held with the same equanimity these ten equal minds cultivate. Having explained all ten kinds of equally purified aspiration, he notes, the treatise turns next to a concluding restatement summarizing their underlying meaning.

40. Milestone: Awakening Without Equal, and Every Method of Awakening Made Equal — Closing the First Aspect

The treatise now closes the first aspect with a summarizing restatement. Awakening without equal, and every method of awakening made equal, surpassing every remaining realm of sentient beings and every dharma, exactly according with equality — this, one should understand, is the brief meaning of these ten kinds of equally purified aspiration. The commentary maps this restatement directly onto the ten items just given: "awakening without equal" corresponds to the first three (past, present, and future buddha-dharma equally realized); "every method of awakening made equal" corresponds to the remaining seven (precept, mind, and wisdom purity, together with their skillful application) — all of them together subsumed under according with every buddha-dharma; and the word "without," attached to both, means that no discrimination whatsoever arises regarding either kind of equality, thereby surpassing pride connected with a sense of self and pride connected with a sense of dharmas alike, genuinely, truthfully penetrating that neither sentient beings nor dharmas admit of any increase or decrease at all.

Chinese Source

酉三、明略義　謂無等覺與諸覺等，超過所餘諸有情界及以諸法，如其平等。當知是名十種平等清淨意樂略所說義。

《披》謂無等覺等者：經說：過去、未來、現在佛法平等深淨心故，即此無等覺義。經說：所餘戒淨、心淨，乃至化度一切眾生淨平等深淨心故，即此無諸覺等義。由彼諸心隨順諸佛法故，總攝一切，名諸覺等。於諸等覺及諸覺等不起分別，說名為無，由是超過補特伽羅我慢及法我慢。於彼有情及以諸法，如實通達無有增減，是名如其平等，此即釋彼清淨意樂名平等義。

Ru-Yi Translation

Third, clarifying the brief meaning. Awakening without equal, and every method of awakening made equal, surpassing every remaining realm of sentient beings and every dharma, exactly according with equality — one should understand this as the brief meaning of these ten kinds of equally purified aspiration.

Commentary: Regarding "awakening without equal" and the like — the sutra says: a deeply pure mind of equality regarding past, future, and present buddha-dharma is exactly this "awakening without equal." The sutra says: the remaining purity of precept, mind, on through purity in transforming and liberating every sentient being is exactly this "every method of awakening made equal." Because all these minds accord with every buddha-dharma, gathering everything together, they are called "every method of awakening." Because no discrimination arises regarding either awakening without equal or every method made equal, this is called "without" — thereby surpassing pride connected with a sense of self and pride connected with a sense of dharmas. Genuinely penetrating that sentient beings and dharmas admit of no increase or decrease is called "exactly according with equality" — this explains why this purified aspiration is named equality.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this closing restatement as an elegant compression: the first three of ten items collapse into "awakening without equal" (the sameness of realized truth across every buddha of every time), while the remaining seven collapse into "every method of awakening made equal" (the sameness of the very training — precept, mind, wisdom, and their skillful application — every bodhisattva undertakes regardless of individual circumstance). The word "without," he notes, is doing real philosophical work here: it is not that awakening and its methods are merely similar across cases, but that no discriminating thought arises comparing one instance against another at all — this very absence of comparison is what allows pride connected with self and pride connected with dharmas to be genuinely surpassed, rather than merely suppressed.

He treats "sentient beings and dharmas admit of no increase or decrease" as the ultimate ground making this equality genuine rather than merely conceptual — in truth, nothing is ever actually added to or subtracted from either, all apparent difference being provisional designation rather than final reality. With the first aspect now fully closed, he notes, the treatise turns next to the second aspect: skillful means for observing the four noble truths themselves.

41. Milestone: Ten Further Aspects for Observing the Four Noble Truths — From Worldly Truth to Gathering the Tathāgata's Own Wisdom

The treatise now explains the second aspect: skillful means for observing the truths. Bodhisattvas abiding in this abode, for the most part, seek out wisdom's own excellent nature, genuinely knowing the four noble truths through ten distinct aspects — the full wording of which follows exactly as the sutra explains. The commentary supplies the Ten Grounds Sutra's own tenfold elaboration: genuinely knowing this is the noble truth of suffering, this is its origin, this is its cessation, this is the path to its cessation; and, skillfully, knowing worldly truth, ultimate truth, the truth of characteristics, the truth of distinctions, the truth of how teaching is established, the truth of actual matters, the truth of arising, the truth of the wisdom of cessation without further arising, the truth of the wisdom that leads into the path, and the truth of each bodhisattva ground's own sequential accomplishment together with gathering the Tathāgata's own wisdom.

Chinese Source

申二、善巧方便觀察諸諦　酉一、指經說　如是菩薩住此住中，多分希求智殊勝性。於四聖諦，由十行相如實了知。一切文辭，如契經說，應知其相。

《披》於四聖諦由十行相如實了知等者：十地經說：是菩薩如實知是苦聖諦、是苦集諦、是苦滅諦，如實知是至滅苦道聖諦，是菩薩善知世諦、善知第一義諦、善知相諦、善知差別諦、善知說成諦、善知事諦、善知生諦、善知盡無生智諦、善知令入道智諦、善知一切菩薩地次第成就諦及善知集如來智諦。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second aspect: skillful means for observing the truths; first, pointing to what the sutra says. Such bodhisattvas, abiding in this abode, for the most part seek out wisdom's own excellent nature. Regarding the four noble truths, through ten distinct aspects, they genuinely know them; the full wording follows exactly as the sutra explains — one should understand their mark accordingly.

Commentary: Regarding "regarding the four noble truths, through ten distinct aspects" and the like — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: this bodhisattva genuinely knows this is the noble truth of suffering, this is the truth of suffering's origin, this is the truth of suffering's cessation; genuinely knows this is the noble truth of the path to suffering's cessation. This bodhisattva skillfully knows worldly truth, skillfully knows ultimate truth, skillfully knows the truth of characteristics, skillfully knows the truth of distinctions, skillfully knows the truth of how teaching is established, skillfully knows the truth of actual matters, skillfully knows the truth of arising, skillfully knows the truth of the wisdom of cessation without further arising, skillfully knows the truth of the wisdom leading into the path, and skillfully knows the truth of every bodhisattva ground's own sequential accomplishment together with gathering the Tathāgata's own wisdom.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains why knowing suffering itself already counts as genuine wisdom: an ordinary being, immersed in suffering, does not actually recognize it as suffering — chasing after more of exactly what causes suffering, mistaking impermanent pleasure for lasting happiness — whereas a noble one, and especially a fifth-ground bodhisattva, genuinely sees the three realms' entire round of rebirth as suffering at its root, this recognition itself already a mark of penetrating wisdom rather than mere pessimism. He traces the remaining three truths in brief: the origin of suffering traced to craving, rooted ultimately in grasping at self; suffering's cessation as affliction and its causes entirely severed; and the path as the noble path itself, genuinely capable of ending affliction and severing suffering at the root.

He then walks through the additional ten items the sutra names for skillfully teaching these same four truths to others: worldly truth (ordinary language, custom, and craft, needed to reach beings not yet ready for higher teaching); ultimate truth (for beings whose faculties have matured, able to receive teaching on cessation and the path directly); the truth of characteristics and of distinctions (the shared and particular marks of each truth, resolving doubt about the profound dharma and confusion between different teachings); the truth of how teaching is established (the actual verbal, logical structure by which the four truths are taught); the truth of actual matters (skill in aggregates, sense-fields, elements, dependent origination, and what is or is not possible); the truth of arising (specifically regarding the truth of origin); the wisdom of cessation without further arising (regarding the truth of cessation); the wisdom leading into the path (regarding the truth of the path); and, finally, knowing the ordered sequence of accomplishment across all ten bodhisattva grounds together with how the Tathāgata's own wisdom is ultimately gathered — this last item requiring mastery of the five branches of learning appropriate to guiding Mahāyāna-capable beings specifically. Having detailed all ten aspects for observing the four truths, he notes, the treatise turns next to a concluding restatement of their brief meaning.

42. Milestone: The Fourfold Restatement — By Whom, By What Source, and For What Purpose the Truths Are Taught

The treatise now closes the second aspect with a fourfold restatement. Relying on awakening others, relying on one's own inner wisdom, relying on the two together — this is called "for this reason it is said." Relying on the sutras, the discipline, and the root-treatises — this is called "by this it is said." Relying on the nature of present suffering itself, relying on the nature that gives rise to future suffering's cause, relying on the nature by which, the cause exhausted, that same suffering is exhausted without further arising, relying on the nature of the very means for cultivating this severance — this is called "explained in just this way." One should understand this as the brief meaning of the ten aspects of wisdom regarding the four noble truths. The commentary clarifies that this is not itself a restatement of all ten aspects individually, but rather the essential meaning underlying that wisdom as a whole: the first fourfold reliance explains why the teaching is given (to mature both oneself and others); the second threefold reliance explains the textual source from which it is drawn (the entirety of buddha-word, comprising scripture, discipline, and treatise together); and the third fourfold reliance explains its actual content (the four truths themselves, each explained according to its own specific nature).

Chinese Source

酉二、明略義　謂依曉悟他、依自內智、依俱處所，名為此說。依於契經、調伏、本母，名由此說。依於現在眾苦自性、依於未來苦因生性、依於因盡彼盡無生性、依於修習彼斷方便性，名如此說。當知是名十種行相四聖諦智所有略義。

《披》謂依曉悟他等者：此中略義，非說四聖諦智十種行相，然是彼智所有略義。言依曉悟他者，是即十種行相略義，由此一切能熟他故。言依自內智者，是即四聖諦智略義，於彼一切如實知故。言依俱處所者，是即總說十相四聖諦智略義，依他、依自而生起故。為此一切，是故宣說名為此說。言契經、調伏、本母者，此顯佛教由事施設建立相，以此三事總攝一切諸佛言教故。由此三事而有所說，名由此說。言依於現在眾苦自性等者，以此為依，如次建立四諦差別。如其自性而宣說故，名如此說。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, clarifying the brief meaning. Relying on awakening others, relying on one's own inner wisdom, relying on the two together — this is called "for this reason it is said." Relying on the sutras, the discipline, and the root-treatises — this is called "by this it is said." Relying on the nature of present suffering, relying on the nature giving rise to future suffering's cause, relying on the nature by which, the cause exhausted, suffering too is exhausted without further arising, relying on the nature of the means for cultivating this severance — this is called "explained in just this way." One should understand this as the brief meaning of the ten aspects of wisdom regarding the four noble truths.

Commentary: Regarding "relying on awakening others" and the like — this brief meaning does not restate the ten aspects individually, but is rather the essential meaning of that wisdom as a whole. "Relying on awakening others" is the brief meaning of the ten aspects, since by these all others are matured. "Relying on one's own inner wisdom" is the brief meaning of the four-truths wisdom itself, since by this everything is genuinely known. "Relying on the two together" is the total brief meaning of the ten-aspect four-truths wisdom, since it arises relying on both others and oneself. For the sake of all this, it is therefore proclaimed — this is called "for this reason it is said." As for "the sutras, the discipline, and the root-treatises" — this displays how the buddha's teaching is established through actual matters, since these three together gather in the entirety of every buddha's own teaching; by these three there is what is taught — this is called "by this it is said." As for "relying on the nature of present suffering" and the like — relying on this, the four truths are established in their distinct order; each is proclaimed exactly according to its own nature — this is called "explained in just this way."

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi clarifies a point easily missed: this restatement is not simply a shorter version of the ten aspects just given, but a different kind of summary altogether — explaining why, from what source, and how the entire teaching of the four truths (in its ten-aspect elaborated form) is given in the first place. He walks through the three-part logic: teaching happens because it serves both the maturation of other beings (the ten aspects' own purpose) and one's own inner realization (the four truths themselves, genuinely known); it draws on the complete body of buddha-word, sutra, discipline, and treatise together, no single one alone sufficient; and its actual content unfolds according to each truth's own specific nature — present suffering, its future-generating cause, the exhaustion of that cause and the suffering it would have generated, and the practical means for bringing about that very exhaustion.

He treats this fourfold restatement's own precision — distinguishing purpose, source, and content so cleanly — as itself an example of the very wisdom this aspect describes: a fifth-ground bodhisattva does not simply repeat received teaching but organizes and understands it with this same systematic clarity. With the second aspect now fully closed, he notes, the treatise turns next to the third aspect: observing the destructibility of all conditioned formations and, from this observation, arousing compassion for suffering sentient beings.

43. Milestone: Formations Seen as Deceptive and Empty, and a Vivid Portrait of Beings Who Will Not Flee Their Own Decaying Bodies

The treatise now explains the third aspect: such a bodhisattva, skilled in the truths, correctly destroys every conditioned formation through wisdom, increases compassionate aspiration toward the realm of sentient beings, and correctly penetrates the wrongful conduct of beings deluded regarding both the prior and later boundary of time. Having observed that every conditioned formation — the five aggregates, presenting a false appearance of permanence, pleasure, self, and purity — is in truth deceptive and empty, exactly like a dream that feels vividly real while it lasts, such a bodhisattva turns this same clarity toward sentient beings themselves: beings deluded about the past cling to a self there, beings deluded about the future cling to a self there too, and, out of this same delusion, grasp at formations as either utterly annihilated moment to moment or as one single, unchanging thing across time — the commentary specifying that this describes beings ignorant of the prior boundary, ignorant of the later boundary, and ignorant of both together, whose incorrect discrimination toward formations results either in nihilism or in eternalism. The Ten Grounds Sutra itself supplies a vivid portrait of this same delusion: countless bodies already destroyed, being destroyed now, and yet to be destroyed, and yet, having witnessed this destruction completely, ordinary beings still give rise to no aversion toward the body at all — continuing instead to generate further karmic mechanisms for yet another suffering body, forever adrift in the world's own current, unable to return; never fleeing the four elements' own venomous nature, unable to draw out the arrow of self-pride and wrong view, unable to extinguish the fire of craving, hatred, and delusion, unable to break through ignorance's own darkness, unable to drain the great river of clinging attachment, never seeking out the great teacher endowed with the ten powers, constantly entering the dense thicket of demonic intention, forever turned about by every kind of evil deliberation upon the sea of birth and death.

Chinese Source

申三、毀壞諸行悲愍有情　如是於諦善巧菩薩，於一切行以慧正毀，於有情界增悲意樂，於前後際愚癡有情所有邪行能正通達。

《披》於前後際愚癡有情所有邪行者：謂如前際無知、後際無知、前後際無知諸有情類，於彼諸行起不如理分別，或執為斷、或執為常，名彼所有邪行。

（十地經）菩薩爾時作如是念：「此諸凡夫甚為可怪，愚癡無智有無量無邊阿僧祇身，已滅、今滅、當滅。如是盡滅已，不能於身生厭離想，轉更增長機關苦身，常隨世間漂流不能得返，歸五陰舍不能捨離，不畏四大毒蛇，不能拔出我慢見箭，不能滅除貪恚癡火，不能破壞無明黑暗，不能乾竭愛著大河，不求十力大聖導師，常入魔意稠林，於生死海中常為諸惡覺觀所轉。」

Ru-Yi Translation

Third aspect: destroying conditioned formations, arousing compassion for sentient beings. A bodhisattva skilled in the truths in this way correctly destroys every conditioned formation through wisdom, increases compassionate aspiration toward the realm of sentient beings, and correctly penetrates the wrongful conduct of beings deluded regarding both the prior and later boundary of time.

Commentary: Regarding "the wrongful conduct of beings deluded regarding both the prior and later boundary" — this refers to beings ignorant of the prior boundary, ignorant of the later boundary, and ignorant of both together, who, toward conditioned formations, give rise to incorrect discrimination, grasping either at annihilation or at permanence — this is called their wrongful conduct.

The bodhisattva at that time reflects thus: these many ordinary beings are truly strange — foolish, without wisdom, having had immeasurable, boundless bodies already destroyed, being destroyed now, and yet to be destroyed. Having witnessed such complete destruction, they still cannot give rise to any thought of aversion toward the body, instead further increasing the karmic mechanism for yet another suffering body, forever following the world's own current, unable to return; unable to depart from the five-aggregate dwelling, unafraid of the four elements' venomous serpents, unable to draw out the arrow of self-pride and view, unable to extinguish the fire of craving, hatred, and delusion, unable to break through ignorance's own darkness, unable to drain the great river of clinging attachment, not seeking the great, noble guide endowed with the ten powers, constantly entering the dense thicket of demonic intention, forever turned about by every evil deliberation upon the sea of birth and death.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats the dream simile as central to understanding this aspect: a dream, however vivid and convincing while it lasts, is recognized as empty the moment one wakes — a fifth-ground bodhisattva sees every conditioned formation this same way, not by denying its vivid appearance but by penetrating that appearance's own emptiness underneath. He explains the two wrong extremes that ignorance of past and future generates: nihilism (grasping formations as utterly annihilated moment to moment, denying any continuity at all, as if the person of one instant bore no relation to the next) and eternalism (grasping formations as one single, unchanging thing persisting across time) — dependent origination itself being neither, a genuine, similar-yet-changing continuity that is neither strictly identical from moment to moment nor strictly severed.

He lingers over the Ten Grounds Sutra's own vivid portrait, treating it as one of the most striking passages in the entire chapter: ordinary beings, having witnessed body after body perish across countless past lives, nonetheless generate not the slightest aversion toward embodiment itself, continuing instead to manufacture the very karmic mechanisms that guarantee yet another cycle of suffering — unafraid even of the body's own four elements, likened here to venomous serpents constantly threatening it from within (earth's excess bringing heaviness, water's excess bringing swelling, fire's excess bringing heat and inflammation); unable to extract the arrow of pride and wrong view lodged within; unable to douse the threefold fire of greed, hatred, and delusion; and, perhaps most poignantly, rarely even seeking out a genuine teacher capable of guiding them out at all. A fifth-ground bodhisattva's own compassion, he notes, arises precisely from this clear-eyed, undeceived observation — not sentimental pity, but genuine grief at witnessing beings so thoroughly caught by their own ignorance that even repeated, direct evidence of impermanence fails to prompt the aversion that might finally motivate liberation. Having explained this compassionate observation, he notes, the treatise turns next to the fourth aspect: vowing to gather the provisions of merit and wisdom needed to actually help such beings.

44. Milestone: Vowing to Gather Merit and Wisdom, and Every Excellent Quality Flourishing Under Mindful, Discerning Conduct

The treatise now explains the fourth and fifth aspects. Fourth, vowing to gather the provisions of merit and wisdom: in order that suffering beings may attain liberation, such a bodhisattva gathers vast provisions of merit and wisdom, the mind arousing a correct vow to this effect — merit mattering not only because a strong, capable body makes sustained cultivation of the noble path possible, but because sentient beings themselves are far more readily persuaded by one whose own merit is manifestly abundant, wisdom mattering because only genuine wisdom directs merit correctly rather than wastefully. Fifth, mindful, discerning conduct flourishing in excellence: precisely within this same aspiration, taking correct mindfulness and discerning conduct as foremost, every one of the many excellent qualities — the six perfections, the ten perfections, and the rest — thereby flourishes in abundance, since a mind guided moment to moment by wisdom-informed mindfulness never lets a single instant pass unused, whether walking, standing, sitting, or lying down.

Chinese Source

申四、發願攝受福智資糧　為欲令彼得解脫故，攝受廣大福智資糧，心發正願。

申五、念慧行等功德增盛　及即於彼意樂引攝正念慧行而為上首，所有眾多殊勝功德皆悉增盛。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fourth aspect: vowing to gather the provisions of merit and wisdom. In order that beings may attain liberation, gathering vast provisions of merit and wisdom, the mind arouses a correct vow to this effect.

Fifth aspect: mindful, discerning conduct flourishing in excellence. And, precisely within that same aspiration, taking correct mindfulness and discerning conduct as foremost, all the many excellent qualities thereby flourish in abundance.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains why merit receives such deliberate emphasis here, easily underrated next to wisdom: a teacher's own words carry differently depending on whether that teacher's merit is manifestly abundant or manifestly lacking, sentient beings naturally more inclined to heed one whose circumstances themselves testify to genuine attainment — merit is not vanity but a genuine skillful means for reaching beings at all. He treats the fifth aspect as the direct behavioral consequence of the fourth: a vow this serious cannot remain merely aspirational, but must express itself as constant, mindful, wisdom-guided conduct across every single moment, the six perfections (and, in this treatise's own fuller enumeration, the ten) flourishing precisely because no moment is wasted on anything else.

Having explained the vow to gather merit and wisdom together with its flourishing expression in conduct, he notes, the treatise turns next to the sixth aspect: setting aside every remaining concern in order to diligently mature sentient beings.

45. Milestone: Every Other Concern Set Aside — Diligently Maturing Sentient Beings Alone

The treatise now explains the sixth aspect: every other kind of intention is entirely set aside, and, through every kind of skillful means for maturation, such a bodhisattva matures sentient beings. The commentary specifies precisely what "every other kind of intention" means: every inferior intention belonging to hearers and solitary-realizers alike, since both, in the end, abandon sentient beings in pursuit of their own personal liberation — a fifth-ground bodhisattva harbors no trace of this same self-concerned intention, mind and conduct alike wholly given over to maturing beings rather than divided between that task and any private agenda.

Chinese Source

申六、無餘作意勤熟有情　諸餘作意皆悉遠離。以其種種成熟方便成熟有情。

《披》諸餘作意皆悉遠離者：一切聲聞、獨覺下劣作意，名餘作意，捨有情故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Sixth aspect: no remaining intention, diligently maturing sentient beings. Every other kind of intention is entirely set aside. Through every kind of skillful means for maturation, one matures sentient beings.

Commentary: Regarding "every other kind of intention is entirely set aside" — every inferior intention belonging to hearers and solitary-realizers is called other intention, since both abandon sentient beings.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this aspect as a pointed contrast with the two lesser vehicles: a hearer or solitary-realizer's own path, however sincerely pursued, ultimately culminates in personal liberation alone, sentient beings left behind in the process — precisely the "other intention" a fifth-ground bodhisattva has entirely set aside, every remaining trace of self-concerned motivation now genuinely absent from a mind wholly devoted to maturing others.

Having explained this single-minded devotion to maturing sentient beings, he notes, the treatise turns next to the seventh aspect: drawing forth every kind of worldly craft and branch of learning as a further skillful means for reaching beings where they actually are.

46. Milestone: Twelve Worldly Crafts Mastered — Writing, Logic, Rhetoric, Medicine, Seals, and Reckoning

The treatise now explains the seventh aspect: as the sutra says, whatever worldly writings, treatises, seals, reckoning, and other crafts and branches of learning genuinely benefit sentient beings, all of these a bodhisattva is able to draw forth and master. The commentary specifies that worldly writings and treatises here mean specifically logical treatises, treatises on rhetoric, and treatises on medicine, while the crafts and branches of learning number twelve in total: farming, commerce, serving a king, writing and reckoning, divination, mantra-craft, construction, animal husbandry, guarding against wrongdoing, reconciling disputes, bringing things to completion, and music — seals and reckoning already named separately, with the remaining items grouped under "and the rest." Out of deep compassion for sentient beings, such a bodhisattva masters all of this gradually, in order to skillfully establish beings, by stages, in wondrous awakening itself.

Chinese Source

申七、引發世間工業明處　酉一、標所為　如契經說：所有種種能益有情世俗書論、印算計等工業明處，於是一切皆能引發；

《披》世俗書論等者：因論聲論及醫方論，是名世俗書論。工業明處有十二種，印算計外，餘置等言。

於諸有情深悲愍故，漸次乃至方便安立妙菩提故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Seventh aspect: drawing forth worldly crafts and branches of learning; first, naming the purpose. As the sutra says: whatever worldly writings and treatises, seals, reckoning, and other crafts and branches of learning genuinely benefit sentient beings — all of these, one is able to draw forth.

Commentary: Regarding "worldly writings and treatises" — logical treatises, treatises on rhetoric, and treatises on medicine are called worldly writings and treatises. The crafts and branches of learning number twelve kinds; beyond seals and reckoning, the rest are placed under "and the rest."

Out of deep compassion for sentient beings, gradually, by stages, skillfully establishing them in wondrous awakening.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains why each of these branches of learning matters specifically: writing remedies the natural fading of memory over time, allowing dharma once heard to be recorded and preserved; logical treatises allow debate with non-Buddhist schools, establishing the correct view against wrong ones; rhetorical treatises remedy any impression that Buddhist teachers lack cultural sophistication, a concern especially relevant when reaching educated audiences; and medical treatises win genuine trust, since a monastic capable of relieving physical suffering earns credibility no argument alone could establish. He explains seals as the ancient method for marking ownership (stamped onto a book or object to prevent others taking it) and reckoning as the mathematics needed for trade and commerce, cross-referencing the chapter on the stage formed through hearing for the fuller, traditional list of all twelve crafts: farming, commerce, court service, writing and reckoning, divination, mantra-craft, construction, animal husbandry, guarding against wrongdoing, reconciling disputes, bringing things to completion, and music — a customary reduction, in ancient India, of the full three hundred sixty recognized trades down to twelve representative categories.

He treats this aspect as continuous with the fourth ground's own cultivation of the mundane absorptions and spiritual powers to reach beings not yet ready for higher teaching: worldly competence, whether meditative or scholarly, is never pursued for its own sake but always as a skillful means for gradually leading beings toward genuine awakening — a bodhisattva whose worldly learning exceeds ordinary experts earns their trust directly; even where it does not exceed them, the bodhisattva's own merit and skillful conduct still opens a door. Having explained the purpose of mastering these twelve worldly crafts, he notes, the treatise turns next to a concluding tenfold restatement of exactly how this same worldly mastery benefits sentient beings in practice.

47. Milestone: Ten Concrete Ways Worldly Mastery Benefits Beings — From Relieving Poverty to Teaching the Undistorted Path of Higher Rebirth

The treatise now closes the seventh aspect with a tenfold restatement of exactly how worldly mastery benefits sentient beings in practice. According with worldly discourse and custom; skillfully breaking the grip of poverty; causing every disturbance and disaster arising from the elements or from non-human and human harm to cease; bestowing blameless amusements and diversions while removing unlawful ones; providing, with minimal effort, whatever dwelling and livelihood-necessities beings genuinely seek; rescuing beings oppressed by kings or by bandits; skillfully permitting what is fitting and restraining what is not among various undertakings; correctly establishing what is auspicious and inauspicious so that beings know what to accept and what to decline; correctly encouraging beings, in the present life, not to plunder or scheme against one another; and proclaiming, for the world to come, the undistorted path of higher rebirth — one should understand this as the brief meaning of the crafts and branches of learning that benefit sentient beings.

Chinese Source

酉二、明略義　隨順世間言說事故；為欲方便壞貧窮故；為令世間諸界錯亂、人非人等所起災患皆息滅故；為施無罪諸戲樂具，除彼非法諸戲樂故；諸有希求種種居處資生具者，為少用功皆能施與種種居處資生具故；為欲拔濟諸王賊等逼惱事故；為欲開制是處非處諸加行故；為欲安立吉非吉事，令取捨故；為正勸獎，於現法中令其展轉不相謀掠，及為宣說當來無倒勝生道故。當知是名能益有情工業明處略所說義。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, clarifying the brief meaning. According with worldly discourse and custom; skillfully breaking the grip of poverty; causing the disturbances and disasters arising from disordered elements, and from non-human and human harm, all to cease; bestowing blameless amusements while removing unlawful ones; for those who seek various dwellings and livelihood-necessities, providing these with minimal effort; rescuing beings from oppression by kings or bandits; skillfully permitting or restraining various undertakings as fitting; correctly establishing what is auspicious and inauspicious, so that beings know what to accept and decline; correctly encouraging beings, in the present life, not to plunder or scheme against one another; and proclaiming, for the world to come, the undistorted path of higher rebirth. One should understand this as the brief meaning of the crafts and branches of learning that benefit sentient beings.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this tenfold list as a genuinely comprehensive, practical catalogue of how worldly competence serves compassion in concrete terms — not abstract benevolence, but specific relief from specific hardships: poverty broken through teaching the causes of prosperity such as generosity; disasters from disordered elements (drought, disease, madness) or from human and non-human harm alike (violence, theft, malevolent spirits) actively addressed; wholesome recreation encouraged in place of unlawful vice such as gambling; housing and basic necessities provided efficiently to genuine seekers; victims of royal or criminal oppression actively rescued; conduct correctly sorted into permitted and forbidden; even traditional divination put to wholesome use, guiding beings toward what is genuinely auspicious — chief among auspicious acts, reverence toward the three jewels themselves; beings discouraged from preying on one another in the present life; and, finally, the deeper teaching of an undistorted path toward higher rebirth and ultimate liberation once trust has been established through all the foregoing.

He notes this list moves deliberately from the most immediate, material benefits toward the most ultimate, spiritual ones — worldly aid is never the endpoint, but the necessary first step that makes the deeper teaching possible at all. With the seventh aspect now fully closed, he notes, the treatise turns next to the eighth aspect: purified wholesome roots, given here with two further, escalated similes.

48. Milestone: Gold Now Set With Agate, and Wisdom Like Sun and Moon Moving in Harmony With the Very Wind That Cannot Outshine Them

The treatise now explains the eighth aspect in two parts, each with its own simile. First, the general account, pointing back to what was already explained: everything else regarding purified wholesome roots follows exactly as at the abode of utmost joy — broadly beholding the buddhas, wholesome roots purified — but now with a simile marking the difference: just as a skillful craftsman's own smelted gold, wrought into an ornament and further set with agate for brilliant adornment, cannot be equaled by any other gold lacking such adornment, so too this bodhisattva's own wholesome roots cannot be outshone by any hearer, solitary-realizer, or bodhisattva of a lesser ground. Second, a further simile specifically for the faculty of wisdom: just as the light of the sun, moon, and constellations cannot be outshone by any wheel of wind, and yet these same heavenly bodies continue to revolve in harmony with that very wind's own direction, so too this bodhisattva's own wondrous wisdom cannot be outshone by any hearer or solitary-realizer, and yet its undertakings proceed in harmony with the ordinary world — genuinely unstained even while genuinely engaged, dust of the world touched yet never soiling, awakening itself found within that same dust.

Chinese Source

申八、善根清淨　酉一、總辨善根　戌一、指同前　其餘一切，如前應知。

戌二、喻差別　此差別者，謂如世間善巧工匠，以所鍊金作莊嚴具，牟娑羅寶瑩飾廁鈿，所有餘金無與等故，不能映奪。如是此中菩薩善根，一切聲聞及諸獨覺、餘地菩薩不能映奪。

酉二、別喻慧根　又如日月諸宿光明，一切風輪不能映奪，然其迴轉共彼風同。如是此中菩薩妙慧，一切聲聞、諸獨覺等不能映奪，然其所作與世共同。

Ru-Yi Translation

Eighth aspect: purified wholesome roots; first, the general account; first, pointing to what was already said. Everything else follows exactly as already explained.

Second, a simile marking the difference. The difference is this: just as, in the world, a skillful craftsman's own smelted gold, wrought into an ornament and set with agate for brilliant adornment, cannot be equaled by any other gold lacking such adornment — so too this bodhisattva's own wholesome roots cannot be outshone by any hearer, solitary-realizer, or bodhisattva of a lesser ground.

Second, a further simile specifically for the faculty of wisdom. Just as the light of the sun, moon, and constellations cannot be outshone by any wheel of wind, and yet these continue to revolve in harmony with that very wind — so too this bodhisattva's own wondrous wisdom cannot be outshone by any hearer or solitary-realizer, and yet its undertakings proceed in harmony with the world.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi traces the simile's own escalation once more: raw ore purified by fire (first ground), gold refined further in alum (second), gold weighed and found exactly equal (third), gold wrought into an ornament (fourth), and now, at the fifth, that same ornament further adorned with agate — each ground adding one further degree of manifest excellence to gold already genuinely pure.

He treats the second simile as capturing precisely this ground's own distinctive achievement: mastering worldly crafts (the seventh aspect) while remaining genuinely unstained by worldly concern — sun, moon, and stars move together with the wind's own direction, yet the wind itself can never dim or extinguish their own independent light, exactly as a fifth-ground bodhisattva's engagement with worldly affairs never compromises the wisdom underlying that engagement. This is the classical image of being "in the dust yet not soiled by it," awakening found precisely within ordinary worldly activity rather than apart from it — a fitting culmination, he notes, of a ground whose entire seventh aspect was devoted to mastering the very worldly crafts this simile now shows to leave wisdom entirely untouched. Having explained purified wholesome roots through both similes, he notes, the treatise turns next to the ninth and tenth aspects: rebirth and majestic power.

49. Milestone: Rebirth as Lord of the Tuṣita Heaven, and Majestic Power Reaching a Thousand Koṭi

The treatise now explains the ninth and tenth aspects. The ninth, rebirth: such a bodhisattva, taking rebirth as a householder, most often becomes the lord of the Tuṣita heaven, skillfully transforming sentient beings there, causing them to abandon every kind of non-Buddhist, wrongful teaching. The tenth, majestic power: whatever majestic power was already described at the previous abode as reaching a koṭi of each kind, one should understand that here it reaches a thousand koṭi instead — a thousandfold increase over the fourth ground's own already vast scale, the same twelvefold catalogue of attainments continuing to multiply at each successive ground.

Chinese Source

申九、受生　受生多作覩史多天王，善化有情，令捨一切外道邪法。

申十、威力　所有威力，當知此說千俱胝數。

Ru-Yi Translation

Ninth aspect: rebirth. Taking rebirth, one most often becomes the lord of the Tuṣita heaven, skillfully transforming sentient beings, causing them to abandon every kind of non-Buddhist, wrongful teaching.

Tenth aspect: majestic power. One should understand that the majestic power spoken of here reaches a thousand koṭi.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi notes the continuing ascent of rebirth's own worldly rank: iron-wheel king, gold-wheel king, Śakra, the lord of Suyāma, and now the lord of Tuṣita — the very heaven, he notes, where a bodhisattva destined for buddhahood in one more lifetime is traditionally said to dwell, teaching there specifically to free beings from non-Buddhist error, an especially fitting mission given this ground's own signature mastery of the four noble truths against every rival, non-Buddhist claim to liberation.

He confirms the scaling now spanning five grounds: a hundred, a thousand, a hundred thousand, a koṭi, and now a thousand koṭi — ten billion — majestic power's own growth continuing to accelerate well beyond any simple fixed multiplier. Having explained rebirth and majestic power, closing all ten aspects, he notes, the treatise turns next to a concluding summary naming them together, followed by the cross-reference explaining why this ground is called, in the wider tradition, the ground hard to conquer.

50. Milestone: The Summary Names All Ten Aspects, and the Treatise Confirms — This Is the Ground Hard to Conquer

The treatise now closes the entire abode joined with the truths with a concluding summary and final cross-reference. In brief, this is called the bodhisattva's abode joined with the truths: because entry is brought to fulfillment through ten kinds of equally purified aspiration; because skillful means for observing the truths gradually increase; because destroying formations and compassion for sentient beings gradually increase; because, for this very purpose, vast provisions of merit and wisdom are cultivated, the mind diligently arousing a correct vow; because qualities of mindfulness, discernment, and conduct increase; because, with no remaining intention, every kind of skillful means diligently matures sentient beings; because worldly crafts and branches of learning are drawn forth; because of purified wholesome roots; because of rebirth; because of majestic power. Should one wish to hear this expounded at greater length, the treatise directs the reader to the Ten Grounds Sutra's own chapter on the ground hard to conquer — explaining that, within this ground, the bodhisattva's own decisive, wondrous wisdom regarding the noble truths is extremely difficult to surpass, and this is precisely why this ground is named the ground hard to conquer; and, by this very same meaning, one should understand this is exactly what is here called the abode joined with the truths.

Chinese Source

未二、結略義　當知是名略說菩薩諸諦相應增上慧住。謂十平等清淨意樂成滿得入故；善巧方便觀察諸諦漸增長故；毀壞諸行、悲愍有情漸增長故；即為是義，長養廣大福智資糧，心發正願勤加行故；念慧行等德增長故；無餘作意，以一切種成熟有情勤加行故；引發世間工巧業故；善根清淨故；受生故；威力故。

午二、廣指　未一、指　若廣宣說，如十地經極難勝地。

未二、釋　今此地中，顯示菩薩於諸聖諦決定妙智極難可勝，是故此地名極難勝。

未三、配　即由此義，應知此中諸諦相應增上慧住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, closing the brief meaning. One should understand this, in brief, as the bodhisattva's abode joined with the truths — namely, because entry is brought to fulfillment through ten kinds of equally purified aspiration; because skillful means for observing the truths gradually increase; because destroying formations and compassion for sentient beings gradually increase; because, for this very purpose, vast provisions of merit and wisdom are cultivated, the mind diligently arousing a correct vow; because qualities of mindfulness, discernment, and conduct increase; because, with no remaining intention, every kind of skillful means diligently matures sentient beings; because worldly crafts and branches of learning are drawn forth; because of purified wholesome roots; because of rebirth; because of majestic power.

Second, the broader pointer; first, pointing to it. Should one wish to hear it expounded at length, it is exactly as the Ten Grounds Sutra expounds it in its chapter on the ground hard to conquer.

Second, explaining. Within this ground, it is displayed that the bodhisattva's own decisive, wondrous wisdom regarding the noble truths is extremely difficult to surpass; this is why this ground is named the ground hard to conquer.

Third, the correspondence. By this very meaning, one should understand this is exactly the abode joined with the truths spoken of here.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi reviews all ten aspects as a final capstone: equally purified aspiration (past, present, future buddha-dharma, precept, mind, and wisdom purity, all equal); skillful observation of the four truths through ten aspects; compassion arising from seeing formations as empty and beings as deluded about past and future; vowing to gather merit and wisdom, expressed through mindful, discerning conduct; single-minded devotion to maturing beings with no remaining self-concerned intention; mastery of twelve worldly crafts deployed through ten concrete forms of benefit; purified wholesome roots (gold now set with agate, wisdom like sun and moon moving in harmony with an unstaining wind); rebirth as lord of Tuṣita; and majestic power reaching a thousand koṭi.

He explains why "hard to conquer" fits this ground so precisely: a bodhisattva here has achieved something genuinely difficult to combine — decisive, unshakeable wisdom regarding ultimate truth on the one hand, and full, unstained engagement with worldly affairs and worldly learning on the other, a combination rival teachings and even earlier grounds' own more exclusively contemplative focus cannot easily match. With this correspondence confirmed, the entire abode joined with the truths — the fifth ground, examined across all ten aspects — reaches its complete conclusion. The treatise turns next to the sixth of the eleven-part granular analysis's own next abode: the abode joined with dependent origination, the sixth ground and last of the three heightened-wisdom grounds, opening with eleven aspects of its own.

51. Milestone: The Abode Joined With Dependent Origination Opens — Ten Kinds of Equality Among All Dharmas

The treatise now opens the seventh abode: joined with dependent origination, corresponding to the sixth ground, the last of the three heightened-wisdom grounds and also called, in the wider tradition, the ground of direct presence. The question is posed: what is a bodhisattva's abode joined with dependent origination? The answer opens with the first of eleven aspects: entry brought to fulfillment through the equality of ten dharmas. Bodhisattvas, previously at the abode joined with the truths, have already attained ten kinds of equality among dharmas — the wording following exactly as the sutra explains at length. The commentary supplies the Ten Grounds Sutra's own full list: the equality of all dharmas being without mark; without conception; unarisen; unaccomplished; quiescent; originally pure; free of conceptual proliferation; free of grasping and rejecting; like an illusion, a dream, a shadow, an echo, the moon in water, a reflection in a mirror, a mirage, and a conjured apparition; and being neither existent nor non-existent — a tenfold contemplation the Ten Grounds Sutra's own commentary explains item by item, to be understood accordingly.

Chinese Source

辰七、緣起相應增上慧住　巳一、問　云何菩薩緣起相應增上慧住？

巳二、答　午一、略說　未一、別辨相　申一、十法平等性成滿得入　酉一、指經說　謂諸菩薩，先於諸諦相應增上慧住，已得十種法平等性，當知文辭如經廣說。

《披》已得十種法平等性等者：十地經說：若菩薩已善具足第五地道，欲入第六菩薩地，當以十平等法，得入第六地。何等為十？一者、一切法無相平等故；二者、一切法無想平等故；三者、一切法無生平等故；四、一切法無成平等故；五、一切法寂靜平等故；六、一切法本淨平等故；七、一切法無戲論平等故；八、一切法無取捨平等故；九、一切法如幻、夢、影、響、水中月、鏡中像、焰、化平等故；十、一切法有無不二平等故。論一一釋，此應準知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Seventh abode, joined with dependent origination; first, the question. What is a bodhisattva's abode joined with dependent origination?

Second, the answer; first, the brief account; first, distinguishing each mark; first, entry brought to fulfillment through the equality of ten dharmas; first, pointing to what the sutra says. Namely, bodhisattvas, previously at the abode joined with the truths, have already attained ten kinds of equality among dharmas; the wording follows exactly as the sutra explains at length.

Commentary: Regarding "ten kinds of equality among dharmas" — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: if a bodhisattva, having well accomplished the path of the fifth ground, wishes to enter the sixth bodhisattva ground, should enter by ten kinds of equal dharma. What are the ten? First, the equality of all dharmas being without mark. Second, being without conception. Third, being unarisen. Fourth, being unaccomplished. Fifth, being quiescent. Sixth, being originally pure. Seventh, being free of conceptual proliferation. Eighth, being free of grasping and rejecting. Ninth, being like an illusion, a dream, a shadow, an echo, the moon in water, a reflection in a mirror, a mirage, and a conjured apparition. Tenth, being neither existent nor non-existent. The treatise explains each individually; this should be understood accordingly.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi previews the sixth ground's own governing theme: where the fourth ground took the thirty-seven factors of awakening as its lens and the fifth ground took the four noble truths, this sixth and final heightened-wisdom ground takes dependent origination itself as its lens — the very principle explaining why anything arises at all, and why, correctly understood, nothing ultimately does. He notes the ground's alternate name in the wider tradition, the ground of direct presence, anticipating that the treatise will explain this name once the ground's own eleven aspects are complete.

He gives a preliminary gloss on the ten equalities before their detailed unpacking to come: dharmas are without mark because ultimate truth transcends every conceptual mark, whether characterized as conditioned or unconditioned; without conception because every mental image formed through conceptual grasping is itself ungraspable; unarisen and unaccomplished because nothing arises from causes alone without the full concurrence of conditions, and dependent arising is therefore no true arising at all; quiescent and originally pure because, lacking any real self-nature to begin with, dharmas were never actually disturbed nor defiled in the first place; free of conceptual proliferation because the appearance of substantial entities dissolves entirely once non-discriminating wisdom is genuinely present; free of grasping and rejecting because neither saṃsāra nor nirvana admits of increase or decrease from the standpoint of ultimate truth; illusion-like because every conditioned phenomenon, however vividly it appears, has no more substance than a dream, an echo, or the moon reflected in water; and neither existent nor non-existent because conventional, dependently-arisen existence is not equivalent to substantial existence, yet nor is it equivalent to utter non-existence — a genuine middle way between the two extremes. Having named all ten equalities, he notes, the treatise turns next to explaining how their fulfillment brings entry into the sixth ground, followed by a fuller unpacking of each in turn.

52. Milestone: Entry Confirmed, and All Ten Equalities Unpacked in Full — Closing the First Aspect

The treatise now explains how entry into the sixth ground is reached, then unpacks each of the ten equalities in turn, closing the first aspect entirely. These ten kinds of equality among dharmas, becoming of the most excellent grade, utterly complete, cause one to pass beyond the previous abode and enter this one. Restating the brief meaning: regarding all dharmas, by their own nature possessing ultimate truth, they are without mark; being mere images constructed through speech, they are without conception; by this very markless nature, they naturally do not arise; their cause, too, does not arise; because neither nature nor cause arises, they are, in the final analysis, originally quiescent; though appearing to possess substantial matters, the wisdom that genuinely grasps this is free of conceptual proliferation; entirely departed from grasping and rejecting; the very afflictive suffering that defiles is itself equal to the liberation that unbinds; the nature of whatever object discriminating attachment grasps at is illusion-like; and the very sphere non-discriminating wisdom operates within is, in its own nature, without duality between existence and non-existence — this, one should understand, is the brief, distinguishing meaning of these ten kinds of equality among dharmas here.

Chinese Source

酉二、明得入　如是十種法平等性成上品故，極圓滿故，超過前住得入此住。

酉三、釋略義　謂於一切法，由有勝義自性無相平等性故；言說造作影像無相平等性故；即由此相自然不生平等性故；因亦不生平等性故；自然與因皆不生故，畢竟本寂平等性故；現有體事能取正智，離諸戲論平等性故；遠離一切取捨造作平等性故；即此煩惱眾苦雜染離繫解脫平等性故；分別所執境界自性如幻化等平等性故；無分別智所行自性有無無二平等性故。當知是名此中十種法平等性略分別義。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, clarifying entry. These ten kinds of equality among dharmas, becoming of the most excellent grade, utterly complete, cause one to pass beyond the previous abode and enter this one.

Third, clarifying the brief meaning. Regarding all dharmas: by their own nature possessing ultimate truth, they are equally without mark; being mere images constructed through speech, they are equally without conception; by this very markless nature, they naturally do not arise, equally; their cause, too, does not arise, equally; because neither their own nature nor their cause arises, they are, in the final analysis, originally quiescent, equally; though appearing to possess substantial matters, the wisdom genuinely grasping this is free of conceptual proliferation, equally; entirely departed from every grasping and rejecting, equally; the very afflictive suffering that defiles is equal to the very liberation that unbinds; the nature of whatever object discriminating attachment grasps is illusion-like, equally; and the very sphere non-discriminating wisdom operates within is, in its own nature, without duality between existence and non-existence, equally. One should understand this as the brief, distinguishing meaning of these ten kinds of equality among dharmas here.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through this restatement as essentially the same ten equalities given a second, more explanatory pass: markless because ultimate wisdom's own object transcends every mark entirely; conception-free because thought itself, once it stirs, always produces some image, and every such image is empty the moment thought itself is seen through; unarisen twice over, first regarding the dharma's own nature and then regarding even its cause (a seed alone, without the concurring conditions of sunlight, water, and soil, produces nothing), hence "originally quiescent" as the natural conclusion; conceptual-proliferation-free specifically at the moment root wisdom directly manifests, sense-faculty, sense-object, and consciousness alike no longer appearing as separately real; and grasping-rejecting-free because a bodhisattva neither clings to saṃsāra nor flees to nirvana, the two ultimately equal.

He highlights the especially striking phrase equating "afflictive suffering that defiles" directly with "liberation that unbinds" — not that affliction and liberation are identical in ordinary experience, but that, from the standpoint of ultimate truth, grasping (which produces the appearance of affliction) and its release (which produces the appearance of liberation) are themselves equally empty of substantial reality, neither one more "real" than the other. With entry now confirmed and all ten equalities unpacked in full, closing the first of eleven aspects, he notes, the treatise turns next to the second aspect: awakening to dependent origination itself and, from this awakening, the arising of the three gates of liberation.

53. Milestone: Awakening to Dependent Origination Gives Rise to the Three Gates of Liberation — Emptiness, Signlessness, Wishlessness

The treatise now explains the second aspect: such a bodhisattva, abiding in this abode, increases compassion toward sentient beings, arouses intense eagerness and longing for great awakening, and, regarding the world's own coming-together and dispersing, arising and ceasing, correctly observes and genuinely understands this through every kind of correct contemplation of dependent origination — contemplating both the forward flow (why suffering arises, from ignorance through conditioned formations all the way to aging and death) and the reverse flow (why aging and death cease, because birth ceases; why birth ceases, because existence ceases, and so on back to ignorance itself). Relying on this same wisdom of dependent origination, three gates of liberation are drawn forth: observing the twofold selflessness of persons and dharmas gives rise to the gate of emptiness; observing that each link of dependent origination can, in reverse, be brought to cessation without remainder gives rise to the gate of signlessness; and observing that this same cyclic flow generates no wish to remain within it — solely great compassion preventing total abandonment of sentient beings — gives rise to the gate of wishlessness, these three gates belonging exclusively to what is world-transcending.

Chinese Source

申二、覺悟緣起生解脫門　如是菩薩住此住中，於諸有情增長悲愍；於大菩提生起猛利欲樂希求；於諸世間合散生滅，以一切種緣起正觀觀察了知。依緣起智，能引發空、無相、無願三解脫門。

《披》於諸世間合散生滅等者：有情自體，是名世間。煩惱繫縛眾緣和合，是名為合，合故有生。若彼煩惱眾緣斷壞，是名為散，散故永滅。於此一切，依緣起支順逆觀察，得如實智，名以一切種緣起正觀觀察了知。由觀緣起二無我性，是故能引空解脫門。觀彼有支一一還滅，是故能引無相解脫門。觀彼流轉不生願樂，唯除大悲不捨眾生，是故能引無願解脫門。此解脫門唯出世攝，如顯揚論別釋其相應知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second aspect: awakening to dependent origination, giving rise to the gates of liberation. Such a bodhisattva, abiding in this abode, increases compassion toward sentient beings; arouses intense eagerness and longing for great awakening; and, regarding the world's own coming-together and dispersing, arising and ceasing, correctly observes and genuinely understands this through every kind of correct contemplation of dependent origination. Relying on this wisdom of dependent origination, one is able to draw forth the three gates of liberation: emptiness, signlessness, and wishlessness.

Commentary: Regarding "the world's own coming-together and dispersing, arising and ceasing" — a sentient being's own embodied existence is called the world. Affliction's own binding conditions converging is called coming-together, and from this coming-together, birth occurs. Should those same afflictive conditions be broken apart, this is called dispersing, and from this dispersing, permanent cessation occurs. Regarding all of this, observing forward and backward through the links of dependent origination, one attains genuine wisdom — this is called correct contemplation of dependent origination through every kind. Because one observes the two kinds of selflessness through dependent origination, one is thereby able to draw forth the gate of emptiness. Because one observes each link, one by one, brought to cessation, one is thereby able to draw forth the gate of signlessness. Because one observes this cyclic flow generating no wish to remain, except solely for great compassion's refusal to abandon sentient beings, one is thereby able to draw forth the gate of wishlessness. These gates of liberation belong exclusively to what is world-transcending, as explained individually in the Compendium Establishing the Teaching, one should understand.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats the twelve links of dependent origination — ignorance conditioning volitional formations, conditioning consciousness, name-and-form, the six sense-bases, contact, feeling, craving, grasping, existence, birth, and aging-and-death — as the single thread running through this entire ground: forward contemplation explains why suffering arises at all, reverse contemplation explains how it can be brought to cessation, and both together yield the three gates of liberation as their direct fruit. He explains why compassion and longing for awakening are named first, before the contemplation itself is even described: a bodhisattva's motivation for undertaking this contemplation at all is precisely compassion for beings caught in this very cycle, together with longing to reach the awakening capable of freeing them.

He unpacks each gate's own distinct origin: the gate of emptiness arises from observing that neither a substantial person nor a substantial dharma can be found anywhere within the twelve links, only a conditioned, empty process; the gate of signlessness arises from observing that every one of the twelve links can, without exception, be brought to cessation, leaving no mark of any of them behind; and the gate of wishlessness arises from observing that this entire cyclic process generates, in a genuinely awakened mind, no wish whatsoever to remain within it — a bodhisattva's own continued presence within saṃsāra owing entirely to compassion overriding what would otherwise be complete disinclination to remain. Having explained how dependent origination gives rise to these three gates, he notes, the treatise turns next to the third aspect: how every wrongful notion, once these gates are opened, no longer manifests at all.

54. Milestone: No Notion of Self or Other, Agent or Patient, Existence or Non-Existence Ever Arises Again

The treatise now explains the third aspect: by this cause and condition, every notion of self and other, of agent and patient, and of existence and non-existence entirely ceases to turn. Having genuinely realized, through the three gates of liberation, that no substantial self, agent, or patient can be found within dependent origination's own unfolding, and that no fixed mark of existence or non-existence attaches to any of its links, such a bodhisattva's mind no longer generates the wrongful, defiling notions that would otherwise arise from mistaking this empty process for something substantial. The commentary explains that the sutra itself describes a bodhisattva practicing these three gates as thereby departing from the mark of self, departing from the mark of agent and patient, and departing from the mark of existence and non-existence — these three departures corresponding, in order, precisely to the three gates of liberation as their own direct cause and condition.

Chinese Source

申三、一切邪想皆不現行　由是因緣，所有自他、作者、受者、有無等想，皆不復轉。

《披》由是因緣等者：經說菩薩修行三解脫門，離彼我相，離作者、受者相，離有無相。如是離三種相，如其次第，三解脫門為其因緣，是故此中亦作是說。

Ru-Yi Translation

Third aspect: every wrongful notion no longer manifests. By this cause and condition, every notion of self and other, of agent and patient, and of existence and non-existence entirely ceases to turn.

Commentary: Regarding "by this cause and condition" and the like — the sutra says a bodhisattva practicing the three gates of liberation thereby departs from the mark of self, departs from the mark of agent and patient, and departs from the mark of existence and non-existence. These three departures, in their proper order, have the three gates of liberation as their own cause and condition; this is why it is said in this way here.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi maps the correspondence precisely: the gate of emptiness (no substantial self findable anywhere in the twelve links) yields departure from the mark of self; the gate of wishlessness (no wish to remain within the cyclic flow, since no one is actually flowing) yields departure from the mark of agent and patient — no one truly acting, no one truly experiencing the result; and the gate of signlessness (every link ultimately ceasing without a trace) yields departure from the mark of existence and non-existence, since neither extreme genuinely applies to a process this thoroughly empty of fixed characteristics.

He notes how demanding this actually is in practice — most people, even sincere practitioners, constantly generate exactly this kind of "I did this," "this happened to me," "this is real, that is unreal" thinking without ever noticing it as thinking at all; a sixth-ground bodhisattva's own sustained cultivation of these three gates has genuinely eliminated this habitual undercurrent, not merely suppressed it temporarily during formal meditation. Having explained the complete cessation of these wrongful notions, he notes, the treatise turns next to the fourth aspect: a bodhisattva's own reason for still taking up embodied existence within saṃsāra, out of genuine regard for sentient beings, despite having seen through it so thoroughly.

55. Milestone: Neither Contaminated by Saṃsāra Nor Abandoning It — Consciousness Itself Not Defiling, Yet Defiled

The treatise now explains the fourth aspect: such a bodhisattva, skilled in ultimate truth, regards sentient beings with genuine care. Genuinely penetrating that conditioned dharmas, bound by affliction and arising through the coming-together of conditions, are by their own nature weak and dependent — free of self and what belongs to self, yet turning under the sway of countless afflictive faults, and not otherwise arising apart from affliction's binding and the coming-together of conditions. The commentary explains "weak and dependent" two ways: form and non-form aggregates alike, each possessing its own particular generating cause, cannot arise from that cause alone but must await further conditions, hence weak; and further, consciousness itself, whose own nature is merely discerning awareness, cannot defile anything else by its own nature, yet becomes defiled once joined with defiling mental factors — hence likewise called weak, in the sense of being susceptible to defilement rather than causing it. Therefore, such a bodhisattva reflects: I should now guard myself, causing every affliction's binding and every coming-together of conditions to be entirely broken apart and destroyed — yet, for the sake of benefiting sentient beings, I should not permanently extinguish all conditioned existence.

Chinese Source

申四、顧念有情攝受生死　菩薩如是善於勝義顧念有情，如理通達煩惱繫故，緣和合故，有為諸法自性羸劣。離我我所無量過失汙染而轉；非離一切煩惱繫縛，眾緣和合。

《披》有為諸法自性羸劣等者：色非色蘊，是名有為諸法。各別生因，名彼自性。自不能生，待緣方生，是名羸劣。又復諸識，自性不能染他，然為他染，故名羸劣。由是當知，有為諸法無量過失汙染而轉，唯由一切煩惱繫縛、眾緣和合，不由我及我所而有所作，以我我所性無實故。

是故我今為自防護，應令一切煩惱繫縛、眾緣和合皆悉斷壞；為益有情，不應永滅一切有為。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fourth aspect: regarding sentient beings with genuine care, taking up saṃsāra for their sake. Such a bodhisattva, skilled in ultimate truth, regards sentient beings with genuine care. Genuinely penetrating that conditioned dharmas, bound by affliction and arising through the coming-together of conditions, are by their own nature weak and dependent. Free of self and what belongs to self, yet turning under countless afflictive faults; not otherwise arising apart from affliction's binding and the coming-together of conditions.

Commentary: Regarding "conditioned dharmas are by their own nature weak and dependent" — form and non-form aggregates are called conditioned dharmas. Each has its own particular generating cause, called its own nature. Unable to arise by itself, awaiting further conditions to arise — this is called weak. Furthermore, consciousness's own nature cannot defile another, and yet is defiled by another — this too is called weak. One should thereby understand that conditioned dharmas turn under countless afflictive faults solely through affliction's own binding and the coming-together of conditions, not through any self or what belongs to self actually doing anything, since self and what belongs to self have no real nature at all.

Therefore I should now guard myself, causing every affliction's binding and every coming-together of conditions to be entirely broken apart and destroyed. Yet, for the sake of benefiting sentient beings, I should not permanently extinguish all conditioned existence.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this fourth aspect as the treatise's own careful resolution of an apparent tension: a bodhisattva who has just seen through saṃsāra so thoroughly (aspects one through three) might seem to have every reason to abandon it entirely — yet precisely here, the treatise insists on both guarding against affliction's own binding conditions and refusing to extinguish conditioned existence altogether, compassion for beings overriding what would otherwise be complete withdrawal. He shares the account of a monastic who, asked what the entire Yogācārabhūmi-śāstra ultimately teaches, answered simply: correct, rational attention — precisely the antidote this passage points to, since afflictive conditions converge only when incorrect attention supplies the crucial catalyst; correct attention, applied consistently, prevents the very convergence that would otherwise give rise to affliction, regardless of whether the surrounding circumstance is itself pleasant or unpleasant.

He explains consciousness's own "weakness" carefully: consciousness itself, in its bare nature as mere awareness, is neither defiled nor defiling — it becomes defiled only when joined with defiling mental factors, exactly as clear water takes on whatever color is mixed into it without possessing that color inherently. A bodhisattva's own continued embodied existence, he notes, is therefore not a compromise or a failure to fully realize emptiness, but a deliberate choice: guarding against the causes of affliction while refusing the equally available option of simply exiting conditioned existence altogether, precisely because sentient beings still need help. Having explained this careful balance between guarding against affliction and remaining for beings' sake, he notes, the treatise turns next to the fifth aspect: the direct manifestation of unattached wisdom itself, dwelling as genuinely present.

56. Milestone: Unattached Wisdom Directly Present — Neither Existence Nor Non-Existence, the Non-Dual Wisdom Opens

The treatise now opens the fifth aspect: unattached wisdom's own abiding directly manifest. Such a bodhisattva, abiding in this abode, wisdom and compassion following one another closely, is named as one in whom unattached wisdom is directly present, and the abiding of the perfection of wisdom is directly present. The commentary explains "unattached wisdom directly present" precisely: this names the non-dual wisdom attained through directly realizing the equality of saṃsāra and nirvana — neither grasping at existence nor grasping at non-existence, both extremes entirely departed from, precisely what is meant by non-dual wisdom.

Chinese Source

申五、無著般若智住現前　酉一、標名　如是菩薩住此住中，智悲隨逐，名無著智現前、般若波羅蜜多住現前。

《披》名無著智現前者：謂於生死、涅槃平等，證得無二智故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fifth aspect: unattached wisdom's own abiding directly manifest; first, naming it. Such a bodhisattva, abiding in this abode, wisdom and compassion following one another closely, is named as one in whom unattached wisdom is directly present, and the abiding of the perfection of wisdom is directly present.

Commentary: Regarding "named as one in whom unattached wisdom is directly present" — this refers to directly realizing that saṃsāra and nirvana are equal, thereby attaining non-dual wisdom.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains why this ground is also called, in the wider tradition, the ground of direct presence — this fifth aspect's own name, "directly present," anticipating the treatise's own etymological confirmation still to come at this ground's close. He draws on the treatise's own earlier chapter on ultimate reality to unpack "non-dual wisdom" further: neither existence nor non-existence, both equally set aside — worldly, provisionally designated phenomena such as the five aggregates, and even the unconditioned nirvana itself, are ordinarily grasped as genuinely "existing," a grasping this wisdom sees through; yet the opposite extreme, denying that dependently-originated, illusion-like phenomena occur at all, is equally mistaken, an over-correction that empties out even the dependent and consummate natures the treatise elsewhere affirms.

He treats "wisdom and compassion following one another closely" as the key phrase binding this aspect to the one before it: precisely because compassion holds a bodhisattva within conditioned existence (the fourth aspect), and precisely because wisdom now directly realizes that existence and non-existence are both merely extremes to be set aside (this fifth aspect), the two together produce a way of abiding in the world that is neither naive realism nor nihilistic denial. Having named unattached wisdom's own direct presence, he notes, the treatise turns next to unpacking its actual characteristics in detail, across three further specific marks.

57. Milestone: Three Marks of Unattached Wisdom — Undefiled by the World, Sharp Forbearance, Abiding in Neither Extreme

The treatise now unpacks unattached wisdom's own three specific marks, closing the fifth aspect entirely. First, undefiled by the world: because of dwelling in this abode, one acts amid every kind of worldly activity without any defilement whatsoever, living and working alongside sentient beings while the mind remains entirely free of craving, hatred, and delusion. Second, possessing sharp forbearance: this same abode possesses a sharp, incisive forbearance — a preliminary form of the very forbearance the seventh ground will bring to its own applied-effort boundary, this sixth-ground forbearance serving as the direct, accordant cause preparing the way for that later attainment. Third, abiding in neither extreme: this unattached wisdom directly present, and the abiding of the perfection of wisdom directly present, are together able to draw forth every condition contributing to awakening; toward every worldly, conditioned activity, one abides without truly abiding — remaining, for beings' sake, yet never attached — and although beholding quiescence's own peaceful virtue in nirvana itself, one likewise does not abide there either.

Chinese Source

酉二、釋相　戌一、不染世間　由此住故，於一切世間行無染而行。

戌二、有猛利忍　又即此住有猛利忍。於第七地有加行行邊際菩薩忍，當知是彼隨順忍攝。

戌三、不住二邊　又此無著智現前、般若波羅蜜多住現前，能引能引菩提眾緣，於諸世間有為諸行住而不住；雖於寂滅見寂靜德，而亦不住。

《披》能引能引菩提眾緣者：菩提分法能滿大菩提果，是名能引菩提眾緣。今此無著智現前、般若波羅蜜多住現前，能引一切菩提分法，是故說言能引能引菩提眾緣。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, explaining the marks; first, undefiled by the world. Because of dwelling in this abode, one acts amid every kind of worldly activity without any defilement whatsoever.

Second, possessing sharp forbearance. This same abode also possesses sharp forbearance. Regarding the seventh ground's own applied-effort boundary bodhisattva-forbearance, one should understand this sixth-ground forbearance as subsumed within its accordant cause.

Third, abiding in neither extreme. This unattached wisdom directly present, and the abiding of the perfection of wisdom directly present, are able to draw forth every condition contributing to awakening. Toward every conditioned activity in the world, one abides without truly abiding; although beholding quiescence's own peaceful virtue, one likewise does not abide there.

Commentary: Regarding "able to draw forth every condition contributing to awakening" — the factors of awakening are able to fulfill the great fruit of awakening; this is called drawing forth every condition contributing to awakening. Now, unattached wisdom directly present and the abiding of the perfection of wisdom directly present are able to draw forth every one of the factors of awakening; this is why it is said, drawing forth every condition contributing to awakening.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the significant forward-reference embedded in the second mark: the seventh ground still to come will possess its own "applied-effort boundary forbearance" — the final, most refined form of effortful cultivation of emptiness before the eighth ground's spontaneous, effortless abiding begins — and this sixth ground's own sharp forbearance, though not yet identical to that seventh-ground attainment, is genuinely its accordant, preparatory cause, ripening directly into it. He treats this as one more instance of the treatise's own recurring pattern: each ground's own attainment is never wholly discontinuous with what follows, but a genuine seed maturing into the next stage's own fuller flower.

He treats the third mark as this aspect's own culminating synthesis: a bodhisattva at this ground neither clings to worldly, conditioned activity (despite continuing to engage in it for beings' sake) nor clings to nirvana's own peaceful quiescence (despite genuinely beholding its virtue) — abiding in neither extreme is precisely what "unattached" and "non-dual" wisdom, named two milestones earlier, actually looks like when lived out moment to moment. With all three marks of unattached wisdom now explained, closing the fifth aspect entirely, he notes, the treatise turns next to the sixth aspect: the immeasurable meditative absorptions this same wisdom is able to draw forth.

58. Milestone: Immeasurable Absorption — Hundreds and Thousands of Gates Into Emptiness, Wishlessness, and Signlessness

The treatise now explains the sixth aspect in two parts. First, citing the gate of emptiness absorption: such a bodhisattva, followed closely by skillful means and wisdom together, is able to enter the absorption of emptiness, causing ten, a hundred, a thousand foremost gates of absorption all to become directly present. Second, extending this by example to wishlessness and signlessness: exactly as with the absorption of emptiness, one should understand that entering the absorptions of wishlessness and signlessness proceeds in just the same way — the commentary specifying, via the Ten Grounds Sutra, that ten kinds of emptiness-absorption gates serve as the foremost among a hundred thousand such gates all becoming directly present, and that signlessness and wishlessness each likewise possess their own ten foremost gates opening onto a comparably vast further multitude, exactly as the sutra explains.

Chinese Source

申六、證無量定　酉一、舉空三摩地　如是菩薩方便般若智所隨逐，能入空三摩地，令十百千上首三摩地門皆現在前。

酉二、例無願無相　如空三摩地，如是無願、無相三摩地，當知亦爾。

《披》令十百千上首三摩地門等者：經說十種空三摩地門為其上首，百千萬空三摩地門皆現在前。準彼應釋。下說無相、無願各復有十為其上首，如經應知。
