Ru-Yi Translation

Sixth aspect: realizing immeasurable absorption; first, citing the absorption of emptiness. Such a bodhisattva, followed closely by skillful means and wisdom, is able to enter the absorption of emptiness, causing ten, a hundred, a thousand foremost gates of absorption all to become directly present.

Second, extending by example to wishlessness and signlessness. Exactly as with the absorption of emptiness, one should understand that entering the absorptions of wishlessness and signlessness proceeds in just the same way.

Commentary: Regarding "causing ten, a hundred, a thousand foremost gates of absorption" — the sutra says ten kinds of emptiness-absorption gates serve as the foremost, a hundred thousand myriad emptiness-absorption gates all becoming directly present. This should be explained accordingly. Below, it says signlessness and wishlessness each further possess their own ten as foremost, exactly as the sutra explains, one should understand.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this aspect as the direct meditative flowering of the three gates of liberation already introduced several milestones earlier: what began as three conceptual gates into liberation (emptiness, signlessness, wishlessness) now becomes, at this ground, an actually cultivated absorption, each gate opening onto not merely one approach but a genuinely vast multitude — ten serving as foremost among a hundred thousand, the treatise's own way of conveying a scope no simple count could capture. He notes that a bodhisattva's own capacity to enter samādhi through countless different objects — sensory objects, even ordinary worldly appearances such as human or animal forms — reflects precisely this vastness, a single underlying realization of emptiness expressible through an effectively unlimited range of specific meditative approaches.

Having explained the immeasurable absorptions this ground's own wisdom draws forth, he notes, the treatise turns next to the seventh aspect: the resulting unshakeable aspiration these same absorptions secure.

59. Milestone: Nine Kinds of Unshakeable Aspiration — From Unshakeable Faith to a Mind Fully Satisfied With Skillful Means

The treatise now explains the seventh aspect: because these foremost gates of absorption are now directly present, aspiration itself becomes unshakeable. The commentary specifies nine distinct kinds of this same unshakeable aspiration, drawn from the sutra: unshakeable faith and outlook, decisive confidence in the buddha-dharma never faltering; an unshakeable capacity for gentleness, afflictions subdued so thoroughly that any hostility from others can be genuinely borne; an unshakeable profundity of mind, no fear arising even before the buddhas' own most profound wisdom; an unshakeable, irreversible conduct, never wavering from the Mahāyāna; an unshakeable, unresting diligence, absorption itself requiring little sleep and sustaining continuous effort; an unshakeable purity of mind, entirely free of envy and free of any stain of broken precepts; an unshakeable, boundless mind, wishing to benefit every sentient being without limit; an unshakeable mind seeking ever-higher wisdom, continually aspiring toward liberation more excellent still; and an unshakeable mind of skillful accomplishment, fully satisfied in the capacity to bring beings to maturity.

Chinese Source

申七、證不壞意樂　由此上首三摩地門現在前故，意樂不壞。

《披》意樂不壞者：謂彼意樂堅固不退，是名不壞。此有九種差別，如經應知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Seventh aspect: realizing unshakeable aspiration. Because these foremost gates of absorption are now directly present, aspiration itself becomes unshakeable.

Commentary: Regarding "aspiration becomes unshakeable" — this means that aspiration is firm and does not regress; this is called unshakeable. This has nine distinct kinds, exactly as the sutra explains, one should understand.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi lists all nine kinds of unshakeable aspiration the Ten Grounds Sutra names, each traced to its own governing mind: unshakeable faith and outlook (decisive, unwavering confidence in the buddha-dharma); an unshakeable capacity for gentleness (afflictions subdued, able to bear any hostility, called a genuine mind); an unshakeable depth of mind before the buddhas' own profound wisdom, called a deep mind — no fear or alarm arising even confronting teachings a bodhisattva has not yet fully realized, since genuine samādhi already gives real confidence such realization lies within reach; an unshakeable, irreversible conduct toward the Mahāyāna, called non-regressing conduct; unshakeable, unresting diligence, called a mind without rest, absorption's own power reducing the ordinary need for sleep and food alike; an unshakeable purity free of both envy and any stain of broken precepts, called a pure mind — genuine accomplishment leaves no room for envy of others' own good fortune; an unshakeable, boundless mind wishing to benefit every sentient being without exception, called a boundless mind; an unshakeable mind continually seeking wisdom more excellent still, called a mind seeking wisdom, advancing from this sixth ground toward the seventh and ultimately toward buddhahood itself; and an unshakeable mind of skillful accomplishment in maturing beings, called a mind satisfied in skillful, wise conduct.

He treats this ninefold unshakeability as the direct fruit of the immeasurable absorptions just described — meditative attainment this vast naturally secures aspiration against every kind of regression, doubt, or fear that might otherwise arise. Having explained all nine kinds of unshakeable aspiration, he notes, the treatise turns next to the eighth aspect: this same aspiration proving entirely beyond the reach of demonic or hostile forces.

60. Milestone: Demonic Forces and Rival Teachings Cannot Wrest Away This Sovereign Mastery

The treatise now explains the eighth aspect: regarding every kind of the buddhas' own noble teaching, every non-Buddhist path, every kind of demonic force, and every hostile power opposed to the noble teaching are entirely unable to seize or wrest it away. Having attained deep contemplative insight into dependent origination, and with mastery now genuinely sovereign over the buddhas' own noble teaching in every one of its kinds, such a bodhisattva finds every wrong view arising from the sixty-two views rooted in self-grasping, together with the demons of the five aggregates, affliction, death, and heaven itself, and every hostile power opposed to the noble teaching, powerless to disturb this same aspiration or wrest away this same wisdom.

Chinese Source

申八、魔怨無奪　於一切種諸佛聖教，一切外道及諸魔軍、聖教怨敵，不能引奪。

Ru-Yi Translation

Eighth aspect: demonic and hostile forces unable to wrest it away. Regarding every kind of the buddhas' own noble teaching, every non-Buddhist path, every kind of demonic force, and every hostile power opposed to the noble teaching, are entirely unable to seize or wrest it away.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this eighth aspect as the sutra's own named "mastery of sovereign power" — a sixth-ground bodhisattva's deep contemplation of dependent origination has produced genuine sovereignty over the buddhas' own teaching in every kind, a mastery so thorough that the sixty-two wrong views rooted in self-grasping, together with every classical demon, simply have nothing left to seize hold of.

He notes the brevity of this aspect, mirroring the equally brief tenth aspect at the fourth ground and matching, once again, the treatise's own recurring pattern: interior cultivation (aspects one through seven) naturally yields imperviousness to external challenge, requiring little further elaboration once genuinely established. Having explained this imperviousness to demonic and rival forces, he notes, the treatise turns next to the ninth aspect: purified wholesome roots, given once more with two escalated similes.

61. Milestone: Gold Now Set With Lapis Lazuli, and Wisdom Like Moonlight No Wind of the Four Seasons Can Extinguish

The treatise now explains the ninth aspect in two parts. First, the general account, pointing back to what was already said: everything else follows exactly as before. Second, a simile marking the difference: just as a skillful craftsman's own smelted gold, wrought into an ornament and set with brilliant lapis lazuli — surpassing even the previous ground's agate — cannot be equaled by any other gold lacking such adornment, so too this bodhisattva's own purified wholesome roots, exactly as already described, cannot be outshone. A further simile specifically for the faculty of wisdom follows: just as moonlight brings genuine delight and coolness to sentient beings' own bodies, and cannot be broken apart by any of the four wheels of wind across the four seasons, so too this bodhisattva's own wisdom-light is able to extinguish the oppressive, stifling heat of affliction in every sentient being, and cannot be broken apart by any non-Buddhist teaching, demonic force, or hostile power whatsoever.

Chinese Source

申九、善根清淨　酉一、總辨善根　戌一、指同前　餘如前說。

戌二、喻差別　此差別者，謂如世間善巧工匠，以所鍊金作莊嚴具，琉璃寶珠瑩飾廁鈿，一切餘金不能映奪。如是此中菩薩善根清淨殊勝，如先所說不能映奪。

酉二、別喻慧根　又如月光，於有情身能令悅豫，非四風輪所能斷壞。如是此中菩薩慧光，一切有情煩惱鬱蒸皆能息滅，一切外道、魔軍、怨敵不能斷壞。

Ru-Yi Translation

Ninth aspect: purified wholesome roots; first, the general account; first, pointing to what was already said. Everything else follows exactly as already explained.

Second, a simile marking the difference. The difference is this: just as, in the world, a skillful craftsman's own smelted gold, wrought into an ornament and set with lapis lazuli for brilliant adornment, cannot be equaled by any other gold lacking such adornment — so too this bodhisattva's own purified, excellent wholesome roots, exactly as already described, cannot be outshone.

Second, a further simile specifically for the faculty of wisdom. Just as moonlight brings genuine delight to sentient beings' own bodies, and cannot be broken apart by any of the four wheels of wind — so too this bodhisattva's own wisdom-light is able to extinguish the oppressive, stifling heat of affliction in every sentient being, and cannot be broken apart by any non-Buddhist teaching, demonic force, or hostile power.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi traces the simile's own steady escalation across six grounds: raw ore purified by fire, gold refined further in alum, gold weighed and found exactly equal, gold wrought into an ornament, that ornament set with agate, and now, at the sixth ground, set with lapis lazuli — each successive gemstone conventionally regarded as more precious than the last, tracking each ground's own further degree of manifest excellence.

He explains the four wheels of wind as a traditional seasonal image: spring's gentle breeze representing the affliction-demon, summer's scorching wind representing the aggregate-demon, autumn's cool wind representing the death-demon, and winter's cold wind representing the heaven-demon — moonlight, cool and soothing, remains undisturbed by any of these four, exactly as a sixth-ground bodhisattva's own wisdom-light remains undisturbed by any of the four classical demons, while actively cooling the "stifling heat" of affliction in beings it touches. Having explained purified wholesome roots through both similes, he notes, the treatise turns next to the tenth and eleventh aspects: rebirth and majestic power.

62. Milestone: Rebirth as Lord of the Heaven of Manifest Delight, and Threefold Majestic Power Reaching a Hundred Thousand Koṭi

The treatise now explains the tenth and eleventh aspects. The tenth, rebirth: such a bodhisattva, taking rebirth as a householder, most often becomes the lord of the Heaven of Manifest Delight — the fifth of the six desire-realm heavens, where the gods conjure their own objects of enjoyment — skillfully transforming sentient beings there, causing them to eliminate every kind of spiritual overconfidence and comparable fault. The eleventh, majestic power: such power is now said to reach a hundred thousand koṭi — a hundredfold increase over the fifth ground's own thousand-koṭi scale — and is here explained as threefold in nature: noble power (the six spiritual super-knowledges, indispensable for reaching and understanding beings efficiently); dharma-power (the six perfections, each carrying its own distinct capacity — generosity dispelling miserliness and securing the very wealth needed to aid others, discipline adorning the body so that its dignity alone inspires reverence, and patience, diligence, meditation, and wisdom each equally potent in its own right); and innate power (power simply arising together with a bodhisattva's own birth, culminating, at buddhahood itself, in even senseless things — trees bending to offer shade, rough ground leveling underfoot — spontaneously honoring the buddha's own presence).

Chinese Source

申十、受生　受生多作妙化天王，善化有情，令除一切增上慢等。

申十一、威力　所有威力，當知此中說百千俱胝數。

Ru-Yi Translation

Tenth aspect: rebirth. Taking rebirth, one most often becomes the lord of the Heaven of Manifest Delight, skillfully transforming sentient beings, causing them to eliminate every kind of spiritual overconfidence and comparable fault.

Eleventh aspect: majestic power. One should understand that the majestic power spoken of here reaches a hundred thousand koṭi.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi notes the continuing ascent of rebirth's own rank: iron-wheel king, gold-wheel king, Śakra, lord of Suyāma, lord of Tuṣita, and now the lord of the Heaven of Manifest Delight — beings there able to conjure their own objects of enjoyment at will, a heaven especially prone to the very spiritual overconfidence (mistaking mere concentrated bliss for genuine liberation) this bodhisattva's own teaching mission specifically targets, drawing directly on this ground's own hard-won freedom from precisely that fault.

He unpacks the threefold majestic power in detail, cross-referencing the treatise's own earlier chapter on majestic power: noble power (the six spiritual super-knowledges any bodhisattva needs to reach beings efficiently, reading their minds and needs directly); dharma-power (each of the six perfections carrying its own distinct capacity — a generous bodhisattva possesses the very wealth needed to help others; a disciplined bodhisattva's own bodily dignity alone can subdue onlookers without a word spoken; patience, diligence, meditation, and wisdom each equally indispensable); and innate power (power simply accompanying birth itself, culminating at full buddhahood in even inanimate nature spontaneously honoring the buddha's own presence — trees bending to offer shade, uneven ground leveling itself underfoot, every sentient and insentient thing alike participating in reverence). Having explained rebirth and this threefold majestic power now scaled to a hundred thousand koṭi, closing all eleven aspects, he notes, the treatise turns next to a concluding summary naming them together, followed by the cross-reference explaining why this ground is called, in the wider tradition, the ground of direct presence.

63. Milestone: The Summary Names All Eleven Aspects, and the Treatise Confirms — This Is the Ground of Direct Presence

The treatise now closes the entire abode joined with dependent origination with a concluding summary and final cross-reference. In brief, this is called the bodhisattva's abode joined with dependent origination: because entry is brought to fulfillment through the equality of ten dharmas; because awakening to dependent origination gives rise to the gates of liberation; because every wrongful notion no longer manifests; because saṃsāra is skillfully taken up for beings' sake; because unattached wisdom and the abiding of the perfection of wisdom are directly present; because immeasurable absorption is realized; because unshakeable aspiration is realized; because nothing can wrest away mastery of the buddhas' own noble teaching; because of broadly beholding the buddhas and purified wholesome roots; because of rebirth; because of majestic power. Should one wish to hear this expounded at greater length, the treatise directs the reader to the Ten Grounds Sutra's own chapter on the ground of direct presence — explaining that, within this ground, because unattached wisdom and the abiding of the perfection of wisdom are both directly present, this ground is named the ground of direct presence; and, by this very same meaning, one should understand this is exactly what is here called the abode joined with dependent origination.

Chinese Source

未二、結略義　當知是名略說菩薩緣起相應增上慧住。謂十法平等性成滿得入故；覺悟緣起生解脫門故；一切邪想不現行故；方便攝受生死故；無著智現前、般若波羅蜜多住現在前故；證得無量三摩地故；證得不壞意樂故；於佛聖教不可引奪故；廣見諸佛、善根清淨故；受生故；威力故。

午二、廣指　未一、指　若廣宣說，如十地經現前地說。

未二、釋　由此地中，無著智現前、般若波羅蜜多住現在前故，名現前地。

未三、配　即由此義，當知亦名緣起相應增上慧住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, closing the brief meaning. One should understand this, in brief, as the bodhisattva's abode joined with dependent origination — namely, because entry is brought to fulfillment through the equality of ten dharmas; because awakening to dependent origination gives rise to the gates of liberation; because every wrongful notion no longer manifests; because saṃsāra is skillfully taken up for beings' sake; because unattached wisdom and the abiding of the perfection of wisdom are directly present; because immeasurable absorption is realized; because unshakeable aspiration is realized; because nothing can wrest away mastery of the buddhas' own noble teaching; because of broadly beholding the buddhas and purified wholesome roots; because of rebirth; because of majestic power.

Second, the broader pointer; first, pointing to it. Should one wish to hear it expounded at length, it is exactly as the Ten Grounds Sutra expounds it in its chapter on the ground of direct presence.

Second, explaining. Within this ground, because unattached wisdom and the abiding of the perfection of wisdom are both directly present, this ground is named the ground of direct presence.

Third, the correspondence. By this very meaning, one should understand this is also named the abode joined with dependent origination.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi reviews all eleven aspects as a final capstone: ten equalities among dharmas; the three gates of liberation arising from dependent origination; every wrongful notion ceasing; compassion sustaining engagement with conditioned existence despite seeing through it; unattached, non-dual wisdom directly present in three marks; immeasurable absorption through hundreds of thousands of gates; ninefold unshakeable aspiration; imperviousness to demonic and rival forces; purified wholesome roots (gold now set with lapis lazuli, wisdom like moonlight no wind can extinguish); rebirth as lord of the Heaven of Manifest Delight; and threefold majestic power (noble, dharma, and innate) now reaching a hundred thousand koṭi.

He treats the closing correspondence — ground of direct presence and abode joined with dependent origination naming the same underlying reality, unattached wisdom and the perfection of wisdom both now directly, continuously present rather than merely occasionally accessed — as marking the genuine completion of the three heightened-wisdom grounds together (the fourth through the thirty-seven factors of awakening, the fifth through the four noble truths, and this sixth through dependent origination itself). With this correspondence confirmed, the entire abode joined with dependent origination — the sixth ground, examined across all eleven aspects — reaches its complete conclusion. The treatise turns next to the eighth of the eleven-part granular analysis's own next abode: the abode with applied effort, having no further mark, the seventh ground, entering territory the treatise has already flagged as directly preparing for the eighth ground's own spontaneous, effortless abiding.

64. Milestone: The Abode With Applied Effort, Having No Further Mark, Opens — Ten Skillful Wisdoms, the First Five

The treatise now opens the eighth abode: with applied effort, having no further mark, corresponding to the seventh ground, also called the ground of going far. The question is posed: what is a bodhisattva's abode with applied effort, having no further mark? The answer opens with the first of twelve aspects: entry brought to fulfillment through uncommon, excellent conduct. Bodhisattvas, previously at the sixth ground joined with dependent origination, have already attained ten kinds of wondrous, skillful wisdom — the commentary supplying the Ten Grounds Sutra's own full list, arousing excellent conduct through ten skillful means: well cultivating emptiness, signlessness, and wishlessness while still gathering great, supportive merit for the path; entering the selflessness of persons, absence of a life-force, and absence of sentient beings, while never abandoning the four immeasurables; arousing meritorious dharmas and heightened perfection-conduct, while grasping at no dharma whatsoever; attaining departure from the three realms, while still manifesting conduct that adorns them; utterly quiescing the very blaze of affliction, while still arousing conduct that extinguishes craving, hatred, and delusion for every sentient being's sake.

Chinese Source

辰八、有加行有功用無相住　巳一、問　云何菩薩有加行有功用無相住？

巳二、答　午一、略說　未一、別辨相　申一、不共勝行成滿得入　酉一、明得入　謂諸菩薩，於前第六緣起相應增上慧住，已得十種妙方便慧。

《披》已得十種妙方便慧者：十地經說：若菩薩善具足六地行已，欲入第七菩薩地者，是菩薩當以十種方便智，發起殊勝行入。何等為十？所謂善修空、無相、無願，而集大功德助道。入諸法無我、無壽命、無眾生，而不捨起四無量。起功德法，作增上波羅蜜行，而無法可取。得遠離三界，而能應化起莊嚴三界行。畢竟寂滅諸煩惱焰，而能為一切眾生起滅貪瞋癡煩惱焰行。

Ru-Yi Translation

Eighth abode, with applied effort, having no further mark; first, the question. What is a bodhisattva's abode with applied effort, having no further mark?

Second, the answer; first, the brief account; first, distinguishing each mark; first, entry brought to fulfillment through uncommon, excellent conduct; first, clarifying entry. Namely, bodhisattvas, previously at the sixth abode joined with dependent origination, have already attained ten kinds of wondrous, skillful wisdom.

Commentary: Regarding "ten kinds of wondrous, skillful wisdom" — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: if a bodhisattva, having well accomplished the conduct of the sixth ground, wishes to enter the seventh bodhisattva ground, should arouse excellent conduct through ten skillful means. What are the ten? Namely: well cultivating emptiness, signlessness, and wishlessness, while gathering great, supportive merit for the path. Entering the selflessness of persons, absence of a life-force, absence of sentient beings, while never abandoning the arousal of the four immeasurables. Arousing meritorious dharmas, undertaking heightened perfection-conduct, while grasping at no dharma whatsoever. Attaining departure from the three realms, while still able to manifest conduct adorning the three realms. Utterly, quiescently extinguishing every blaze of affliction, while still able to arouse, for every sentient being's sake, conduct extinguishing the blaze of craving, hatred, and delusion.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the seventh ground's own governing tension, embedded directly in its name: this ground still requires applied effort — deliberate, sustained exertion — yet is already free of any further conceptual mark to strive after, standing at the very threshold where the eighth ground's own spontaneous, effortless conduct will finally begin. He explains that each of the ten skillful wisdoms follows the identical pattern: a profound realization of emptiness paired directly with continued, undiminished engaged conduct — never resolving into either extreme of nihilistic withdrawal or naive worldly attachment.

He walks through the first five: the first pairs the three gates of liberation, already mastered at the sixth ground, with continued accumulation of merit through the perfections, since emptiness alone, without merit, yields only meager fruit, while merit without emptiness's own wisdom remains attached and limited; the second pairs realized selflessness with undiminished cultivation of loving-kindness, compassion, sympathetic joy, and equanimity, compassion for beings never abandoned despite penetrating that no substantial being exists to be compassionate toward; the third pairs vast, active cultivation of every perfection with genuinely grasping at none of them, avoiding the trap of treating one's own spiritual accomplishments as substantial possessions; the fourth pairs genuine departure from the three realms with continued willing manifestation within them, since a bodhisattva who has genuinely transcended saṃsāra can nonetheless choose embodiment there for beings' sake; and the fifth pairs personal freedom from affliction's own blaze with actively teaching methods for extinguishing that same blaze in others, since a bodhisattva who has severed affliction can still skillfully manifest and address it in order to teach. Having explained the first five of ten skillful wisdoms, he notes, the treatise turns next to the remaining five.

65. Milestone: The Remaining Five Skillful Wisdoms — Illusion-Like Yet Active, Adorning Buddha-Lands, Manifesting Body, Voice, and Mind

The treatise now completes the ten skillful wisdoms. Sixth, according with illusion, dream, shadow, echo, conjured apparition, the moon in water, and a reflection in a mirror, their own nature non-dual, while still arousing an immeasurable variety of active, distinct undertakings. Seventh, well knowing that every buddha-land's own path is like space itself, while still arousing conduct that adorns and purifies buddha-lands. Eighth, knowing that the buddhas' own dharma-body is, by its own nature, without any body at all, while still arousing conduct adorning a form-body with excellent marks. Ninth, knowing that the buddhas' own voice is without sound, originally quiescent, its mark inexpressible, while still, according with every sentient being, arousing every kind of distinct, adorned voice. Tenth, entering the buddhas' own capacity to penetrate, within a single instant, the affairs of all three times, while still able to distinguish countless eon-spans of cultivation according to each and every sentient being's own distinct mind.

Chinese Source

隨順幻、夢、影、響、化、水中月、鏡中像，自性不二，而起作業無量差別心。善知一切國土道如虛空，而起莊嚴淨佛國土行。知諸佛法身自性無身，而起色身相好莊嚴行。知諸佛音聲無聲，本來寂滅，不可說相，而隨一切眾生起種種差別莊嚴音聲行。入諸佛於一念頃通達三世事，而能分別種種相劫數修行，隨一眾生心差別觀故。論一一釋，如彼應知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Sixth, according with illusion, dream, shadow, echo, conjured apparition, the moon in water, and a reflection in a mirror, their own nature non-dual, while still arousing an immeasurable variety of active, distinct minds. Seventh, well knowing that every buddha-land's own path is like empty space, while still arousing conduct that adorns and purifies buddha-lands. Eighth, knowing the buddhas' own dharma-body is, by its own nature, without any body, while still arousing conduct adorning a form-body with excellent marks. Ninth, knowing the buddhas' own voice is without sound, originally quiescent, its mark inexpressible, while still, according with every sentient being, arousing every kind of distinct, adorned voice. Tenth, entering the buddhas' own capacity to penetrate, within a single instant, the affairs of the three times, while still able to distinguish countless eon-spans of cultivation, according with each and every sentient being's own distinct mind. The treatise explains each individually; one should understand accordingly.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi continues the same paired pattern through the remaining five: the sixth pairs genuine realization that all conditioned phenomena are illusion-like — a dream's six destinies vanishing entirely upon waking, an echo answering with no one truly speaking, a reflection with no one truly present — with still actively performing countless deeds for beings' benefit, building what he calls a water-moon monastery amid empty-sky buddha-work; the seventh pairs knowing that every land is, at bottom, as empty as space with still actively adorning and purifying buddha-fields, addressing what might be called both outer and inner environmental purification; the eighth pairs knowing the dharma-body's own bodilessness (suchness itself has no location for a body to occupy) with still manifesting an excellent, adorned form so that beings can see, approach, and generate merit through reverence; the ninth pairs knowing the buddhas' own voice is fundamentally soundless — Vimalakīrti's own thunderous silence before the very question of non-duality being the classical illustration — with still teaching ceaselessly, the voice adapting itself to each listener's own preference exactly as each being present at a single teaching believes the buddha speaks to them alone; and the tenth pairs the buddhas' own instantaneous penetration of past, present, and future with the patient, particular work of discerning each individual being's own specific karmic history across countless eons.

He treats all ten together as displaying a single, unified structure: genuine emptiness realized to its very depths, paired at every point with sustained, undiminished, skillful engagement — neither extreme permitted to cancel the other. Having detailed all ten skillful wisdoms, he notes, the treatise turns next to how the Ten Grounds Sutra itself organizes them under four underlying kinds of excellence, before returning to the treatise's own conclusion confirming entry into the seventh ground.

66. Milestone: Four Underlying Excellences Gathering All Ten Wisdoms — Wealth, Guarding Against Evil, Guarding Wholesome Roots, and Gathering Beings

The treatise's commentary now explains how the Ten Grounds Sutra's own companion commentary gathers these same ten skillful wisdoms under four underlying kinds of excellence. First, excellence in wealth and body: able to freely obtain whatever is needed, corresponding to the first wisdom. Second, excellence in guarding against evil: never abandoning sentient beings, arousing the four immeasurables, corresponding to the second. Third, excellence in guarding wholesome roots: diligently cultivating correct conduct while grasping at not even the slightest dharma, corresponding to the third. Fourth, excellence in gathering sentient beings — itself further divided into seven distinct kinds, corresponding to the fourth through tenth wisdoms in turn: taking rebirth according to vow in order not to abandon beings; skillfully teaching the true dharma so that all beings might permanently extinguish afflictive conduct; extinguishing only defilement while never extinguishing conditioned activity itself, undertaking immeasurable deeds; adorning every buddha-land for the sake of gathering together the great assembly of the true dharma; and knowing the Tathāgata to be without mark, without sound, without any conceptual discrimination whatsoever, yet still manifesting a variety of resembling appearances so that beings may see, approach, make offerings, and cultivate merit, turn the dharma-wheel, and skillfully answer whatever is asked. The treatise then confirms that, by this same wondrous, skillful wisdom becoming of the most excellent grade, utterly complete, one passes beyond the sixth abode and enters the seventh.

Chinese Source

《披》依能起世間興盛攝受福德等者：十地經論說：此勝行有四功德。一、財及身勝，隨其所須，自在能取故。二、護惡行勝，不捨眾生，起四無量故。三、護善根勝，勤修正行，不取少法故。四、攝眾生勝，此復多別：隨願受生，為不捨有情故；又復善說正法，為令一切眾生永滅煩惱行故；又復唯滅雜染，不滅有為，作業無量故；又復莊嚴一切佛土，為於大法眾會集故；又復了知如來無相、無聲、無諸分別，然有種種相似示現，為彼有情見聞、親近、供養、修行，生福德故，及為轉法輪故，隨彼所問善記別故。

即由如是妙方便慧所引不共進道勝行成上品故，極圓滿故，超過第六住，得入第七住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Commentary: Regarding "arousing world-flourishing" and the like — the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra says: this excellent conduct has four kinds of merit. First, excellence in wealth and body — freely able to obtain whatever is needed. Second, excellence in guarding against evil — never abandoning sentient beings, arousing the four immeasurables. Third, excellence in guarding wholesome roots — diligently cultivating correct conduct, grasping at not even the slightest dharma. Fourth, excellence in gathering sentient beings, itself further divided: taking rebirth according to vow, in order not to abandon sentient beings; furthermore, skillfully teaching the true dharma, so that all sentient beings might permanently extinguish afflictive conduct; furthermore, extinguishing only defilement, never extinguishing conditioned activity, undertaking immeasurable deeds; furthermore, adorning every buddha-land, for the sake of gathering together the great assembly of the true dharma; furthermore, knowing the Tathāgata to be without mark, without sound, without any conceptual discrimination, yet possessing every kind of resembling manifestation, so that sentient beings may see, approach, make offerings, and cultivate merit, and for the sake of turning the dharma-wheel, skillfully answering whatever is asked.

By this same wondrous, skillful wisdom's uncommon, excellent conduct becoming of the most excellent grade, utterly complete, one passes beyond the sixth abode and enters the seventh.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this fourfold grouping as a helpful compression of the same ten items already explained in full: the first three correspond directly to the first three skillful wisdoms (self-benefiting wealth, guarding against evil through compassion, and guarding one's own wholesome roots through non-attachment), while the fourth, gathering sentient beings, absorbs the remaining seven, each a different facet of reaching beings — through deliberate rebirth, through skillful teaching, through active yet non-defiling engagement, through adorning the physical setting where teaching occurs, and through the buddhas' own threefold manifestation of body, voice, and responsive mind despite each being, in ultimate truth, without any fixed mark whatsoever.

With entry into the seventh ground now confirmed, closing the first of twelve aspects, he notes, the treatise turns next to restating the same ten skillful wisdoms' own brief meaning from a slightly different angle, organized around what each wisdom "relies upon" as its underlying basis.

67. Milestone: The Tenfold Restatement — Relying on World-Flourishing, Benefiting Beings, and the Tathāgata's Own Body, Voice, and Mind

The treatise now restates the brief meaning of these same ten skillful wisdoms from a further angle, organized around what each relies upon as its basis, closing the first aspect entirely. Relying on what is able to give rise to world-flourishing and gather merit; relying on heightened aspiration for benefiting and bringing ease to sentient beings; relying on provisions of merit for the sake of awakening, and ever-further advancement in the factors of awakening; relying on what is uncommon to hearers; relying on what is uncommon to solitary-realizers; relying on the realm of sentient beings; relying on the realm of dharmas; relying on the realm of worlds; and relying on the Tathāgatas' own body, voice, mind, and wisdom — one should understand this as the brief meaning of the very ground on which wondrous, skillful wisdom's uncommon, excellent conduct rests.

Chinese Source

酉二、指略義　如是文辭，如經廣說，應知其相。謂依能起世間興盛攝受福德；依於有情利益安樂增上意樂；依為菩提，福德資糧、菩提分法後後勝進；依不共聲聞；依不共獨覺；依有情界；依諸法界；依諸世界；依諸如來身語心智。當知是名妙方便慧所引不共進道勝行處所略義。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, pointing to the brief meaning. This wording, exactly as the sutra explains at length; one should understand its mark accordingly. Namely, relying on what is able to give rise to world-flourishing and gather merit; relying on heightened aspiration for the benefit and ease of sentient beings; relying on provisions of merit for the sake of awakening, and ever-further advancement in the factors of awakening; relying on what is uncommon to hearers; relying on what is uncommon to solitary-realizers; relying on the realm of sentient beings; relying on the realm of dharmas; relying on the realm of worlds; relying on the Tathāgatas' own body, voice, mind, and wisdom. One should understand this as the brief meaning of the ground on which wondrous, skillful wisdom's uncommon, excellent conduct rests.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this ninefold restatement (the ninth item, relying on the Tathāgatas' own body, voice, mind, and wisdom, itself covering three of the original ten wisdoms at once) as offering a genuinely different organizing lens than the fourfold-excellence grouping just given: rather than grouping by kind of merit, this restatement groups by underlying object relied upon — world-flourishing wealth, beings' own benefit, one's own provisions toward awakening, distinction from the two lesser vehicles, the realm of beings, the realm of dharmas, the realm of worlds, and finally the Tathāgata's own threefold body-voice-mind expression — a genuinely comprehensive scan across every dimension a bodhisattva's skillful wisdom must address.

With the first aspect of twelve now fully closed through this second restatement, he notes, the treatise turns next to the second aspect: penetrating the buddhas' own state and diligently arousing sustained applied effort toward it.

68. Milestone: Penetrating the Buddhas' Own State, Arousing Diligent Applied Effort Toward the Eighth Ground

The treatise now explains the second aspect: such a bodhisattva, corresponding fully to these ten skillful wisdoms' own excellent conduct, is thereby able to penetrate the immeasurable, countless state of the buddhas, and, in order to observe that state, gives rise to conduct without effortful application, without mark, without discrimination, and without variant discrimination in observing the buddhas' own immeasurable state — arousing, without interruption or deficiency, diligent cultivation of every bodily deportment, whether walking or standing, constantly bringing attention to bear, never departing, in any circumstance whatsoever, from the noble path itself. The commentary explains "without variant discrimination" precisely: at the eighth ground, contemplation of ultimate truth requires no effortful application at all, hence "without effortful application, without mark, without discrimination" describes that ultimate contemplation, while conventional truth still requires some effortful application there, knowing conventional phenomena as illusion-like — hence "without variant discrimination" describes the combined, twofold contemplation of both truths together, observing the buddhas' own immeasurable state through both at once, without interruption and without deficiency.

Chinese Source

申二、通達佛境起勤加行　菩薩與彼共相應故，便能通達無量無數如來境界，及為彼起無功用、無相、無分別、無異分別觀無量佛境界，起無間無闕精勤修學一切威儀行住作意，一切分位不遠離道。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second aspect: penetrating the buddhas' own state, arousing diligent applied effort. Such a bodhisattva, corresponding fully to this excellent conduct, is thereby able to penetrate the immeasurable, countless state of the tathāgatas, and, in order to observe that state, gives rise to conduct without effortful application, without mark, without discrimination, without variant discrimination, observing the buddhas' own immeasurable state — arousing, without interruption or deficiency, diligent cultivation of every bodily deportment, whether walking or standing, constantly bringing attention to bear, never departing, in any circumstance whatsoever, from the path.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi cites the Discourse Record's own gloss: within the eighth ground, genuine contemplation of ultimate truth requires no effortful application whatsoever — hence "without effortful application, without mark, without discrimination" — while contemplation of conventional truth there still involves some effortful application, knowing conventional phenomena to be illusion-like, hence called "without variant discrimination." The eighth ground's own contemplation of both truths together is called observing the buddhas' own immeasurable state; the two contemplations arising is called without interruption, and both truths observed simultaneously is called without deficiency. This seventh-ground bodhisattva, aspiring to give rise to precisely this eighth-ground contemplation, diligently cultivates every bodily deportment accordingly.

He treats this aspect as the direct forward-reaching motivation for everything the seventh ground undertakes: a bodhisattva here does not yet possess the eighth ground's own effortless contemplation, but sustained, unbroken, attentive cultivation across every activity — walking, standing, and every constant application of mindfulness — is precisely how that future spontaneity is being prepared. Having explained this diligent applied effort aimed at the buddhas' own state, he notes, the treatise turns next to the third aspect: fully realizing the factors of awakening in every single instant of mind.

69. Milestone: Every Single Instant Fully Realizes the Factors of Awakening — Each Ground's Own Distinguishing Excellence Reviewed

The treatise now explains the third aspect in two parts. First, distinguishing this ground from those below: in every single instant of mind, the ten perfections taken as foremost, every factor belonging to awakening is completely, excellently fulfilled — a completeness no ground prior to the seventh has yet achieved. Second, an explanatory review of each ground's own distinguishing excellence in turn: at the first, utmost joy, the great vow itself is the most excellent object; at the second, the removal of every stain of precept violation; at the third, correct vows increasing, attaining the light of dharma; at the fourth, correctly entering the path; at the fifth, correctly entering every kind of worldly undertaking; at the sixth, correctly entering the profound principle of dependent origination; and now, at this seventh ground, every buddha-dharma's own factor of awakening is fully, completely aroused.

Chinese Source

申三、一一剎那圓證覺支　酉一、標簡　彼於一一心剎那中，十波羅蜜多而為上首，一切菩提分法圓滿殊勝。諸餘下住則不如是。

酉二、辨釋　謂於第一極歡喜住，正以大願為勝所緣；於第二住，正能除遣毀犯戒垢；於第三住，正願增長，得法光明；於第四住，正趣入道；於第五住，正入一切世間事業；於第六住，正入甚深緣起道理；今即於此第七住中，具足發起一切佛法覺支圓滿。

Ru-Yi Translation

Third aspect: every single instant fully realizes the factors of awakening; first, distinguishing this ground. In every single instant of mind, the ten perfections taken as foremost, every factor belonging to awakening is completely, excellently fulfilled. Every ground below this one is not yet so.

Second, an explanatory review. At the first, utmost joy, the great vow is the most excellent object. At the second, correctly removing every stain of precept violation. At the third, correct vows increasing, attaining the light of dharma. At the fourth, correctly entering the path. At the fifth, correctly entering every kind of worldly undertaking. At the sixth, correctly entering the profound principle of dependent origination. Now, at this seventh ground, every buddha-dharma's own factor of awakening is fully, completely aroused.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this concise, ground-by-ground review as an unusually valuable capstone in miniature — a single-sentence summary of everything the treatise has covered across the previous six grounds, each captured in its own most memorable feature: the great vow (utmost joy), freedom from precept violation (heightened discipline), the light of dharma from meditative concentration (heightened mind), entering the path through the thirty-seven factors of awakening (joined with the factors of awakening), mastering worldly undertakings without being stained by them (joined with the truths), and penetrating dependent origination through the three gates of liberation (joined with dependent origination) — before this seventh ground's own distinguishing mark: complete, instant-by-instant fulfillment of the factors of awakening themselves, at last, in their very fullness.

He notes the significance of "the ten perfections taken as foremost" appearing explicitly here for the first time in quite this phrasing — this treatise consistently supplements the classical six perfections with four further ones (skillful means, vow, power, and wisdom) to reach the fuller Mahāyāna list of ten, and it is precisely this complete tenfold set that a seventh-ground bodhisattva now brings to bear in every single moment of mind. Having reviewed each ground's own distinguishing excellence, closing the third aspect, he notes, the treatise turns next to the fourth aspect: this ground's applied-effort conduct now reaching its own complete fulfillment.

70. Milestone: Applied-Effort Conduct Now Complete — the Direct Threshold Into the Eighth Ground's Utmost Purity

The treatise now explains the fourth aspect: a bodhisattva at this abode is subsumed within the complete fulfillment of applied-effort conduct; the conduct of wondrous wisdom and spiritual power is now genuinely pure, able to enter the eighth abode. By this, such a bodhisattva, without any further interval, is able to enter the eighth abode of utmost purity.

Chinese Source

申四、有加行行圓滿所攝　此住菩薩加行行圓滿所攝故；妙智神通行清淨，能入第八住故；由是菩薩此住無間，能入第八極清淨住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fourth aspect: subsumed within the complete fulfillment of applied-effort conduct. A bodhisattva at this abode is subsumed within the complete fulfillment of applied-effort conduct. The conduct of wondrous wisdom and spiritual power is genuinely pure, able to enter the eighth abode. By this, such a bodhisattva, without any further interval, is able to enter the eighth abode of utmost purity.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this fourth aspect as a pivotal turning point within the seventh ground itself: everything cultivated so far — the ten skillful wisdoms, diligent applied effort toward the buddhas' state, and instant-by-instant fulfillment of the factors of awakening — now reaches its own complete fulfillment specifically as applied-effort conduct, the very last stage of effortful cultivation before the eighth ground's spontaneous, effortless abiding begins without any further interval whatsoever.

He notes the phrase "without any further interval" as significant — echoing the treatise's own earlier description of the instantaneous transition from resolute practice into the first ground, and again from the peak of superior forbearance into utmost joy — suggesting that the seventh-to-eighth-ground transition is likewise a single, decisive moment rather than a further gradual process, applied effort giving way to spontaneity in one clean transition once fully ripened. Having explained this complete fulfillment directly threshold to the eighth ground, he notes, the treatise turns next to the fifth aspect: a careful, paradoxical account of in what sense this seventh ground counts as defiled or undefiled.

71. Milestone: A Careful Paradox — Undefiled Relative to What Follows, Yet Still Falling Within Defiled Conduct

The treatise now explains the fifth aspect in two parts. First, viewed against the eighth abode: that eighth abode is uniformly pure, while this seventh abode is still called mixed — since it serves as forerunner to that pure abode, this abode should be called undefiled; yet since that purity is not yet actually attained, this abode should equally be said to fall within defiled conduct. Second, viewed solely in terms of this abode itself: within this very abode, every ringleading affliction such as craving and the rest has been entirely eliminated and severed; one should understand this abode as neither possessing affliction nor as free of affliction — no affliction manifests at all, and yet the wisdom of the buddhas being longed for still has not been attained.

Chinese Source

申五、安立染汙及不染汙　酉一、望第八住　彼第八住一向清淨，此第七住猶名為雜。與清淨住為前導故，當言此住名不染汙；猶未得故，當言此住墮雜染行。

酉二、唯約自住　今此住中，一切貪等上首煩惱皆悉除斷。當知此住非有煩惱，非離煩惱。一切煩惱不現行故，希求佛智猶未得故。

《披》希求佛智猶未得故者：此釋前說非離煩惱。由有加行、有功用，猶未證得清淨無相故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fifth aspect: establishing defiled and undefiled; first, viewed against the eighth abode. That eighth abode is uniformly pure; this seventh abode is still called mixed. Because it serves as forerunner to that pure abode, this abode should be called undefiled; because that purity is not yet attained, this abode should be said to fall within defiled conduct.

Second, viewed solely in terms of this abode. Within this abode, every ringleading affliction such as craving is entirely eliminated and severed. One should understand this abode as neither possessing affliction nor free of affliction. No affliction manifests at all, and yet the wisdom of the buddhas longed for has still not been attained.

Commentary: Regarding "the wisdom of the buddhas longed for has still not been attained" — this explains the earlier statement "not free of affliction." Because of still having applied effort, still having effortful application, the purity of the markless has not yet been genuinely realized.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this fifth aspect as one of the treatise's own most carefully calibrated passages, refusing any simple yes-or-no answer to whether the seventh ground is "pure": measured against the eighth ground's own uniform purity, the seventh ground counts as impure by comparison, yet, precisely because it serves as that purity's own direct forerunner, it can equally be called undefiled in its own right — both descriptions genuinely true depending on the standard of comparison invoked.

He treats the second, self-contained account as resolving what might otherwise seem a contradiction: no affliction whatsoever manifests here (hence "not possessing affliction"), yet the seventh ground has not yet attained the buddhas' own complete wisdom, still requiring effortful application rather than the eighth ground's own spontaneous purity (hence "not free of affliction," in the sense of not yet having fully arrived) — the very fact of still needing effort is itself classified, in this precise technical sense, as a remaining trace of impurity, however free of any actual afflictive emotion the seventh-ground bodhisattva genuinely is. Having explained this careful, twofold account of purity and defilement, he notes, the treatise turns next to the sixth aspect: pure conduct and worldly craft both reaching complete fulfillment.

72. Milestone: Teacher to the Entire Trichiliocosm — Unmatched by Any Save Bodhisattvas of the Eighth Ground and Beyond

The treatise now explains the sixth aspect: such a practitioner's own heightened aspiration has now attained purity, immeasurable bodily, verbal, and mental karma following accordingly, and, regarding what the Tathāgatas praise and what they condemn, exactly as already explained at length, the worldly craft and skillful learning already drawn forth back at the fifth abode now turns genuinely complete and perfect. The entire trichiliocosm of world-systems, by common consent, acknowledges this bodhisattva as teacher — with the sole exception of bodhisattvas abiding at a ground higher still, and the tathāgatas themselves — no one else's own aspiration and applied effort proving equal to this bodhisattva's own.

Chinese Source

申六、淨業工巧皆得圓滿　如是行者，增上意樂已得清淨，無量身語意業隨轉，於諸如來所讚毀業，如前廣說，於第五住所引世間工巧業智轉得圓滿。三千世界共許為師，唯除安住上住菩薩及諸如來，意樂加行無與等者。

Ru-Yi Translation

Sixth aspect: pure conduct and worldly craft both reaching complete fulfillment. Such a practitioner's heightened aspiration has attained purity, immeasurable bodily, verbal, and mental karma following accordingly. Regarding what the Tathāgatas praise and condemn, exactly as already explained at length, the worldly craft and skillful learning drawn forth at the fifth abode now turns genuinely complete and perfect. The entire trichiliocosm, by common consent, acknowledges this bodhisattva as teacher — excepting only bodhisattvas abiding at a higher ground and the tathāgatas themselves — no one else's aspiration and applied effort proving equal.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this aspect as a natural continuation and deepening of the fifth ground's own mastery of worldly crafts: what began there as skillful means for reaching beings at their own level has, by the seventh ground, matured into genuine mastery so complete that an entire trichiliocosm of world-systems recognizes this bodhisattva as its own teacher — a recognition withheld only from those genuinely surpassing this bodhisattva's own attainment, namely bodhisattvas of the eighth ground and beyond, together with the buddhas themselves.

He notes the careful qualification embedded in the phrase "what the Tathāgatas praise and condemn": even at this advanced stage, subtle traces of grasping at the reality of what is done can still, technically, draw the same kind of critique already explained at the second ground, the seventh ground's own purity being genuinely vast yet not yet absolutely complete. Having explained this ground's own unmatched worldly and spiritual mastery, he notes, the treatise turns next to the seventh aspect: attaining immeasurable, uncommon meditative absorption.

73. Milestone: Immeasurable, Uncommon Absorption — Still Cultivated Through Effort, Yet Already Surpassing Every Hearer and Solitary-Realizer

The treatise now explains the seventh aspect across three parts. First, the mark of attainment: such a bodhisattva is able to bring every meditative absorption and factor of awakening directly to presence, through the mark of active cultivation becoming directly present — not through abiding in a ripened, resultant state, as will be the case at the eighth abode. Second, displaying its immeasurability: through skillful means of this kind, such a bodhisattva is well able to discern among meditative absorptions, drawing forth foremost gates of bodhisattva absorption numbering in the tens, hundreds, and thousands. Third, clarifying what is uncommon, itself in two parts: regarding absorption itself, by attaining absorption of this kind, one surpasses the entire domain of every hearer and solitary-realizer's own absorption; and regarding the sphere of activity, such a bodhisattva, every affliction now entirely departed from, yet still possessing an extremely subtle, difficult-to-discern discriminating activity following bodily, verbal, and mental karma, abides in all of this without ever abandoning the pursuit of ever-greater advancement, courageous applied effort, and heartfelt regard for sentient beings — the commentary further distinguishing the seventh ground's own superiority as residing in the discerning wisdom itself, whereas the first six grounds surpassed the two lesser vehicles chiefly through the vastness of their own object of concern (buddha-dharma and every sentient being's ultimate benefit) rather than through wisdom's own inherent superiority alone.

Chinese Source

申七、得三摩地無量不共　酉一、辨得相　於一切靜慮等菩提分法皆能現前，由修行相現在前故，非由安住異熟分位，如第八住。

酉二、顯無量　此諸菩薩如是方便，能善思擇諸三摩地，引發菩薩三摩地上首十百千種三摩地門。

酉三、明不共　戌一、於三摩地　由得如是三摩地故，超過一切聲聞、獨覺三摩地境。

戌二、於諸境界　亥一、辨所行境　菩薩如是一切煩惱皆悉遠離；難可了知一切分別現行隨逐身語意業皆悉安住；而不捨離尋求勝進勇猛加行，顧念有情。

亥二、料簡差別　於此住中，由自覺慧境界故，超過一切聲聞、獨覺境界；餘六住中，但由佛法增上所緣故，超過一切聲聞、獨覺境界。

Ru-Yi Translation

Seventh aspect: attaining immeasurable, uncommon absorption; first, distinguishing the mark of attainment. Regarding every meditative absorption belonging to the factors of awakening, one is able to bring all directly to presence, through the mark of active cultivation becoming directly present — not through abiding in a ripened, resultant state, as at the eighth abode.

Second, displaying its immeasurability. Such bodhisattvas, through skillful means of this kind, are well able to discern among meditative absorptions, drawing forth foremost gates of bodhisattva absorption numbering in the tens, hundreds, and thousands.

Third, clarifying what is uncommon; first, regarding absorption itself. By attaining absorption of this kind, one surpasses the entire domain of every hearer and solitary-realizer's own absorption.

Second, regarding the sphere of activity; first, distinguishing the sphere engaged. Such a bodhisattva, every affliction entirely departed from; yet possessing an extremely subtle, difficult-to-discern discriminating activity following bodily, verbal, and mental karma, abiding in all of this; and never abandoning the pursuit of ever-greater advancement, courageous applied effort, and heartfelt regard for sentient beings.

Second, distinguishing this from the earlier grounds. Within this abode, by the very object of one's own awakened wisdom, one surpasses the entire domain of every hearer and solitary-realizer. Within the remaining six abodes, it was solely through buddha-dharma as one's heightened object of concern that one surpassed the entire domain of every hearer and solitary-realizer.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi draws out the crucial distinction the third part makes explicit: at the first six grounds, a bodhisattva surpassed hearers and solitary-realizers primarily because of what was contemplated — the vast object of buddha-dharma and every sentient being's own ultimate benefit, sustained across countless future eons, rather than any lesser vehicle's own more limited concern with personal liberation alone; but at this seventh ground, surpassing them owes directly to the wisdom itself, now genuinely superior in its own inherent quality, not merely in its object.

He revisits the still-subtle activity described here — an extremely fine, difficult-to-discern discrimination following bodily, verbal, and mental karma, cross-referenced elsewhere in the commentary to the seventh and eighth consciousnesses' own continued, barely perceptible activity — as showing precisely how refined the seventh ground's own remaining imperfection actually is: not the coarse afflictions of ordinary experience, nor even the subtler afflictions already severed at earlier grounds, but activity so fine it borders on the imperceptible, distinguishing this ground's own genuine achievement from the eighth ground's own still-further, complete transcendence of even this. Having explained immeasurable, uncommon absorption in full, he notes, the treatise turns next to the eighth aspect: entering the attainment of cessation in every single instant, without ever actually certifying it.

74. Milestone: Entering Cessation in Every Instant, Yet Never Certifying It — the Astonishing, Inconceivable Undertaking

The treatise now explains the eighth aspect: whereas bodhisattvas at the sixth abode were able to enter the attainment of cessation, bodhisattvas at this seventh abode are able to enter it in every single instant of mind — and yet these bodhisattvas' own undertaking is astonishing and inconceivable, since they constantly abide within the very state of ultimate reality, while never actually certifying that same quiescent cessation. Just as Vimalakīrti, without rising from cessation's own attainment, nonetheless manifests every ordinary deportment, conversing and acting among beings while inwardly abiding in cessation's own profound stillness, so too a seventh-ground bodhisattva enters this same attainment moment after moment without ever taking the final step of actually certifying it — able, unlike a hearer bound within a strict seven-day limit before that same attainment forces final departure from the body, to remain fully engaged in ordinary life indefinitely, tasting the very threshold of ultimate liberation yet declining, again and again, to cross fully over.

Chinese Source

申八、剎那剎那能入滅定　又諸菩薩第六住中所入滅定，今此住中念念能入。然此菩薩甚希奇業不可思議。謂常安住實際住中，而於寂滅能不作證。

《披》第六住中所入滅定等者：經說：菩薩從第六地來，能入寂滅定，今住第七菩薩地，於念念中能入寂滅定，而不證寂滅定，是菩薩畢竟成就不可思議身口意業。此應準知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Eighth aspect: entering the attainment of cessation in every single instant. Furthermore, whereas bodhisattvas at the sixth abode were able to enter the attainment of cessation, bodhisattvas at this abode are able to enter it in every single instant of mind. And yet these bodhisattvas' own undertaking is astonishing, inconceivable — constantly abiding within the very state of ultimate reality, while never actually certifying that same quiescent cessation.

Commentary: Regarding "the attainment of cessation entered at the sixth abode" and the like — the sutra says: a bodhisattva, from the sixth ground onward, is able to enter the attainment of quiescent cessation; now, abiding at the seventh bodhisattva ground, in every single instant one is able to enter the attainment of quiescent cessation, and yet does not certify that same quiescent cessation — this bodhisattva ultimately accomplishing inconceivable bodily, verbal, and mental karma. This should be understood accordingly.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the classical rules governing the attainment of cessation for hearers: an ordinary practitioner who enters it, the sixth consciousness entirely stilled, can remain there for at most seven days — sustained by only the subtlest of the four kinds of nourishment (the store-consciousness's own nourishment alone, the other three, including the pleasant taste of meditative absorption itself, entirely absent) — beyond which the physical body cannot be sustained; more advanced hearers of great wisdom face less restriction, but a bodhisattva's own attainment of this same cessation, the Mahāvibhāṣā itself states, is genuinely beyond what any hearer can match.

He treats the Vimalakīrti reference as the classical illustration of exactly this seventh-ground phenomenon: outwardly conversing, teaching, and acting among ordinary beings with every appearance of normal engagement, while inwardly, moment to moment, abiding in the very cessation a hearer could sustain only briefly and only in isolated stillness — entering repeatedly, yet declining each time to take the final step of certifying it, precisely so as to remain available for beings' sake. He treats this astonishing capacity — tasting liberation's own threshold constantly, while continually choosing engagement over final withdrawal — as perhaps this ground's single most vivid illustration of what "applied effort with no further mark" actually means in lived practice. Having explained this remarkable capacity, he notes, the treatise turns next to the ninth aspect: manifesting every uncommon, wondrous conduct in actual practice.

75. Milestone: Nine Uncommon Undertakings — Resembling the World in Appearance, Yet Never Sharing Its Nature

The treatise now explains the ninth aspect in two parts. First, naming its mark: such a bodhisattva, through the heightened power drawn forth by these same wondrous, skillful means, is able to undertake bodhisattva conduct uncommon to any ordinary sentient being — though appearing to resemble the world, never actually sharing its nature. Second, explaining the sutra's own meaning at length, gathered under a single overarching theme with nine distinct undertakings: gathering various kin and followers through meritorious deeds (the conduct of gathering beings); distinctions in seeking rebirth, according to vow (the conduct of rebirth by vow); arousing ever more excellent abiding in the three gates of liberation (the conduct of excellent liberation); skillfully subduing those inclined toward the lesser vehicles through resolute understanding (the conduct of subduing the lesser vehicle); partaking of sensory desires such as food and sleep (the conduct of partaking of desire); displaying distinctions in seeking desire, including old age, sickness, and death, and every demonic realm (the conduct of distinct seeking); and a threefold conduct of transformation — transforming non-Buddhist paths, according with others' own minds, and according with the great assembly, whether celestial, draconic, or otherwise.

Chinese Source

申九、現行一切不共妙行　酉一、標彼相　彼由如是妙方便智之所引發增上力故，能行一切有情不共菩薩妙行。雖與世間相似顯現，而非彼性。

酉二、釋經義　如經廣說。此中總義，謂依福業事攝受種種親屬徒眾，求生差別，發起勝進三解脫住，信解劣乘方便調伏，受用諸欲，求欲差別，轉諸外道，隨他心轉，隨大眾轉。

《披》如經廣說等者：此中略引經義，釋前菩薩不共妙行，隨順世間，非世間性。一、攝受有情行。二、願力受生行。三、勝進解脫行。四、調伏劣乘行。五、受用諸欲行，受用飲食睡夢等境界故。六、求欲差別行，示老病死魔境界故。七、轉行，此復有三，一、轉外道，二、隨他心，三、隨眾轉。此中大眾，謂天龍等。是即經中所有總義。

Ru-Yi Translation

Ninth aspect: manifesting every uncommon, wondrous conduct; first, naming its mark. Such a bodhisattva, through the heightened power drawn forth by this same wondrous, skillful wisdom, is able to undertake bodhisattva conduct uncommon to any ordinary sentient being. Though appearing to resemble the world, it never actually shares its nature.

Second, explaining the sutra's own meaning. Exactly as the sutra explains at length. The total meaning here is: relying on meritorious undertakings to gather various kin and followers; distinctions in seeking rebirth; arousing ever more excellent abiding in the three gates of liberation; skillfully subduing, through resolute understanding, those inclined toward the lesser vehicles; partaking of sensory desires; distinctions in seeking desire; transforming non-Buddhist paths; according with others' own minds; according with the great assembly.

Commentary: Regarding "exactly as the sutra explains at length" — this briefly cites the sutra's own meaning, explaining the bodhisattva's uncommon, wondrous conduct as according with the world without sharing the world's own nature. First, the conduct of gathering beings. Second, the conduct of rebirth by vow. Third, the conduct of excellent liberation. Fourth, the conduct of subduing the lesser vehicle. Fifth, the conduct of partaking of desire — partaking of food, sleep, dreaming, and comparable states. Sixth, the conduct of distinct seeking — displaying old age, sickness, death, and demonic realms. Seventh, the conduct of transformation, itself threefold: transforming non-Buddhist paths, according with others' minds, according with the great assembly — the great assembly here meaning celestial beings, dragons, and the rest. This is the total meaning present in the sutra.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through all nine undertakings as together illustrating a single principle: full, convincing participation in ordinary human existence — family ties, chosen rebirths, sensory experience including food and sleep and even dreaming, the appearance of aging, illness, and death, and full fluency in swaying non-Buddhist teachers, individual minds, and entire assemblies alike — combined with an interior nature genuinely untouched by any of it. He notes the striking inclusion of dreaming here, citing the Mahāvibhāṣā's own observation that only a fully awakened buddha never dreams at all; every other being, hearers and solitary-realizers included, continues to dream, dream-content itself serving as a rough gauge of how much conceptual proliferation still remains active in an otherwise purified mind — though buddhas, he adds, sometimes deliberately use dreams as a skillful teaching device for beings who can be reached no other way.

He treats "subduing" here as one of two classical modes of teaching, contrasted with wrathful correction: gentle, compassionate accommodation of a being's own current capacity, gradually leading hearers and solitary-realizers toward the greater vehicle rather than confronting them directly — itself a form of what the treatise elsewhere calls skillful means adapted to provisional teaching before ultimate truth. Having explained all nine uncommon undertakings, closing the ninth aspect, he notes, the treatise turns next to the tenth aspect: purified wholesome roots, given once more with two further escalated similes.

76. Milestone: Gold Now Set With Wish-Fulfilling Jewels, and Sunlight That Dries Every Damp, Defiling Thing It Touches

The treatise now explains the tenth aspect in two parts. First, the general account, pointing back to what was already said: everything else follows exactly as before. Second, a simile marking the difference: just as a skillful craftsman's own smelted gold, wrought into an ornament and set with brilliant wish-fulfilling jewels for adornment — surpassing even the previous ground's lapis lazuli — shines with such radiant splendor that no other gold throughout the entire southern continent can equal it, so too this bodhisattva's own wholesome roots, growing ever more pure, cannot be outshone by any hearer, solitary-realizer, or bodhisattva of any lower abode. A further simile specifically for the faculty of wisdom follows: just as sunlight is largely able to dry up every damp, defiling wetness throughout the southern continent, no lesser light — moonlight, starlight, lamplight — able to match it, so too this bodhisattva's own wisdom-light is largely able to dry up every kind of poisonous affliction in every sentient being, unmatched by the wisdom-light of any hearer as already described — surpassing them, moreover, through the very quality of realized wisdom itself, and not merely, as at the first six grounds, through the vastness of its own object of concern.

Chinese Source

申十、善根清淨　酉一、總辨善根　戌一、指同前　餘如前說。

戌二、喻差別　此差別者，謂如世間善巧工匠，以所鍊金作莊嚴具，諸末尼寶瑩飾廁鈿，甚為光麗，餘贍部洲一切金寶不能映奪。如是此中菩薩善根轉復清淨，一切聲聞、獨覺善根，及餘下住菩薩善根，不能映奪。

酉二、別喻慧根　又如日光，多分乾竭贍部洲中所有穢濕，餘一切光不能映奪。如是此中菩薩慧光，多分乾竭一切有情煩惱諸毒，如前所說諸聲聞等所有智光不能映奪。

Ru-Yi Translation

Tenth aspect: purified wholesome roots; first, the general account; first, pointing to what was already said. Everything else follows exactly as already explained.

Second, a simile marking the difference. The difference is this: just as, in the world, a skillful craftsman's own smelted gold, wrought into an ornament and set with wish-fulfilling jewels for adornment, shines with such radiance that no gold throughout the entire southern continent can equal it — so too this bodhisattva's own wholesome roots, growing ever more pure, cannot be outshone by any hearer, solitary-realizer, or bodhisattva of any lower abode.

Second, a further simile specifically for the faculty of wisdom. Just as sunlight is largely able to dry up every damp, defiling wetness throughout the southern continent, no other light able to match it — so too this bodhisattva's own wisdom-light is largely able to dry up every kind of poisonous affliction in every sentient being, unmatched by the wisdom-light of any hearer as already described.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi traces the simile's own full seven-ground progression: raw ore purified by fire, gold refined further in alum, gold weighed and found exactly equal, gold wrought into an ornament, that ornament set with agate, then lapis lazuli, and now, at the seventh ground, set with wish-fulfilling jewels themselves — mani gems traditionally believed capable of producing whatever is wished for merely by touching them, the most precious material this entire recurring simile has yet invoked.

He underscores the closing qualification as this milestone's own most important point: the seventh ground's own wisdom surpasses hearers and solitary-realizers specifically through the quality of the wisdom itself, exactly as already explained two milestones earlier, whereas the first six grounds surpassed them chiefly through the vastness of their object of contemplation — a bodhisattva's concern for buddha-dharma and every sentient being's ultimate benefit being vaster than a hearer's own more limited concern, even where the underlying realization was not yet categorically superior in kind. Having explained purified wholesome roots through both similes, he notes, the treatise turns next to the eleventh and twelfth aspects: rebirth and majestic power.

77. Milestone: Rebirth as Lord of the Heaven of Free Enjoyment of Others' Creations, Teaching Even Hearers and Solitary-Realizers Their Own Direct Realization

The treatise now explains the eleventh and twelfth aspects. The eleventh, rebirth: such a bodhisattva, taking rebirth, most often becomes the lord of the Heaven of Free Enjoyment of Others' Creations — the sixth and highest of the six desire-realm heavens, where the gods freely enjoy delights conjured by others rather than needing to conjure their own — skillfully bestowing upon every hearer and solitary-realizer the very skillful means for their own direct realization, guiding beings of that capacity to genuinely, personally realize emptiness of self for themselves. The twelfth, majestic power: such power is now said to reach a koṭi times a hundred thousand — surpassing even the sixth ground's own hundred-thousand-koṭi scale by yet another order of magnitude, whether such a bodhisattva remains a householder or takes up monastic life.

Chinese Source

申十一、受生　受生多作他化自在天王，於能授與一切聲聞、獨覺現觀方便善巧。

《披》於能授與一切聲聞獨覺現觀方便善巧者：謂彼所作自在，能令一切聲聞、獨覺獲得現觀故。

申十二、威力　所有威力，當知此說俱胝百千數。

Ru-Yi Translation

Eleventh aspect: rebirth. Taking rebirth, one most often becomes the lord of the Heaven of Free Enjoyment of Others' Creations, skillfully bestowing upon every hearer and solitary-realizer the very means for direct realization.

Commentary: Regarding "skillfully bestowing upon every hearer and solitary-realizer the means for direct realization" — this means that, through this bodhisattva's own sovereign activity, every hearer and solitary-realizer is enabled to attain direct realization.

Twelfth aspect: majestic power. One should understand that the majestic power spoken of here reaches a koṭi times a hundred thousand.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi notes the completed ascent of rebirth across all six desire-realm heavens by this seventh ground: iron-wheel king, gold-wheel king, then Śakra, Suyāma, Tuṣita, Manifest Delight, and now the Heaven of Free Enjoyment of Others' Creations, the very highest and most sovereign heaven the desire realm contains — an especially fitting culmination for a bodhisattva now capable of personally guiding hearers and solitary-realizers toward their own direct realization of selflessness, a teaching capacity requiring genuine mastery of exactly the path those two vehicles themselves walk.

He confirms the scaling now spanning all seven grounds: a hundred, a thousand, a hundred thousand, a koṭi, a thousand koṭi, a hundred thousand koṭi, and now a koṭi times a hundred thousand — each ground's own majestic power continuing to compound well beyond any simple fixed multiplier. Having explained rebirth and majestic power, closing all twelve aspects, he notes, the treatise turns next to a concluding summary naming them together, followed by the cross-reference explaining why this ground is called, in the wider tradition, the ground of going far.

78. Milestone: The Summary Names All Twelve Aspects, and the Treatise Confirms — This Is the Ground of Going Far

The treatise now closes the entire abode with applied effort, having no further mark, with a concluding summary and final cross-reference. In brief, this is called the bodhisattva's abode with applied effort, having no further mark: because wondrous, skillful wisdom's world-transcending, excellent conduct reaches complete fulfillment and entry; because, penetrating the Tathāgata's own state, one arouses diligent applied effort without interruption or deficiency; because every factor of awakening is fully realized in every single instant; because applied-effort conduct is subsumed within its own complete fulfillment; because defiled and undefiled are both established; because, in reliance on purified aspiration's own conduct, every kind of worldly craft and skillful learning reaches complete fulfillment; because immeasurable absorption uncommon to every hearer and solitary-realizer is attained; because the attainment of cessation is entered in every single instant; because every conduct uncommon to ordinary sentient beings, though resembling the world, is manifested; because of purified wholesome roots; because of rebirth; because of majestic power. Should one wish to hear this expounded at greater length, the treatise directs the reader to the Ten Grounds Sutra's own chapter on the ground of going far — explaining that this ground, subsumed within the complete fulfillment of applied-effort conduct, is thereby named the ground of going far; and, by this very same meaning, one should understand this is exactly what is here called the abode with applied effort, having no further mark.

Chinese Source

未二、結略義　當知是名略說菩薩有加行有功用無相住。謂妙方便慧所引世間進道勝行成滿得入故；通達如來佛境界，起無間無闕勤加行故；一一剎那圓證一切菩提分法故；有加行行圓滿攝故；安立染汙不染汙故；依於意樂清淨業轉，一切世間工巧業等皆圓滿故；逮得無量不共一切聲聞、獨覺三摩地故；剎那剎那入滅定故；現行一切有情不共世間行故；善根清淨故；受生故；威力故。

午二、廣指　未一、指　若廣宣說，如十地經遠行地說。

未二、釋　此地菩薩有加行行圓滿攝故，名遠行地。

未三、配　即由此義，當知亦名有加行有功用無相住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, closing the brief meaning. One should understand this, in brief, as the bodhisattva's abode with applied effort, having no further mark — namely, because wondrous, skillful wisdom's world-transcending, excellent conduct reaches complete fulfillment and entry; because, penetrating the Tathāgata's own state, one arouses diligent applied effort without interruption or deficiency; because every factor of awakening is fully realized in every single instant; because applied-effort conduct is subsumed within its own complete fulfillment; because defiled and undefiled are both established; because, in reliance on purified aspiration's own conduct, every kind of worldly craft and skillful learning reaches complete fulfillment; because immeasurable absorption uncommon to every hearer and solitary-realizer is attained; because the attainment of cessation is entered in every single instant; because every conduct uncommon to ordinary sentient beings, though resembling the world, is manifested; because of purified wholesome roots; because of rebirth; because of majestic power.

Second, the broader pointer; first, pointing to it. Should one wish to hear it expounded at length, it is exactly as the Ten Grounds Sutra expounds it in its chapter on the ground of going far.

Second, explaining. This ground, subsumed within the complete fulfillment of applied-effort conduct, is thereby named the ground of going far.

Third, the correspondence. By this very meaning, one should understand this is also named the abode with applied effort, having no further mark.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi reviews all twelve aspects as a final capstone: ten skillful wisdoms of excellent conduct; diligent applied effort toward the buddhas' own state; instant-by-instant fulfillment of the factors of awakening with a full review of every prior ground's own excellence; applied-effort conduct itself completely fulfilled; the careful paradox of defilement and purity; unmatched worldly and spiritual mastery; immeasurable absorption surpassing the two lesser vehicles through wisdom's own quality; entering cessation each instant without ever certifying it; nine uncommon undertakings resembling yet never sharing the world's own nature; purified wholesome roots (gold set with wish-fulfilling jewels, wisdom like sunlight drying every defiling dampness); rebirth as lord of the highest desire-realm heaven; and majestic power reaching a koṭi times a hundred thousand.

He explains "ground of going far" as capturing two senses at once: a bodhisattva here has traveled such a distance along the path that only three grounds remain before buddhahood itself, genuinely "far" from the ordinary world in spiritual attainment; and this same ground marks the very last stage of effortful, applied cultivation, having "gone the distance" of every possible effort before the eighth ground's own spontaneous, effortless abiding begins without any further interval. With this correspondence confirmed, the entire abode with applied effort, having no further mark — the seventh ground, examined across all twelve aspects — reaches its complete conclusion.
