The treatise turns next to the ninth of the eleven-part granular analysis's own next abode: the abode without applied effort, without effortful application, having no further mark, the eighth ground, opening with eight aspects of its own and, at last, the spontaneous abiding this entire seventh ground has been diligently preparing.

79. Milestone: The Abode Without Applied Effort Opens — Ten Kinds of Wisdom Into Ultimate Truth, Three Shared and Seven Distinct

The treatise now opens the ninth abode: without applied effort, without effortful application, having no further mark, corresponding to the eighth ground, also called the ground of immovability — the very spontaneous abiding the entire seventh ground has been diligently preparing. The question is posed: what is a bodhisattva's abode without applied effort, without effortful application, having no further mark? The answer opens with the first of eight aspects: entry brought to fulfillment through ultimate-truth wisdom. Bodhisattvas, at the initial markless abode, have already attained ten kinds of wisdom entering into every dharma's own ultimate truth, exactly as the sutra explains at length. The commentary supplies the Ten Grounds Sutra's own full list, organized around three shared qualities every ground from the first onward must cultivate — skillfully gathering the skillful means of wisdom, skillfully purifying every conduct, skillfully gathering the dharmas supporting the path — followed by seven distinct qualities, each paralleling one of the seven grounds already traversed: skillfully arousing the power of great vows (paralleling the first ground); skillfully strengthened by the Tathāgata's own empowering power through purified precept-observance (paralleling the second); gaining strength through one's own wholesome-root power via unretreating meditative absorption (paralleling the third); constantly, mindfully according with the Tathāgata's own powers, fearlessnesses, and exclusive qualities (paralleling the fourth); skillfully purifying a profound mind of awakening through the equality of all dharmas (paralleling the fifth); accomplishing the power of merit and wisdom through great compassion and sovereign mastery over dependent origination (paralleling the sixth); and great compassion never abandoning any sentient being's own conduct, penetrating the immeasurable path of wisdom (paralleling the seventh).

Chinese Source

辰九、無加行無功用無相住　巳一、問　云何菩薩無加行無功用無相住？

巳二、答　午一、略說　未一、別辨相　申一、第一義智成滿得入　酉一、標經說　謂諸菩薩，於初無相住中，已得十種入一切法第一義智，如經廣說。

《披》已得十種入一切法第一義智等者：十地經說：若菩薩於七地中，善集慧方便，善清淨諸行，善集助道法，善起大願力，善加如來力加自善根力得力故，常念隨順如來力、無畏、不共佛法故，善淨深心覺故，成就福德智力故，大慈悲等不捨一切眾生行故，通達無量智道故。是名十種入一切法第一義智。論一一釋，如彼應知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Ninth abode, without applied effort, without effortful application, having no further mark; first, the question. What is a bodhisattva's abode without applied effort, without effortful application, having no further mark?

Second, the answer; first, the brief account; first, distinguishing each mark; first, entry brought to fulfillment through ultimate-truth wisdom; first, pointing to what the sutra says. Namely, bodhisattvas, at the initial markless abode, have already attained ten kinds of wisdom entering into every dharma's own ultimate truth, exactly as the sutra explains at length.

Commentary: Regarding "ten kinds of wisdom entering into every dharma's own ultimate truth" — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: if bodhisattvas, at the seventh ground, skillfully gather the skillful means of wisdom, skillfully purify every conduct, skillfully gather the dharmas supporting the path, skillfully arouse the power of great vows, are skillfully strengthened by the Tathāgata's own empowering power and gain strength through one's own wholesome-root power, constantly, mindfully according with the Tathāgata's own powers, fearlessnesses, and exclusive qualities, skillfully purify a profound mind of awakening, accomplish the power of merit and wisdom, and, through great compassion, never abandon any sentient being's own conduct, penetrating the immeasurable path of wisdom — these are called the ten kinds of wisdom entering into every dharma's own ultimate truth. The treatise explains each individually; one should understand accordingly.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this eighth ground's opening as a moment of genuine culmination: everything cultivated across the first seven grounds now converges, at last, into spontaneous, effortless abiding — the ground of immovability precisely because nothing further can disturb it. He explains the three shared qualities as constants every ground from the first onward must continually renew (gathering wisdom's own skillful means, purifying conduct, gathering supportive dharmas), while the seven distinct qualities each directly echo one specific ground already traversed: great vows echoing utmost joy, empowered precept-purity echoing heightened discipline, unretreating absorption echoing heightened mind, mindful accordance with the buddhas' own qualities echoing the ground joined with the factors of awakening, profound equality echoing the ground joined with the truths, mastery over dependent origination echoing the ground of direct presence, and boundless compassion echoing the ground of going far.

He notes the significance of this backward-looking structure: the eighth ground does not introduce some wholly new content unrelated to what came before, but rather brings the entire prior sevenfold cultivation to its own natural, spontaneous completion — nothing genuinely new is added; what was cultivated with effort simply becomes, at last, effortless. Having named all ten kinds of ultimate-truth wisdom, he notes, the treatise turns next to the sutra's own brief restatement of how these ten converge on entry into this ground.

80. Milestone: Past and Future Have Neither Substance Nor Mark — the Present, Though Seemingly Present, Is Equally Illusion-Like

The treatise now begins a threefold restatement of how these ten wisdoms converge on entry into the eighth ground. First, relying on the three times, as each accordingly requires: all dharmas are, from the very outset, unarisen, unoriginated, and markless. The commentary explains that although this markless, unarisen, unoriginated nature applies to the formations of all three times alike, the precise meaning differs slightly for each, hence "as each accordingly requires": past and future formations, having no genuine substantial existence at all, are for this reason unarisen, unoriginated, and markless; present formations, though their nature likewise lacks genuine substantial existence, nonetheless appear illusion-like, their very arising possessing no fixed nature of its own — and so these too are said to be unarisen, unoriginated, and markless, though in a subtly different sense, since something does, after all, appear to be occurring right now, however illusory that appearance ultimately proves to be.

Chinese Source

酉二、舉略義　謂依三世，如其所應，本來無生、無起、無相；

《披》謂依三世等者：三世諸行，本來無生、無起、無相，然義有別，是故說言如其所應。過去、未來性非實有，是故無生、無起、無相。現在諸行性雖實有，然如幻像，生無自性，是故亦說無生、無起、無相。當知此依相無自性，故作是說。約彼自體，說無生起。約彼自相，說名無相。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, presenting the brief meaning. Namely, relying on the three times, as each accordingly requires, all dharmas are, from the very outset, unarisen, unoriginated, and markless.

Commentary: Regarding "relying on the three times" and the like — formations of all three times are, from the very outset, unarisen, unoriginated, and markless; yet the meaning differs somewhat, hence it is said "as each accordingly requires." Past and future have no genuine substantial existence, and are for this reason unarisen, unoriginated, and markless. Present formations, though their nature seems to genuinely exist, are nonetheless illusion-like, their arising possessing no fixed nature of its own, and are for this reason likewise said to be unarisen, unoriginated, and markless. One should understand this as spoken with reference to a mark's own lack of fixed nature. With reference to their own substance, it is said they are without arising or origination. With reference to their own mark, it is called markless.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi poses the vivid question this passage invites: where has yesterday's self actually gone? Search the ten directions and it cannot be found — the past has no location, no remaining substance whatsoever, and tomorrow's self is equally unfixed, not yet determined by anything that genuinely, independently exists. The present moment is subtler: something does appear to be happening right now, this very thought, this very body — yet, examined closely, even this present arising has no fixed, independent nature, each instant itself dependent on countless conditions, ceaselessly renewing rather than persisting as a single, stable thing.

He notes the careful distinction drawn here between substance and mark: with reference to their own underlying substance, all three times are said to be without arising, since nothing genuinely, independently comes into being at all; with reference to their own perceived mark or appearance, they are called markless, since even the vivid appearance of arising and ceasing has no fixed characteristic to which it could be pinned down. Having established this first of three summarizing points regarding the nature of the three times, he notes, the treatise turns next to the second point: the equally ungraspable nature of the causes underlying these same formations.

81. Milestone: Seeds Have No Genuine Accomplishing or Destroying — A Verse From the Chapter Formed Through Reflection

The treatise now presents the second summarizing point: relying on the remaining causal nature, there is neither genuine accomplishing nor genuine destroying. The commentary explains that each formation's own particular generating seed is called this "remaining causal nature," distinguished from the formation's own substance and mark; because the seed-consciousness has no genuine, substantially real capacity to turn and produce effects, it is said that there is neither accomplishing nor destroying. The commentary then cites a verse from the ultimate-truth section of the chapter formed through reflection: the present swiftly perishes; the past abides nowhere; the future depends on manifold conditions; and yet the mind continues to turn accordingly — explaining that, from the standpoint of ultimate truth, the mind's own capacity to genuinely "turn along with" formations cannot actually be established, even though, conventionally, seeds are said to be "accomplished" through habituation and "destroyed" once their function has been spent.

Chinese Source

依餘因性無成、無壞；

《披》依餘因性無成無壞者：諸行自種，名餘因性，望彼體相名為餘故。由種子識無有實隨轉性，是故說言無成、無壞。勝義伽他頌云：現在速壞滅，過去住無方，未來依眾緣，而復心隨轉。長行釋云：若心與彼諸行相應，或不相應；非此與彼或時不相應，或時非不相應。如是由勝義故，心隨轉性不得成就。

Ru-Yi Translation

Relying on the remaining causal nature, there is neither accomplishing nor destroying.

Commentary: Regarding "relying on the remaining causal nature, there is neither accomplishing nor destroying" — each formation's own particular seed is called this remaining causal nature, distinguished from that formation's own substance and mark, hence called "remaining." Because the seed-consciousness has no genuine, substantially real capacity to turn and produce effects, it is said that there is neither accomplishing nor destroying. A verse from the ultimate-truth section says: the present swiftly perishes; the past abides nowhere; the future depends on manifold conditions; and yet the mind continues to turn accordingly. The accompanying prose explains: whether the mind corresponds to these formations, or does not correspond — it is not the case that this mind, at one time not corresponding and at another time not failing to correspond, is genuinely, substantially the same throughout. In this way, from the standpoint of ultimate truth, the mind's own capacity to genuinely turn along cannot be established.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains "remaining causal nature" carefully: every dharma possesses its own particular generating seed — the visual consciousness its own seed, sound and scent and the rest each their own — these seeds distinguished from the dharma's own manifest substance and characteristic mark, hence termed "remaining" relative to that manifest appearance. He explains why seeds are said to have "no genuine accomplishing or destroying": were a seed genuinely, substantially real, it could never actually change — an afflicted mind would remain afflicted forever, a wholesome mind wholesome forever, an ordinary being ordinary forever — yet plainly the mind does shift, sometimes corresponding to greed, sometimes to hatred, sometimes to wholesome intention, precisely because no fixed, substantial "seed-nature" genuinely governs this turning at all; "accomplished through habituation" and "destroyed through exhausted function" are merely conventional, provisional descriptions laid over this genuinely fluid process.

He connects this directly to the treatise's own reconciliation of Madhyamaka and Yogācāra approaches to emptiness: where Madhyamaka speaks more directly of dependent origination as itself empty by nature, Yogācāra's own three-nature framework — the imagined, the dependent, and the perfected — offers a more granular account of precisely how something can be genuinely empty of fixed self-nature while still functioning, conventionally, as a real causal process, karma and its fruit never simply negated even as ultimate emptiness is fully affirmed. Having established this second point regarding causal nature, he notes, the treatise turns next to the third and final summarizing point: the three natures themselves, and how ultimate truth relates to what is utterly beyond words.

82. Milestone: The Three Natures Distinguished — What Is Utterly Empty, What Is Illusion-Like, and What Is Beyond All Words

The treatise now presents the third and final summarizing point: relying on ultimate truth's own utterly word-transcending, own-natured reality — the self-nature of images constructed through mere verbal designation, and of images constructed through actual doing, are both, whether considered in terms of substance and mark or in terms of causal nature, entirely without existence. Precisely because of this defiled nature, there is no genuine flowing-on, and no genuine cessation of flowing; relying on ignorant, wrongful grasping as its own cause, toward that same word-transcending, real matter, throughout the initial, middle, and final stages and every span of time, defilement is equally so; relying on suchness, undistortedly realizing the equality free of all discrimination, one is thereby able to remove defilement — this, one should understand, is the brief meaning presented here.

Chinese Source

依第一義畢竟離言諸自性事，言說造作影像自性，由體相故及因性故，都無所有；即由如是雜染體性，無流轉性；無止息性；依此無智邪執為因，於彼離言諸有體事，初中後位一切時分，染平等性；依於真如，無倒證入無有分別平等性故，能除雜染。是名此中略所說義。

Ru-Yi Translation

Relying on ultimate truth's own utterly word-transcending, own-natured reality — the self-nature of images constructed through mere verbal designation, and of images constructed through actual doing, are both, whether by substance and mark or by causal nature, entirely without existence. Precisely because of this defiled nature, there is neither genuine flowing-on nor genuine cessation of flowing. Relying on ignorant, wrongful grasping as its own cause, toward that same word-transcending, real matter, throughout the initial, middle, and final stages and every span of time, defilement is equally so. Relying on suchness, undistortedly realizing the equality free of all discrimination, one is thereby able to remove defilement. One should understand this as the brief meaning presented here.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi unpacks the three natures this passage distinguishes: the imagined nature — mere verbal designation's own constructed image, such as the word "flower" conjuring a mental picture with no corresponding real flower behind it at all — is utterly without existence, neither substance nor mark nor causal seed genuinely present; the dependent nature — the image actually constructed through karmic activity, such as an actual flower arising from causes and conditions — resembles the imagined nature in ultimately lacking fixed, independent existence, yet is not simply nothing either, appearing illusion-like the way a dream-tiger, though empty of any real tiger, still vividly appears and, within the dream, could still "attack"; and the perfected nature — ultimate truth itself, utterly beyond words, the twofold emptiness of self and dharmas — is neither the flat nothingness of the imagined nor the illusion-like appearance of the dependent, but the genuine, if inexpressible, reality both are ultimately grounded in.

He recounts Venerable Miao Jing's own classic illustration of this same distinction: told that "if you don't discriminate, the tiger isn't there," a worried student objects — but what if a real, hungry tiger actually appears in the mountains? The master's answer distinguishes precisely these two natures: the imagined "tiger" as a mere concept is indeed never there at all; but the dependent, karmically-arisen tiger genuinely can appear and genuinely can be dangerous, non-discrimination removing only the added, unnecessary layer of grasping and fear, never the actual causal event itself requiring a sensible response. He extends this to a broken car needing repair, or to an advanced meditator's own sense that the body has vanished during deep stillness — the felt absence being itself a further conceptual overlay, the body's own karmically-conditioned existence continuing regardless of what is or isn't consciously noticed. He then offers the treatise's own most memorable image for the seventh-to-eighth-ground transition: bodhisattvas below the eighth ground are like a dreamer who knows, dimly, that the dream is not fully real, yet must still cry out for help when danger appears within it — while an eighth-ground bodhisattva has genuinely awoken, the dream's own images no longer needing any effort to see through, spontaneous equanimity now simply given rather than effortfully maintained; the Buddha himself, this passage's own commentary tradition adds, must gently remind such a newly-awoken bodhisattva of the original vow to keep returning to the dream for other beings' sake, lest genuine awakening tip over into premature withdrawal from all further engagement. Having presented all three summarizing points regarding ultimate-truth wisdom, he notes, the treatise turns next to confirming that their fulfillment brings genuine entry into the eighth ground itself.

83. Milestone: Entry Confirmed — Passing From the Seventh Ground's Own Mixed Purity Into the Eighth's Purity Alone

The treatise now confirms entry into the eighth ground, closing the first aspect entirely. These ten kinds of wisdom entering into every dharma's own ultimate truth, becoming of the most excellent grade, utterly complete, cause one to pass beyond the seventh abode's own mixed purity and enter the eighth abode of purity alone.

Chinese Source

酉三、明得入　如是十種入一切法第一義智成上品故，極圓滿故，超過第七雜清淨住，得入第八純清淨住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Third, clarifying entry. These ten kinds of wisdom entering into every dharma's own ultimate truth, becoming of the most excellent grade, utterly complete, cause one to pass beyond the seventh abode's own mixed purity and enter the eighth abode of purity alone.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi recalls the treatise's own earlier characterization of the seventh ground as "mixed" — undefiled relative to what follows, yet still falling within defiled conduct by strict standard, precisely because effortful application itself was classified as a subtle residue of impurity. Here, at last, that residue is fully resolved: the eighth ground's own purity is no longer mixed with any trace of effortful striving whatsoever, spontaneous and complete.

He treats this confirmed entry as the treatise's own formal marking of the very transition the dream-and-awakening simile just illustrated so vividly — a bodhisattva genuinely, completely awake, no further effort required to see through what previously demanded constant, vigilant attention. With the first of eight aspects now fully closed, he notes, the treatise turns next to the second aspect: attaining the very pure forbearance regarding the non-arising of all dharmas that this ultimate-truth wisdom makes possible.

84. Milestone: The Four Investigations Explained in Depth — Name Is Only Name, the Matter Only the Matter

The treatise now explains the second aspect, standing and posing its own question: such a bodhisattva, abiding in this abode, certifies the bodhisattva's own foremost, most excellent, utterly pure forbearance regarding the non-arising of dharmas. What, then, does this actually consist in? The treatise answers by explaining its skillful means: bodhisattvas, through the four investigations, having correctly investigated every dharma, at the point of attaining the four kinds of genuine knowledge, genuinely know every dharma exactly as it is — at which point every wrongful, discriminating attachment is entirely departed from. The four investigations, cultivated from the very outset at the stage of resolute practice all the way to this eighth ground, examine name (mere verbal designation, containing no corresponding substance whatsoever — thinking of candy conjures no actual candy); matter (the dependently-arisen thing itself, prior to and apart from any name attached to it — an ancient people who called a flower by an entirely different sound still experienced the very same dependently-arisen matter); the imagined own-nature attributed to that matter (treating a merely nominal designation as though it named a genuinely real, independent substance); and the imagined distinctions among such matters (treating differences such as red or white, likewise merely nominal, as though they marked some genuinely real, independent quality).

Chinese Source

申二、於無生法得清淨忍　酉一、標　住此住中，於無生法，證得菩薩第一最勝極清淨忍。

酉二、徵　此復云何？

酉三、釋　戌一、明方便　謂諸菩薩由四尋思，於一切法正尋思已，若時獲得四如實智，如實了知一切諸法，爾時一切邪分別執皆悉遠離。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second aspect: certifying pure forbearance regarding the non-arising of dharmas; first, naming it. Abiding in this abode, one certifies the bodhisattva's own foremost, most excellent, utterly pure forbearance regarding the non-arising of dharmas.

Second, posing the question. What, then, does this consist in?

Third, explaining; first, clarifying the skillful means. Bodhisattvas, through the four investigations, having correctly investigated every dharma, at the point of attaining the four kinds of genuine knowledge, genuinely know every dharma exactly as it is; at that point, every wrongful, discriminating attachment is entirely departed from.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi recalls Venerable Miao Jing's own vivid analogy for why investigation alone never suffices without sustained practice: a medical student who has memorized every textbook still cannot treat a single patient without years of actual clinical practice combining theory with lived experience — a practitioner, likewise, cannot simply know about the four investigations intellectually, but must apply them again and again to one's own body, mind, and surrounding world until genuine transformation follows.

He walks through all four investigations in turn, cross-referenced to fascicle 36's own fuller treatment: investigating name recognizes that a name is merely a name, containing no corresponding substance whatsoever — the word "candy" conjures no actual candy; investigating the matter itself recognizes that the dependently-arisen thing exists, word-transcending in its own right, entirely independent of whatever name happens to be attached to it — the very same flower existed before any name was given to it, and would exist under any other name equally; investigating the imagined own-nature recognizes that treating any nominal designation as naming some genuinely independent, real substance is itself a further layer of unwarranted attachment laid over the bare dependently-arisen matter; and investigating imagined distinctions recognizes that even apparent qualities such as color are, at bottom, merely provisional descriptions of underlying causal conditions such as light itself, not independently real properties in their own right.

He notes the treatise's own careful insistence that this fourfold investigation never denies dependent origination itself — only the further, unwarranted layer of substantial, independent existence imagined on top of it — precisely the distinction already illustrated by the tiger analogy several milestones earlier. Having explained the skillful means underlying this pure forbearance, he notes, the treatise turns next to the correct contemplation this same investigation makes possible.

85. Milestone: Correct Contemplation — Neither Defilement Arising Now, Nor Any Residue of Past Wrong Attachment Ever Arising Again

The treatise now explains correct contemplation itself: observing every dharma, one accords, within one's own present experience, with the non-arising of every kind of defilement; and, observing that every wrongful, discriminating attachment previously held, together with the dharmas produced from that same cause, will, in every future time, never again arise, without any remainder whatsoever. The first half corresponds to the exhaustion-knowledge a hearer might describe as afflictions already ended; the second corresponds to the knowledge of non-arising, that no further affliction will ever arise again — except that here, for a bodhisattva, both kinds of knowledge are attained together through the wisdom of the emptiness of dharmas, itself already including the emptiness of self.

Chinese Source

戌二、明正觀　觀一切法，於現法中隨順一切雜染無生；觀彼先時一切所有邪分別執因所生法，於當來世一切無餘永不復生。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, clarifying correct contemplation. Observing every dharma, one accords, within present experience, with the non-arising of every kind of defilement. Observing that every wrongful, discriminating attachment previously held, together with the dharmas produced from that cause, will, in every future time, never again arise, without any remainder.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi distinguishes these two halves precisely: the first corresponds to what a hearer tradition calls exhaustion-knowledge, confirming that afflictive defilement is not presently arising; the second corresponds to non-arising knowledge, confirming that it will never arise again in the future either — together constituting complete freedom from affliction, both present and future. He notes that this bodhisattva's own version of these two knowledges differs from a hearer's own in being grounded specifically in the emptiness of dharmas, which necessarily already includes and surpasses the emptiness of self alone a hearer's own liberation rests upon.

Having explained both the skillful means and the correct contemplation underlying pure forbearance, he notes, the treatise turns next to pointing the reader toward the treatise's own earlier, fuller treatment of these same four investigations.

86. Milestone: Cross-Referencing the Chapter on Ultimate Reality, and Closing the Aspect — Purity Finally Reaches Its Utmost

The treatise now points to its own earlier, fuller treatment before closing the second aspect entirely. These same four investigations and four kinds of genuine knowledge are explained at length exactly as in the earlier chapter on ultimate reality — the commentary cross-referencing that chapter's own eightfold classification of wrongful discrimination (self-nature, differentiation, and totalizing discrimination generating the discriminating consciousness together with its basis and object; self- and mine-discrimination generating the view of self and self-conceit; and desire-, aversion-, and neither-discrimination generating craving, hatred, and delusion) as precisely the wrongful attachment this fourfold investigation dismantles. The treatise then closes with a summarizing statement: this genuine knowledge, cultivated from the very stage of resolute practice all the way through the abode with applied effort, having no further mark, had not yet reached its utmost purity; only now, within this eighth abode, has it become utterly pure — this is why it is said that, regarding the non-arising of dharmas, one certifies the bodhisattva's own foremost, most excellent, utterly pure forbearance.

Chinese Source

酉四、指　此四尋思、四如實智，廣說如前真實義品。

酉五、結　此如實智，始從勝解行住乃至有加行有功用無相住，未極清淨；今此住中已極清淨，是故說言：於無生法，證得菩薩第一最勝極清淨忍。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fourth, pointing to the fuller treatment. These four investigations and four kinds of genuine knowledge are explained at length exactly as in the earlier chapter on ultimate reality.

Fifth, the summary. This genuine knowledge, cultivated from the stage of resolute practice all the way through the abode with applied effort, having no further mark, had not yet reached utmost purity. Only now, within this abode, has it become utterly pure. This is why it is said: regarding the non-arising of dharmas, one certifies the bodhisattva's own foremost, most excellent, utterly pure forbearance.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this closing summary as confirming a single, continuous thread stretching all the way back to resolute practice, long before even the first ground: the very same four investigations and four kinds of genuine knowledge, cultivated with steadily increasing clarity — like peeling away an onion's own successive layers — across every intervening ground, only now reaching genuinely utmost purity at the eighth. Nothing new is introduced here; what has been practiced continuously simply reaches its own natural completion.

He notes the treatise's own significant aside: from the eighth ground onward through the tenth and into buddhahood itself constitutes the third and final of the three incalculable eons traditionally said to span a bodhisattva's entire path — genuine, direct realization of suchness having now been reached, with only refinement and completion remaining. Having closed this second aspect entirely, he notes, the treatise turns next to the third aspect: eliminating four specific calamities and thereby attaining profound abiding.

87. Milestone: Four Calamities Eliminated — Even the Very Wish to Advance Further Now Recognized as a Subtle Residue

The treatise now explains the third aspect across three parts. First, naming it: such bodhisattvas, because of attaining this forbearance, attain profound abiding — the four calamities present back at the very first markless abode now entirely eliminated. Second, listing all four: first, eliminating the very matter of applied effort and effortful application within the markless state itself; second, eliminating diligent yearning after a purer abode still higher; third, eliminating diligent yearning after great capacity for benefiting every kind of sentient being; and fourth, eliminating the presently-manifesting activity of even the most subtle conceptual notion. Third, the conclusion: this abode is therefore called utterly pure.

Chinese Source

申三、除斷災患得甚深住　酉一、標　是諸菩薩得此忍故，得甚深住。先於第一無相住中，四種災患今悉除斷。

酉二、列　一者、除斷於無相中有加行有功用事；二者、除斷於上清淨住精勤思慕；三者、除斷於一切種利有情事有大堪能精勤思慕；四者、除斷有微細想現在前行。

酉三、結　是故此住名極清淨。

Ru-Yi Translation

Third aspect: eliminating calamities, attaining profound abiding; first, naming it. Such bodhisattvas, because of attaining this forbearance, attain profound abiding. The four calamities present back at the first markless abode are now entirely eliminated.

Second, listing them. First, eliminating the very matter of applied effort and effortful application within the markless state itself. Second, eliminating diligent yearning after a purer abode still higher. Third, eliminating diligent yearning after great capacity for benefiting every kind of sentient being. Fourth, eliminating the presently-manifesting activity of even the most subtle conceptual notion.

Third, the conclusion. This is why this abode is called utterly pure.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats these four eliminations as tracing a progressively subtler residue of striving, each surprising in its own right: the first simply confirms that applied effort itself, having accomplished its purpose, is no longer needed — comparable to no longer needing to watch for highway signs once already safely on the correct road. The second is more surprising still: even the very yearning to advance toward a still-higher, purer ground — something every bodhisattva from the first through the seventh ground has genuinely needed as motivation — is itself now classified as a calamity, a residual grasping no longer appropriate once genuine spontaneity has been reached; a hearer's own comparable yearning to finally attain arhatship functions similarly at an earlier stage, useful until it, too, becomes superfluous.

The third eliminates yearning even for the capacity to benefit beings — the very thing a bodhisattva's entire path has been devoted to — since this capacity, too, has now been genuinely attained rather than merely aspired to, the mind now capable of manifesting wherever needed, exactly as Avalokiteśvara is traditionally said to appear in a thousand places at once for a thousand simultaneous appeals. The fourth eliminates the most subtle conceptual activity still present at the seventh ground — the very effort of straining toward markless samādhi is itself, at last, entirely absent, this bodhisattva's own store-consciousness itself now renamed the maturing consciousness, since its function has shifted from accumulating further karmic seeds toward simply ripening what remains. Having eliminated all four calamities and thereby confirmed utmost purity, he notes, the treatise turns next to the seven aspects of profundity this same purity comprises.

88. Milestone: Seven Aspects of Profundity, and the Buddha's Own Gentle Reminder Not to Abandon the Vow

The commentary now supplies the seven aspects of profundity the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra identifies as constituting this ground's own "profound abiding": profundity of difficult entry (suchness so thoroughly spontaneous that it can scarcely even be described); profundity of shared practice (fellow bodhisattvas below this ground cannot fully discern it); profundity of object (grasper and grasped no longer manifesting at all, the great dream truly ended); profundity of cultivation (immeasurable self- and other-benefiting practice now fully accomplished); profundity of non-regression (no hearer or solitary-realizer able to shake it, never again receding toward the lesser vehicles); profundity of freedom from obstruction (both afflictive and cognitive hindrances now genuinely far removed); and profundity of the remedy's own direct presence (this ground's own wisdom abiding markless at every moment without exception) — the Ten Grounds Sutra itself likening this entire ground to someone who has genuinely awoken from a great dream, no longer needing, as a seventh-ground bodhisattva still does, to keep telling oneself to wake up. The treatise then explains a further development: such a bodhisattva, deeply delighting in this same profound abiding, is, precisely within this flow of dharma, awakened and guided by the Tathāgatas themselves, who bestow immeasurable gateways of wisdom opening onto spiritual power and undertaking — since even here, delight in quiescence itself risks becoming an obstacle to benefiting beings, requiring the buddhas' own gentle intervention to rekindle the original vow.

Chinese Source

申四、蒙佛悟導授妙智等　又此菩薩於甚深住極生愛樂，即於如是法門流中，蒙諸如來覺悟勸導，授與無量引發門智神通事業。

《披》於甚深住極生愛樂等者：此甚深住寂靜解脫，是故菩薩於此住中，極生愛樂。如是愛樂，從彼寂靜解脫平等所流，名法門流。即於如是法門流中，如來為欲除彼由愛樂此不欲利生障故，覺悟勸導，令其愍念未能解脫一切眾生，授與無量如來妙智。由此妙智，能引一切神通所作事業。由此事業，能辦眾生一切義利。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fourth aspect: awakened and guided by the Buddha, bestowed with wondrous wisdom. Furthermore, such a bodhisattva, deeply delighting in this profound abiding, is, precisely within this flow of dharma, awakened and guided by the Tathāgatas, bestowed with immeasurable gateways of wisdom opening onto spiritual power and undertaking.

Commentary: Regarding "deeply delighting in this profound abiding" and the like — this profound abiding is quiescent liberation itself; this is why such a bodhisattva, within this abode, deeply delights in it. This delight flows from that same quiescent, liberated equality; this is called the flow of dharma. Precisely within this flow of dharma, the Tathāgatas, wishing to remove the obstacle to benefiting beings that arises from delighting in this state, awaken and guide such a bodhisattva, causing genuine, compassionate regard for all sentient beings not yet liberated, and bestowing the Tathāgatas' own immeasurable, wondrous wisdom. By this wisdom, every undertaking of spiritual power is drawn forth. By this undertaking, every benefit for sentient beings is accomplished.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi shares two illustrative stories here: first, a monk resting by a lotus pond, gently scolded by the pond's own guardian spirit for "stealing" its fragrance merely by enjoying it, losing mindfulness in that subtle pleasure — when the monk protests that another had stolen the pond's actual lotus roots without rebuke, the spirit explains that an ordinary, coarse person is simply beyond correction, not worth the effort, whereas a genuine practitioner, so close to attainment, is worth correcting precisely because the correction can actually help; second, the story of a monastic who grudgingly grants a pregnant beggar-woman an early, threefold portion of alms (for herself, her unborn child, and her dog) before the ritual signal has even sounded, only to have her vanish into the sky — recognizing, too late, that she had been Mañjuśrī testing his capacity for genuinely equal-minded generosity, and nearly gouging out his own eyes in anguished remorse before being stopped.

He treats both stories as illustrating the same principle this fourth aspect describes directly: a teacher's silence or gentle correction reflects the student's own genuine readiness, not indifference — and the buddhas' own intervention here, waking a deeply-delighted eighth-ground bodhisattva from quiescence's own subtle pull back toward the original vow, exemplifies precisely this same compassionate, well-timed guidance, arriving unbidden exactly when readiness allows it to actually help. Having explained this awakening and the wisdom it bestows, he notes, the treatise turns next to the fifth aspect: realizing the wondrous wisdom of manifesting countless emanation-bodies, and attaining tenfold sovereignty.

89. Milestone: Countless Emanation-Bodies, and Tenfold Sovereignty — a Thousand Places, a Thousand Simultaneous Responses

The treatise now opens the fifth aspect: having thus been awakened and guided by the Buddha, such a bodhisattva draws forth immeasurable, wondrous wisdom regarding emanation-bodies, and attains tenfold sovereignty — the wording following, the treatise notes, exactly as the sutra explains at length, directing the reader there for the precise mark of each. No longer confined, as an ordinary being would be, to appearing in only one place at a time, such a bodhisattva, like Avalokiteśvara answering a thousand simultaneous appeals with a thousand simultaneous appearances, manifests wherever needed at once.

Chinese Source

申五、悟入分身妙智及得自在　酉一、指經說　如是蒙佛覺悟勸導，引發無量分身妙智，得十自在。如經廣說，應知其相。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fifth aspect: realizing the wondrous wisdom of emanation-bodies, attaining sovereignty; first, pointing to what the sutra says. Thus awakened and guided by the Buddha, such a bodhisattva draws forth immeasurable, wondrous wisdom regarding emanation-bodies, and attains tenfold sovereignty. This follows exactly as the sutra explains at length; one should understand its mark accordingly.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi notes the direct continuity with the previous aspect: having been reminded by the buddhas not to abandon the original vow, this bodhisattva's own wisdom now expresses itself concretely as the capacity for countless simultaneous emanation-bodies, precisely the capacity needed to fulfill that vow at the scale it actually requires — no longer bound, as an ordinary being is, to a single location, but present wherever any sentient being's own need calls.

He anticipates that the treatise will next enumerate this tenfold sovereignty in detail, following the sutra's own list — sovereignty over life, mind, resources, karma, birth, aspiration, vow, spiritual power, dharma, and wisdom, each a further facet of the same underlying spontaneous mastery this ground as a whole has been building toward. Having opened this fifth aspect with its own pointer to the sutra, he notes, the treatise turns next to explaining this tenfold sovereignty's own actual content.

90. Milestone: Ten Sovereignties in Full — Life, Mind, Resources, Karma, Birth, Vow, Resolute Understanding, Wish-Fulfillment, Dharma, and Wisdom

The treatise now details this tenfold sovereignty in full. Attaining sovereignty, one abides however one wishes, able to abide at will; abides in whatever meditative absorption, liberation, or other mental state one delights in, able to abide at will; whatever provisions such as food and other necessities are merely thought of for even a moment, all are accomplished, and every kind of worldly craft and skillful learning manifests exactly as wished; universally, regarding every karma capable of producing rebirth, and every place of rebirth, one takes birth exactly as wished, entirely at will; whatever one delights in, every undertaking of spiritual power is able to be performed; every excellent vow is fulfilled exactly as wished; giving rise to resolute understanding regarding any matter, whatever is wished becomes exactly so, without variance; whatever object one wishes to know is genuinely known exactly as it is; universally, regarding the entire body of names, phrases, and letters, one obtains whatever is wished; and in correctly establishing every dharma, one attains genuine skill. The commentary maps each phrase directly onto the Ten Grounds Sutra's own named list — sovereignty over life, mind, resources, birth, spiritual power, vow, resolute understanding, wisdom, dharma, and karma — while noting that Venerable Miao Jing's own independent analysis, checked directly against the Flower Ornament Sutra, the Lotus Sutra, and the Compendium of the Great Vehicle, arrives at a mapping that agrees on eight of the ten but differs from the commentary's own sequence on two points regarding which phrase names karma-sovereignty and which names wish-fulfillment sovereignty.

Chinese Source

酉二、辨自在　得自在故，隨所欲住，如意能住；隨樂安住靜慮、解脫等諸心住，如意能住；若暫思惟一切食等諸資生具，悉皆成辦，一切世間工業明處，如其所欲悉能現行；普於一切能感生業，及於一切受生處所，皆隨所欲自在往生；隨所愛樂，一切神通所作事業皆能起作；一切妙願，隨其所欲皆得稱遂；隨於事物發起勝解，如所欲為皆成無異；隨所欲知所知境界皆如實知；普於一切名句文身得隨所欲；於一切法正安立中皆得善巧。

《披》得十自在等者：十地經說：得命自在、得心自在、得物自在、得業自在、得生自在、得願自在、得信解自在、得如意自在、得法自在、得智自在，是名得十自在。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, distinguishing sovereignty. Attaining sovereignty, one abides however one wishes, able to abide at will. Abiding in whatever meditative absorption, liberation, or mental state one delights in, able to abide at will. Whatever provisions such as food are merely thought of for even a moment, all are accomplished; every worldly craft and skillful learning manifests exactly as wished. Universally, regarding every karma producing rebirth and every place of rebirth, one takes birth exactly as wished, entirely at will. Whatever one delights in, every undertaking of spiritual power is able to be performed. Every excellent vow is fulfilled exactly as wished. Giving rise to resolute understanding regarding any matter, whatever is wished becomes exactly so, without variance. Whatever object one wishes to know is genuinely known exactly as it is. Universally, regarding every name, phrase, and letter, one obtains whatever is wished. In correctly establishing every dharma, one attains genuine skill.

Commentary: Regarding "attaining tenfold sovereignty" — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: attaining sovereignty over life, mind, resources, karma, birth, vow, resolute understanding, wish-fulfillment, dharma, and wisdom — these are called the ten sovereignties.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through all ten in turn: life-sovereignty (able to extend one's own lifespan at will, whether a hundred years or an entire eon, unlike an ordinary being for whom even reaching old age is rare and death arrives unbidden); mind-sovereignty (able to enter any absorption or liberation at will, unlike an ordinary meditator for whom even access concentration is hard-won and easily lost); resource-sovereignty (food, robes, and every other necessity manifesting the instant they are thought of, together with complete mastery of worldly crafts); karma-sovereignty (able to manifest whatever karmic result would best teach a given being, entirely free of any actual unwholesome or neutral karma oneself); birth-sovereignty (able to choose exactly where, to whom, and in what form to be born, unlike an ordinary being who cannot even be certain of a human rebirth); spiritual-power-sovereignty (able to manifest transformation throughout every buddha-land simultaneously); vow-sovereignty (every vow, including the eight classical marks of a buddha's own career, fulfilled exactly as wished); resolute-understanding-sovereignty (a conviction so decisive it can transmute one element into another, associated with the classical eight liberations and eight bases of mastery over attachment to visual and elemental appearances); wisdom-sovereignty (genuine, direct knowledge of whatever object is wished to be known, already partaking of the buddhas' own ten powers, four fearlessnesses, and eighteen exclusive qualities); dharma-sovereignty (unobstructed mastery over the entire body of names, phrases, and letters constituting the teaching itself, at both worldly and ultimate levels).

He notes candidly that Venerable Miao Jing's own analysis and the commentary's own sequential mapping disagree specifically on which phrase names karma-sovereignty versus wish-fulfillment (spiritual-power) sovereignty — a genuine scholarly puzzle the commentary itself leaves for the reader's own further judgment rather than resolving definitively. He adds the Ten Grounds Sutra's own further threefold grouping of all ten: sovereignty over the physical world (resource-sovereignty, freely transforming the material environment), sovereignty over the world of sentient beings (birth- and karma-sovereignty, freely transforming rebirth and its result), and sovereignty over one's own awakened wisdom (vow, resolute understanding, dharma, wisdom, spiritual power, mind, and life together) — and a final, poignant correspondence: each of the ten sovereignties directly overcomes one of ten classical fears, life-sovereignty overcoming the fear of death, mind-sovereignty overcoming the fear of affliction's own stain, resource-sovereignty overcoming the fear of poverty, karma-sovereignty overcoming the fear of unwholesome karma, birth-sovereignty overcoming the fear of unfortunate rebirth, vow-sovereignty overcoming the fear of unfulfilled wishes, resolute-understanding-sovereignty overcoming the fear of slandering the dharma, wish-fulfillment-sovereignty overcoming the fear of restless, unsettled seeking, dharma-sovereignty overcoming the fear of doubt, and wisdom-sovereignty overcoming the fear of facing a great, formidable assembly. Having explained all ten sovereignties in full, closing the fifth aspect, he notes, the treatise turns next to the sixth aspect: the excellent benefit this same sovereignty confers.

91. Milestone: The Excellent Benefit of Sovereignty — Great Wisdom, Great Karma, and Seven Further Qualities

The treatise now explains the sixth aspect: such a bodhisattva, having attained this sovereignty, from this point onward receives the excellent benefit this attainment produces, exactly as the sutra explains at length. The commentary cites the Ten Grounds Sutra's own naming of the resulting titles: such a bodhisattva is now called one of inconceivable wisdom, of immeasurable wisdom, of vast wisdom, of unbreakable wisdom, and so on at length — this bodhisattva's own attainment of such wisdom-power displaying every undertaking without fault in any matter whatsoever. Grouped under three headings: great wisdom (neither abiding in saṃsāra nor in nirvana); great karma (bodily, verbal, and mental karma now utterly pure); and, arising from both together, seven further qualities of great excellence — skillfully abiding on the path of great compassion and wisdom; unforgetting, through the power of retentive recollection; accomplished in verbal karma, skillful in teaching the dharma; sovereign mastery of mind, spiritual power reaching everywhere at once; accomplished through vow-power, freely transforming into whatever form guides beings from evil toward good; accomplished in cultivation, the perfection of vow especially heightened; and joined with wisdom, the buddhas' own all-encompassing wisdom now directly present, however incompletely.

Chinese Source

申六、領受所得自在勝利　如是菩薩獲得自在，從是已去，所得自在所作勝利，廣說如經，應知其相。

《披》所得自在所作勝利等者：十地經說：是菩薩得是菩薩十自在已，即時名為不可思議智者、無量智者、廣智者、不可壞智者，乃至廣說是菩薩得如是智力，示一切所作，一切事中無有過咎故。此應準知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Sixth aspect: receiving the excellent benefit of attained sovereignty. Such a bodhisattva, having attained sovereignty, from this point onward receives the excellent benefit this attainment produces, exactly as the sutra explains at length; one should understand its mark accordingly.

Commentary: Regarding "the excellent benefit this attainment produces" — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: this bodhisattva, having attained these ten sovereignties, is at once called one of inconceivable wisdom, of immeasurable wisdom, of vast wisdom, of unbreakable wisdom, and so on at length — this bodhisattva attaining such power of wisdom, displaying every undertaking without fault in any matter. This should be understood accordingly.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through the seven further qualities in turn: skillfully abiding on the path unites great compassion with great wisdom as this bodhisattva's own settled way of life; unforgetting draws on the power of dhāraṇī, retentive memory so strong that nothing genuinely learned is ever again lost; accomplished verbal karma names skillful, faultless teaching of the dharma to any audience; sovereign mastery of mind names spiritual power reaching, as already explained, every place at once; accomplished through vow-power names the capacity to freely manifest as any form — even as an evildoer, a beast, or an outcast, should compassion require it — while never actually performing an evil deed oneself, using such manifestation solely to guide other beings from wrongdoing toward wholesome conduct; accomplished cultivation singles out the perfection of vow as this ground's own especially heightened perfection among the ten; and joined with wisdom names the buddhas' own comprehensive wisdom now genuinely present in this bodhisattva's own mind, if not yet in its fullest, most complete measure.

He treats this sixth aspect as the direct, lived consequence of the fifth aspect's own tenfold sovereignty — sovereignty is not an abstract capacity but issues concretely in titles the wider tradition itself confers, and in qualities of settled excellence a bodhisattva at this ground genuinely, visibly embodies. Having explained this excellent benefit, he notes, the treatise turns next to the seventh aspect: purified wholesome roots, given once more with escalated similes of gold and light.

92. Milestone: Never Once Departed From Seeing the Buddha — Gold Fit for a King's Own Crown, Light Rivaling a Thousand-World Brahmā

The treatise now explains the seventh aspect in two parts. First, naming the cause: whereas earlier grounds still beheld the buddhas somewhat coarsely, such a bodhisattva is now able to relinquish even this coarse beholding of the buddhas, constantly, without interruption, never once departed from seeing the buddha — genuine, uninterrupted communion with suchness itself now counting as seeing the buddha directly, beholding dependent origination's own true nature being itself beholding the dharma and thereby the buddha. Second, pointing to the sutra's own similes: every other purified wholesome root follows the gold-simile and light-simile the sutra itself explains, which should be understood accordingly — the commentary supplying both in full: just as a skillful goldsmith takes genuine gold and fashions from it an ornament fit to adorn the very neck or crown of Jambudvīpa's own king, an ornament no other treasure throughout that entire continent can outshine, so too this bodhisattva's own wholesome roots, abiding at the ground of immovability, cannot be surpassed by any hearer, solitary-realizer, or bodhisattva of even the seventh ground — this bodhisattva's own great wisdom-light dispelling the darkness of every sentient being's own affliction through the very gateway of skillfully discerning wisdom; and just as a Mahābrahmā ruling a thousand world-systems can, in a single moment, spread loving-kindness pervading all thousand worlds at once, and emit light illuminating them equally, so too this bodhisattva, abiding at the ground of immovability, emits bodily light illuminating sentient beings across countless world-systems numbering as many as the fine motes of dust in ten thousand myriad great trichiliocosms, gradually extinguishing the fire of their affliction and bringing them to genuine coolness.

Chinese Source

申七、善根清淨　酉一、舉因緣　又能棄捨麤見諸佛，恆常無間不離見佛。

酉二、指經喻　其餘所有善根清淨，金喻、光喻，如經應知。

《披》金喻光喻如經應知者：十地經說：譬如本真金，善巧金師作莊嚴具已，繫在閻浮提王若頸、若頂，閻浮提人餘寶莊嚴具無能奪者。如是佛子！菩薩住此不動地，彼諸善根，一切聲聞、辟支佛，乃至七地菩薩所不能壞。菩薩得是地，大智光明滅諸眾生煩惱闇障，以善分別智門故。佛子！譬如千世界主大梵天王，能於一時流布慈心滿千世界，亦能放光遍照其中。如是佛子！菩薩住此菩薩不動地中，能放身光照十千萬三千大千世界微塵數世界眾生，漸能除滅諸煩惱火，令得清涼。

Ru-Yi Translation

Seventh aspect: purified wholesome roots; first, naming the cause. Furthermore, able to relinquish even the coarse beholding of the buddhas, constantly, without interruption, never once departed from seeing the buddha.

Second, pointing to the sutra's own similes. Every other purified wholesome root, the gold-simile and light-simile, should be understood exactly as the sutra explains.

Commentary: Regarding "the gold-simile and light-simile, exactly as the sutra explains" — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: just as genuine gold, fashioned by a skillful goldsmith into an ornament, is fastened upon the very neck or crown of Jambudvīpa's own king, and no other treasure-ornament throughout Jambudvīpa can outshine it — so too, O sons of the buddhas, a bodhisattva abiding at this ground of immovability has wholesome roots that no hearer, solitary-realizer, nor even a seventh-ground bodhisattva can destroy. Such a bodhisattva, attaining this ground, dispels every sentient being's own dark obstruction of affliction through the great light of wisdom, by means of the very gateway of skillfully discerning wisdom. O sons of the buddhas! Just as a Mahābrahmā ruling a thousand worlds is able, in a single moment, to spread loving-kindness pervading a thousand worlds, and is also able to emit light illuminating them equally — so too, O sons of the buddhas, a bodhisattva abiding at this ground of the bodhisattva's own immovability is able to emit bodily light illuminating sentient beings across world-systems as numerous as the fine motes of dust in ten thousand myriad great trichiliocosms, gradually extinguishing every fire of affliction, bringing them to coolness.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats "never once departed from seeing the buddha" as this ground's own distinctive completion of a theme running through every prior ground: earlier bodhisattvas beheld buddhas through meditative absorption or through vow-empowered rebirth in a buddha-land, an encounter still occasional and effortful; here, constant abiding in suchness itself simply is constant beholding of the buddha, since dependent origination's own true nature and the dharma-body itself are, at bottom, the same reality.

He notes the simile's own final escalation: gold now fit specifically for a king's own crown or necklace — the most exalted human adornment imaginable — surpassed by no lesser ornament throughout an entire continent, precisely as this ground's own wholesome roots cannot be surpassed even by a seventh-ground bodhisattva; and the light-simile drawing on Mahābrahmā, ruler of a thousand worlds, whose own compassion and radiance pervade that entire domain at once — this bodhisattva's own light now reaching not merely a thousand worlds but countless trichiliocosms, extinguishing affliction's own fire and bringing genuine coolness to beings on a scale the earlier grounds' own similes could not yet match. Having explained purified wholesome roots through both similes, he notes, the treatise turns next to the eighth and final aspect: rebirth and majestic power together.

93. Milestone: Rebirth as the Great Brahmā Ruling a Thousand Worlds, and Majestic Power Beyond All Prior Counting

The treatise now explains the eighth and final aspect: this bodhisattva's own rebirth and majestic power, every excellent matter belonging to them, follow exactly as the sutra explains at length; one should understand their mark accordingly. The commentary cites the Ten Grounds Sutra directly: such a bodhisattva, abiding at this ground, most often becomes a great Brahmā ruling a thousand world-systems, sovereign and supreme, and so on at length — one should understand rebirth's own mark accordingly. It further says: through the power of diligence, within a single instant, such a bodhisattva attains a number of absorptions equal to the fine motes of dust in a million great trichiliocosms, and so on at length, able to transform the body into that same number of forms, and, within each and every one of those transformed bodies, able to display a retinue of bodhisattvas equal to that same number of motes of dust — one should understand majestic power's own mark accordingly.

Chinese Source

申八、受生威力　此住菩薩受生、威力諸殊勝事，皆如經說，應知其相。

《披》受生威力諸殊勝事皆如經說者：十地經說：菩薩住是地中，多作大梵天王，諸千世界，自在最勝，乃至廣說。如是應知彼受生相。又說：以精進力，於一念間得百萬三千大千世界微塵數三昧，乃至廣說能變身為百萬三千大千世界微塵數，於一一身能示百萬三千大千世界微塵數菩薩以為眷屬。如是應知彼威力相。

Ru-Yi Translation

Eighth aspect: rebirth and majestic power. This bodhisattva's own rebirth and majestic power, every excellent matter belonging to them, follow exactly as the sutra explains at length; one should understand their mark accordingly.

Commentary: Regarding "every excellent matter belonging to rebirth and majestic power, exactly as the sutra explains" — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: such a bodhisattva, abiding at this ground, most often becomes a great Brahmā ruling a thousand world-systems, sovereign and supreme, and so on at length. One should understand rebirth's own mark accordingly. It further says: through the power of diligence, within a single instant, such a bodhisattva attains a number of absorptions equal to the fine motes of dust in a million great trichiliocosms, and so on at length, able to transform the body into that same number of forms, and, within each and every one of those transformed bodies, able to display a retinue of bodhisattvas equal to that same number of motes of dust. One should understand majestic power's own mark accordingly.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi notes rebirth's own continued ascent beyond the desire realm entirely: the first seven grounds climbed through the six desire-realm heavens, culminating at the highest, while this eighth ground's own rebirth now surpasses the entire desire realm, reaching Mahābrahmā himself, ruler of an entire thousand-world cluster in the form realm — a fitting culmination for the ground whose own light-simile, two milestones earlier, already invoked this same Mahābrahmā directly.

He notes that majestic power's own scale has now grown so vast — motes of dust in a million trichiliocosms — that the earlier grounds' own progression through hundreds, thousands, and koṭis has been left behind entirely for a wholly different order of magnitude, fitting for the ground marking entry into the path's own final, third incalculable eon. Having explained rebirth and majestic power, closing all eight aspects, he notes, the treatise turns next to a concluding summary naming them together, followed by the cross-reference explaining why this ground is called, in the wider tradition, the ground of immovability, together with nine further alternate names.

94. Milestone: The Summary Names All Eleven Items, and Ten Names for a Single Ground — Immovable, Unturning, Hard to Attain, a Prince

The treatise now closes the entire abode without applied effort, without effortful application, having no further mark, with a concluding summary and final cross-reference. In brief, this is called the bodhisattva's abode without applied effort, without effortful application, having no further mark: because entry into every dharma's own ultimate-truth wisdom reaches complete fulfillment; because forbearance regarding the non-arising of dharmas is attained; because every calamity is eliminated; because the bodhisattva's own profound abiding is attained; because, within the flow of dharma, one is bestowed by the Buddha with immeasurable gateways of wisdom opening onto spiritual power and undertaking; because the wisdom of immeasurable emanation-bodies is realized; because sovereignty is attained; because the excellent benefit of that sovereignty is received; because of purified wholesome roots; because of rebirth; because of majestic power. Should one wish to hear this expounded at greater length, the treatise directs the reader to the Ten Grounds Sutra's own chapter on the ground of immovability — explaining that, within this ground, relinquishing every prior path involving applied effort and effortful application, the mind rises to a supreme, immovable path without applied effort, without effortful application, spontaneously turning; this is why this ground is named the ground of immovability; and, by this very same meaning, one should understand this is exactly what is here called the abode without applied effort, without effortful application, having no further mark. The commentary adds that the Ten Grounds Sutra names this same ground by ten further titles: immovable (its wholesome roots unbreakable); unturning (Mahāyāna wisdom never regressing); hard to attain (its own state scarcely knowable to the ordinary world); a prince's ground (free of the household's own afflictive faults); a ground of birth (sovereign, taking birth exactly as wished); a ground of accomplishment (its undertaking already complete, comparable to a hearer's own arhatship); the ultimate ground (its own skillful discernment already so excellent, though buddhahood itself remains ahead); the ground of nirvana (its own great vow-power already so well established); the ground of increase (immovable, unshaken); and the ground without effortful application (having skillfully given rise to the noble, markless path).

Chinese Source

未二、結略義　當知是名略說菩薩無加行無功用無相住。謂入一切法第一義智成滿得入故；得無生法忍故；除斷一切災患故；逮得菩薩甚深住故；於法門流，蒙佛授與無量引發門智神通事業故；悟入無量分身智故；得自在故；領受所得自在勝利故；善根清淨故；受生故；威力故。

午二、廣指　未一、指　若廣宣說，如十地經不動地說。

未二、釋　於此地中，捨先所有有加行、有功用道，其心昇上無加行、無功用、任運而轉不動勝道，是故此地名不動地。

未三、配　即由此義，當知說名無加行無功用無相住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, closing the brief meaning. One should understand this, in brief, as the bodhisattva's abode without applied effort, without effortful application, having no further mark — namely, because entry into every dharma's own ultimate-truth wisdom reaches complete fulfillment; because forbearance regarding the non-arising of dharmas is attained; because every calamity is eliminated; because the bodhisattva's own profound abiding is attained; because, within the flow of dharma, one is bestowed by the Buddha with immeasurable gateways of wisdom opening onto spiritual power and undertaking; because the wisdom of immeasurable emanation-bodies is realized; because sovereignty is attained; because the excellent benefit of that sovereignty is received; because of purified wholesome roots; because of rebirth; because of majestic power.

Second, the broader pointer; first, pointing to it. Should one wish to hear it expounded at length, it is exactly as the Ten Grounds Sutra expounds it in its chapter on the ground of immovability.

Second, explaining. Within this ground, relinquishing every prior path involving applied effort and effortful application, the mind rises to a supreme, immovable path without applied effort, without effortful application, spontaneously turning; this is why this ground is named the ground of immovability.

Third, the correspondence. By this very meaning, one should understand this is named the abode without applied effort, without effortful application, having no further mark.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi reviews all eleven items as a final capstone: ultimate-truth wisdom's own complete fulfillment; forbearance regarding non-arising through the four investigations; four calamities eliminated yielding sevenfold profundity; the Buddha's own awakening and guidance; countless emanation-bodies; tenfold sovereignty; the resulting excellent benefit; purified wholesome roots (gold fit for a king's crown, light rivaling a thousand-world Brahmā); rebirth as Mahābrahmā himself; and majestic power beyond all prior counting.

He treats the ten alternate names the Ten Grounds Sutra itself supplies as together painting a remarkably full portrait of this single ground from ten distinct angles — unbreakable, non-regressing, scarcely knowable, free of household affliction, sovereign over birth, its task essentially accomplished, excellent though not yet final, its vow-power fully established, immovable, and spontaneously arisen — each name capturing some genuine facet of the same underlying attainment. With this correspondence confirmed, the entire abode without applied effort, without effortful application, having no further mark — the eighth ground, examined across all eight aspects — reaches its complete conclusion. The treatise turns next to the tenth of the eleven-part granular analysis's own next abode: the abode of unobstructed understanding, the ninth ground, also called the ground of excellent wisdom, where a bodhisattva perfects the fourfold unobstructed understanding needed to become a genuine, great dharma-teacher.

95. Milestone: The Abode of Unobstructed Understanding Opens — the Ninth Ground, Perfecting the Great Dharma-Teacher's Own Fourfold Mastery

The treatise now opens the tenth abode: unobstructed understanding, corresponding to the ninth ground, also called the ground of excellent wisdom — the ground where a bodhisattva perfects the qualities of a genuine, great dharma-teacher through fourfold unobstructed understanding: unobstructed understanding of dharma (the teaching itself, the very words and letters that convey it); unobstructed understanding of meaning (the sense those words actually convey); unobstructed understanding of language (every sentient being's own tongue, whatever it may be); and unobstructed understanding of eloquence (skillfully discerning immeasurable meanings and illustrating them through fitting analogies, so that eloquence itself flows unimpeded). The question is posed: what is a bodhisattva's abode of unobstructed understanding?

Chinese Source

辰十、無礙解住　巳一、問　云何菩薩無礙解住？

Ru-Yi Translation

Tenth abode, unobstructed understanding; first, the question. What is a bodhisattva's abode of unobstructed understanding?

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi introduces the ninth ground's own governing theme: where the eighth ground perfected spontaneous, effortless realization of ultimate truth, this ninth ground turns that same realization outward into the specific capacity to teach it — the four kinds of unobstructed understanding together constituting exactly what makes someone a genuine "great dharma-teacher" rather than merely a realized practitioner. He explains each in turn: unobstructed understanding of dharma masters the teaching's own textual, terminological content; unobstructed understanding of meaning masters what that content actually conveys; unobstructed understanding of language masters every sentient being's own tongue, so that no audience is ever linguistically out of reach; and unobstructed understanding of eloquence masters the living, spontaneous art of actually conveying all of this persuasively, fluently, and without hesitation, drawing on whatever analogy a given audience most needs to finally understand.

He notes that this ninth ground builds directly and necessarily on the eighth: only a bodhisattva whose own realization has become fully spontaneous and effortless can, in turn, make the teaching of that realization equally spontaneous and effortless to any audience whatsoever — realization and communication maturing together as two faces of a single, continuous development. Having posed the opening question, he notes, the treatise turns next to answering it in full.

96. Milestone: Not Yet Satisfied — Delighting in Entering a Wisdom Higher Still Than the Eighth Ground's Own Profundity

The treatise now explains the first of five aspects distinguishing the abode of unobstructed understanding. Such bodhisattvas, not yet satisfied with the profound abiding already attained at the eighth ground, further delight in entering the very excellent nature of heightened wisdom still higher — the commentary explaining that this heightened wisdom names the Tathāgatas' own wondrous wisdom, and its excellent nature names the ultimate accomplishment of benefiting both oneself and others that such wisdom alone makes possible.

Chinese Source

巳二、答　午一、略說　未一、別辨相　申一、於殊勝智愛樂隨入　謂諸菩薩，於甚深住不生喜足，復於增上智殊勝性愛樂隨入。

《披》增上智殊勝性者：如來妙智，名增上智。能利自他究竟所作，名殊勝性。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, the answer; first, the brief account; first, distinguishing each mark; first, delighting in entering excellent wisdom. Namely, bodhisattvas, not yet satisfied with profound abiding, further delight in entering the very excellent nature of heightened wisdom still higher.

Commentary: Regarding "the excellent nature of heightened wisdom" — the Tathāgatas' own wondrous wisdom is called heightened wisdom. The ultimate accomplishment of benefiting both oneself and others is called its excellent nature.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this opening as a striking reminder that even the eighth ground's own profound, spontaneous abiding — already so remarkable that the treatise likened it to genuine awakening from a great dream — is not itself the final resting point: a bodhisattva here still presses onward, not from any dissatisfaction born of craving, but from genuine delight in wisdom still more excellent, oriented now specifically toward the buddhas' own wondrous, all-encompassing wisdom rather than merely toward one's own liberation.

He notes the significant shift in emphasis this ninth ground represents: the eighth ground perfected realization itself; this ninth ground now turns that realization toward the specific, practical accomplishment of benefiting both self and others through actual teaching — wisdom's own excellent nature measured precisely by this capacity to help. Having named this delight in entering excellent wisdom, he notes, the treatise turns next to the second aspect: arousing wisdom's own applied effort to teach every kind of dharma.

97. Milestone: Arousing Wisdom's Own Applied Effort to Teach Every Kind of Dharma to Every Kind of Being

The treatise now explains the second aspect: such bodhisattvas, regarding every dharma, arouse wisdom's own applied effort, and should, for the sake of others, teach every kind of dharma — a vast undertaking requiring genuinely comprehensive wisdom, since teaching effectively means addressing vastly different kinds of dharma to vastly different kinds of sentient beings, each according to their own specific need and capacity.

Chinese Source

申二、起智加行宣說諸法　是諸菩薩於諸法中起智加行，應為他說一切種法。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second aspect: arousing wisdom's own applied effort to teach the dharma. Such bodhisattvas, regarding every dharma, arouse wisdom's own applied effort, and should, for the sake of others, teach every kind of dharma.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats this brief aspect as the direct, active consequence of the first: delight in entering excellent wisdom (the first aspect) naturally issues in the diligent effort actually to teach that wisdom to others (this second aspect) — realization and its transmission moving together as a single continuous motion rather than two separate stages.

Having named this arousal of wisdom's own applied effort, he notes, the treatise turns next to the third aspect: genuinely, thoroughly knowing everything that teaching the dharma actually requires — an aspect of extraordinary scope, cataloguing eleven distinct "thickets" of complexity a great dharma-teacher must master.

98. Milestone: Eleven Thickets of Complexity — Mastering the Mind's Own Eightfold Tangle, Affliction's Own Depths, and Karma's Ninefold Web

The treatise now opens the third aspect: universally, regarding everything teaching the dharma actually requires, genuine knowledge exactly as it is. One should understand that what teaching the dharma requires means being able to genuinely know, regarding conduct approaching every kind of dense thicket — dense thicket meaning both "manifold" and "difficult to know" — what is defiled and what is pure in this way, what causes this defilement and this purity, what becomes defiled and what becomes purified, and whether a given case is not fixed one way, is fixed one way, or partakes of both. The commentary supplies the Ten Grounds Sutra's own list of eleven such thickets: the thicket of mental conduct, of afflictive conduct, of karmic conduct, of faculty-conduct, of faith-conduct, of disposition-conduct, of profound-mind conduct, of latent-tendency conduct, of birth-conduct, of habituation-conduct, and of the threefold-grouping conduct. The mind's own thicket alone unfolds across eight distinct marks — its own distinguishing character across the six consciousnesses; its own momentary arising and ceasing; its own ultimate markless nature, apart from any body; its own boundless capacity to grasp any object whatsoever; its own inherently unstained nature, neither wholesome nor unwholesome in itself; its own varying correspondence, or lack of correspondence, with affliction; its own bondage or release from latent tendencies; and the causal difference between a bodhisattva's own vow-generated rebirth and an ordinary being's own karma-generated rebirth. Affliction's own thicket unfolds across three: how deeply it penetrates, reaching even to the summit of existence; how boundlessly difficult it is to fully know, spanning countless accumulated wholesome roots' own struggle against it; and how it defiles, whether bound together with the mind or not, correlated with any of the eighty-four thousand classical divisions of affliction. And karmic conduct's own thicket unfolds across nine: whether wholesome, unwholesome, or neutral; whether already performed or not yet performed; whether bound together with the mind or not; the fixed sequence by which a karmic cause, once accumulated, is never lost even across countless past eons; whether its fruit has already ripened or not yet ripened; whether black, white, mixed, or neither black nor white; its own causal conditions, themselves immeasurable; whether already accumulated or not yet accumulated; and whether its timing and vehicle are fixed or not fixed — down to genuinely knowing all eighty-four thousand distinct kinds of karma.

Chinese Source

申三、如實了知說法所作　普於一切說法所作皆如實知。當知此中說法所作，謂於一切近稠林行，如此雜染、如此清淨，由此雜染、由此清淨，若所雜染、若所清淨，若非一向、若是一向、若通二種，如是一切皆如實知。

《披》謂於一切近稠林行等者：十地經說：心行稠林、煩惱行稠林、業行稠林、根行稠林、信行稠林、性行稠林、深心行稠林、使行稠林、生行稠林、習氣行稠林、三聚差別行稠林，是名一切近稠林行。

Ru-Yi Translation

Third aspect: genuinely knowing everything that teaching the dharma requires. Universally, regarding everything teaching the dharma requires, one genuinely knows exactly as it is. One should understand that what teaching the dharma requires means: regarding conduct approaching every kind of dense thicket, genuinely knowing what is defiled in this way, what is pure in this way, what causes this defilement, what causes this purity, what becomes defiled, what becomes purified, whether a case is not fixed one way, is fixed one way, or partakes of both — genuinely knowing all of this exactly as it is.

Commentary: Regarding "conduct approaching every kind of dense thicket" — the Ten Grounds Sutra says: the thicket of mental conduct, of afflictive conduct, of karmic conduct, of faculty-conduct, of faith-conduct, of disposition-conduct, of profound-mind conduct, of latent-tendency conduct, of birth-conduct, of habituation-conduct, and of the threefold-grouping conduct — these are called every kind of conduct approaching a dense thicket.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains why "thicket" is the chosen image: a genuine forest's own density and difficulty of passage mirrors exactly how manifold and hard-to-discern sentient beings' own mental life actually is — an ordinary being cannot even fully map their own present mind, let alone another's, whereas a ninth-ground bodhisattva must master eleven such thickets across every sentient being in order to teach effectively at all, an image the treatise itself likens to reading the lines of one's own palm with total clarity despite their genuine intricacy.

He walks through the mind's own eight marks as together painting a complete picture: distinguishing among the six consciousnesses and their own associated mental factors; observing the mind's own momentary birth and cessation; recognizing that, in ultimate truth, mind has no fixed, findable form at all, separate from any body; recognizing the mind's own capacity to engage boundlessly many objects across every possible domain; recognizing the mind's own basic nature as neutral, taking on wholesome or unwholesome coloring only through what accompanies it; distinguishing when the mind does or does not correspond with affliction; distinguishing bondage from release with respect to affliction's own latent, seed-form tendencies; and finally the causal difference between a bodhisattva who takes rebirth purely through vow-power and an ordinary being carried along helplessly by karmic force alone. He then walks through affliction's threefold thicket (penetrating even to the most refined level of existence, defying complete cataloguing, and correlating precisely with whichever of the eighty-four thousand classical divisions applies) and karmic conduct's ninefold thicket, noting the memorable exchange between Śāriputra and Subhūti on whether dream-actions produce karmic fruit — Subhūti's own careful answer distinguishing genuinely willed, completed, deliberate action (which does ripen) from fleeting, powerless mental movements such as dream-conduct (which generally do not), the Mahāprajñāpāramitā-śāstra itself confirming that only intentional, followed-through, correctly-targeted action constitutes a genuinely complete karmic path.
