Having explained the first three of eleven thickets in full, he notes, the treatise turns next to the remaining eight.

99. Milestone: Eight Further Thickets — Faculties, Faith, Disposition, Habituation, Rebirth, and the Threefold Grouping of Beings

The treatise's commentary now details the remaining eight thickets. The thicket of faculties covers the differing sharpness of sentient beings' own capacities, their past and future distinctions, their belonging to one of the three vehicle-lineages, and every subtlety of whether a hearer's own lineage is fixed or provisional. The thicket of faith-conduct covers the differing ways confidence itself arises — sometimes through genuine understanding (grasping why something is true firsthand), sometimes simply through absence of doubt (trusting a reliable source without needing to verify directly), different temples and different teaching styles naturally drawing different beings toward faith by different routes. The thicket of disposition-conduct covers settled character, habituated through repetition until it becomes difficult to change — illustrated by a monk so fixed in his resolve to renounce the household life that he threatened his own life rather than relent, until his own mother, faced with losing him either way, finally consented. The thicket of profound-mind conduct covers cultivated, habituated preference itself — some beings drawn by disposition toward monastic life, others toward medicine, others toward teaching, each preference itself a kind of thicket. The thicket of latent-tendency conduct covers the seed-level tendencies underlying manifest conduct, the deep, often centuries-old momentum driving a being's own habitual patterns forward. The thicket of birth-conduct covers the manifold forms and dwelling-places of rebirth across every realm. The thicket of habituation-conduct covers the residue left by repeated association — a monastic who spends too much time among worldly, status-seeking company gradually absorbs that same worldly flavor, exactly as the classical Venerable Gavāṃpati's own past-life habit of chewing cud persisted bodily into a later rebirth. And the thicket of the threefold grouping covers the fixed, wrong, and indeterminate groupings of sentient beings with respect to their own eventual liberation, together with every further distinction of wholesome and unwholesome conduct, good and bad rebirth-paths, and non-Buddhist, hearer, and bodhisattva paths alike.

Chinese Source

（節錄《披尋記》）根行稠林……信行稠林……性行稠林……深心行稠林……使行稠林……生行稠林……習氣行稠林……三聚差別行稠林……是名一切近稠林行。

Ru-Yi Translation

The thicket of faculties, the thicket of faith-conduct, the thicket of disposition-conduct, the thicket of profound-mind conduct, the thicket of latent-tendency conduct, the thicket of birth-conduct, the thicket of habituation-conduct, and the thicket of the threefold-grouping conduct — together with the three already explained, these are called every kind of conduct approaching a dense thicket, all of which a bodhisattva teaching the dharma must genuinely know exactly as it is.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi draws out two especially memorable illustrations from this section. First, on faith's own twofold origin — understanding-based and trust-based — he notes that a devotee who becomes convinced of Buddhism after hearing a profound sutra expounded and a devotee who becomes convinced after simply feeling moved by a temple's own chanting and atmosphere have arrived at the very same faith through entirely different routes, both genuine, neither superior, since every temple and every teaching style resonates with a different range of beings according to their own causes and conditions. Second, on disposition's own remarkable fixity, he recounts the story of a monk who, when his mother threatened suicide rather than let him renounce the household life, countered that he would take his own life first if refused — a standoff his mother finally yielded to, illustrating just how settled certain karmic dispositions toward the path can become, resistant even to a parent's most desperate opposition.

He also highlights the classical figure of Gavāṃpati, whose habit of chewing cud in a past life as a cow persisted bodily into a subsequent human, monastic rebirth — offered as vivid proof that habituation-conduct's own thicket can span lifetimes, some habits carried forward from the deep past, others freshly acquired through present company, which is precisely why the treatise elsewhere cautions monastics against too much familiarity with worldly, status-driven circles. Having now completed all eleven thickets, closing the third aspect entirely, he notes, the treatise turns next to the fourth aspect: how such comprehensive knowledge, joined with retentive mastery, makes a bodhisattva a genuine great dharma-teacher.

100. Milestone: Becoming a Great Dharma-Teacher — Boundless Retention, Illustrated Through the Buddha's Own Skillful Encounter With a Proud Brahmin

The treatise now opens the fourth aspect: such a bodhisattva, skilled in every aspect of teaching and in every undertaking that teaching requires, becomes, in every respect, a genuine great dharma-teacher, obtaining immeasurable gateways of retentive recollection, skillfully analyzing every kind of sound, word, and grammatical component, eloquence itself inexhaustible. The commentary elaborates: because of possessing immeasurable retentive recollection, whatever is heard is never forgotten, the meaning of the dharma thoroughly understood — hence called skillful analysis; and because of being able to expound this for others through countless distinct approaches, eloquence itself is called inexhaustible — this very quality is precisely what it means to be a bodhisattva who has become genuinely capable of retentive reception.

Chinese Source

申四、成大法師　酉一、明領受　如是菩薩，於說法中方便善巧，於說所作方便善巧，於一切種成大法師，獲得無量陀羅尼門，於一切種音詞支具剖析善巧，辯才無盡。

《披》於一切種音詞支具等者：謂佛宣說相應、助伴、隨順、清亮、有用、相稱、應順名句文身所有言論，是名一切種音詞。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fourth aspect: becoming a great dharma-teacher; first, clarifying retentive reception. Such a bodhisattva, skilled in teaching the dharma and in every undertaking teaching requires, becomes, in every respect, a genuine great dharma-teacher, obtaining immeasurable gateways of retentive recollection, skillfully analyzing every kind of sound, word, and grammatical component, eloquence inexhaustible.

Commentary: Regarding "every kind of sound, word, and grammatical component" — this refers to the buddhas' own proclaimed teaching, coherent, mutually supporting, according, clear and bright, useful, fitting, and accordant in every name, phrase, and letter it employs.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi recounts the story Venerable Miao Jing tells of the Buddha's own skillful teaching encounter with the brahmin Bhāradvāja: the Buddha, begging alms daily at this same brahmin's house, perceived the brahmin's own growing discomfort at feeling perpetually indebted, and, arriving one day, taught first before accepting any food at all — reciting that seasonal rain, falling again and again, ripens the crop again and again; cultivating merit again and again brings its fruit again and again; but repeated rebirth likewise brings repeated death, and one may well ask who, ultimately, is doing all this repeated dying. Struck with shame at being so transparently seen through, the brahmin rushed to offer a full bowl of food — which the Buddha, having deliberately taught before accepting anything, then declined, explaining that no being in heaven or earth could actually digest an offering given specifically in response to the dharma; instructed instead to pour it into insect-free water, the offering caused the water to boil and smoke as though poured onto red-hot iron, so overwhelming was its own accumulated merit — moving the brahmin to request ordination on the spot, his hair and beard falling away of their own accord as the Buddha simply said, "come, monk," swiftly attaining arhatship thereafter.

He treats this story as directly illustrating what "skillful in every undertaking teaching requires" actually looks like in practice — reading a listener's exact state of mind, choosing precisely the right moment and the right words, and following through with a demonstration decisive enough to dissolve pride entirely. He explains dhāraṇī itself as a gateway of such powerful retentive recollection that a single syllable can unlock and express an entire range of meanings, wholesome and unwholesome alike, whatever is once heard becoming permanently, effortlessly available — the very foundation making boundless, inexhaustible eloquence genuinely possible rather than merely aspirational. Having explained retentive reception as the foundation of becoming a great dharma-teacher, he notes, the treatise turns next to displaying the actual capacities such reception makes possible.

101. Milestone: Displaying True Capacity Begins — Eight Qualities of Sound Perfecting Every Word a Teacher Speaks

The treatise now opens the second part of the fourth aspect: displaying true capacity. Such a bodhisattva, having accomplished this capacity for retentive reception of the dharma's own retentive formula, is thereby able, through this excellent unobstructed understanding, to draw forth speech capable of occupying the very seat of this wondrous dharma-teaching itself. The commentary elaborates on the eight qualities perfecting every word such a teacher speaks: coherent (names, phrases, and letters correctly ordered in sequence); mutually supporting (each part of speech reinforcing the sequence of what is taught); according (explained in accordance with the very point being made); clear and bright (phrasing so lucid that meaning shines through unmistakably); useful (genuinely moving the listener's own heart to actual practice, the way an effective advertisement moves a customer to buy); fitting (matched precisely to this particular audience's own capacity); worthy (teaching the noble path itself, worthy of the offerings of gods and humans alike); and accordant (perfectly timed and suited to the occasion) — together constituting every kind of sound a buddha, and, in more limited measure, a ninth-ground bodhisattva, brings to teaching.

Chinese Source

酉二、顯堪能　戌一、標　成就如是法陀羅尼領受堪能菩薩，由此勝無礙解引發言辭，能坐如是微妙法座。

《披》……一切種音詞……相應、助伴、隨順、清亮、有用、相稱、應順名句文身所有言論，是名一切種音詞。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, displaying true capacity; first, naming it. Such a bodhisattva, having accomplished this capacity for retentive reception of the dharma's own retentive formula, is thereby able, through this excellent unobstructed understanding, to draw forth speech capable of occupying the very seat of this wondrous dharma-teaching.

Commentary: Regarding every kind of sound — coherent, mutually supporting, according, clear and bright, useful, fitting, worthy, and accordant, in every name, phrase, and letter employed — this is called every kind of sound.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi elaborates on "useful" with a memorable modern comparison: just as an effective advertisement is crafted specifically to move a customer's own desire to purchase, a buddha's own teaching is never spoken idly, but always crafted to actually move the listener toward practice — the Buddha does not merely suggest meditation; the suggestion itself carries the force needed to actually prompt the student to sit down and meditate. He notes "fitting" as requiring genuine discernment of what a specific audience, in this specific moment, actually needs to hear, and "accordant" as requiring correct timing and manner down to the smallest detail of occasion and decorum.

Having explained the eight qualities of sound, he notes, the treatise turns next to the eight qualities of grammatical structure completing a teacher's own perfected speech.

102. Milestone: Eight Qualities of Speech Itself — the Missionary Who Nearly Overfed His Only Listener

The commentary now details the eight qualities of "grammatical component" completing a great teacher's own perfected speech: foremost speech (taking nirvana's own palace as the very first priority, exactly as a hearer takes nirvana and a bodhisattva takes unsurpassed awakening as the ultimate destination); most wondrous speech (a voice made beautiful by ripened karmic reward, as sweetly resonant as the mythical kalaviṅka bird's own song, purified by a lifetime free of false, frivolous, or divisive speech); utterly clear speech (phrasing that speaks in the listener's own idiom, using local terms and images so that the teaching feels immediately familiar rather than foreign); easily understood speech (skillful explanation moving fluidly from simple to profound, illustrated through apt analogy until understanding dawns); speech that is a joy to hear (drawing the listener's own genuine desire to grasp the dharma's meaning more deeply); speech never contrary to what is needed (knowing precisely how much a given listener can actually absorb — neither too much nor too little); speech relying on nothing (spoken without any desire for fame or gain); and boundless speech (a single word capable of unfolding boundless further meaning, as though a single syllable could sustain months of further explanation).

Chinese Source

《披》……成就最上首語、極美妙語、甚顯了語、易悟解語、樂欲聞語、無違逆語、無所依語、無邊際語，是名一切種支具。

Ru-Yi Translation

Accomplishing foremost speech, most wondrous speech, utterly clear speech, easily understood speech, speech that is a joy to hear, speech never contrary to what is needed, speech relying on nothing, and boundless speech — these are called every kind of grammatical component.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi illustrates "utterly clear speech" with the example of regional dialect — a Taipei speaker and a Kaohsiung speaker use entirely different words for the very same object, and speaking in whichever term feels native to the listener creates immediate warmth and trust that a foreign-sounding term never could. He illustrates "speech never contrary to what is needed" with a missionary's own comic misjudgment: discouraged that only a single person showed up to hear him preach, he was reminded by that very listener that even a single chicken still deserves to be fed — heartened, he then talked at such length that the same listener finally interrupted to ask whether he intended to feed that one chicken so much grain it would burst; teaching too much overwhelms exactly as teaching too little starves, and knowing the audience's own true capacity is the entire skill.

He treats "speech relying on nothing" as a pointed caution against teaching for personal fame — a teacher eager to appear on television or broadcast widely risks teaching from a place of self-promotion rather than genuine benefit, and any resulting error would then mislead a correspondingly vast audience. With all eight qualities of grammatical component now explained, closing the account of perfected speech itself, he notes, the treatise turns next to the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra's own list of twenty qualities constituting the complete work of a great dharma-teacher, beginning with eight times when teaching should not even be attempted.

103. Milestone: Eight Times Never to Teach — the King in Grief, in Anger, in Danger, Without a Loyal Minister

The commentary now cites the Commentary on the Ten Grounds Sutra's own twenty qualities constituting a great dharma-teacher's complete work, beginning with a classical verse: just as a king overcome by grief, by illness, by rage, by attachment to pleasure, in a place of danger, without bodyguards, surrounded by flatterers, or lacking a loyal minister, should not be addressed even by his own wisest counselor in any of these eight circumstances, so too the mind itself, likened to that same king, should not be taught the dharma when any of these eight conditions apply. Grief and illness alike leave no room for the dharma to actually enter the mind; rage, like a fire already blazing, must be allowed to cool before any teaching can land; attachment to sensory pleasure leaves a listener simply unwilling to hear anything that would draw them away from it; danger demands immediate escape, not a discourse on the eightfold path; absence of wholesome support, companionship with flatterers, and absence of a genuine spiritual friend all likewise leave the mind unready to receive what is taught.

Chinese Source

（十地經論卷11引偈）如王懷憂惱、病、恚、著諸欲、嶮處、無侍衛、讒佞、無忠臣，如是八難時，智臣不應語。心王亦如是，非時不應說。

Ru-Yi Translation

Just as a king overcome by grief, by illness, by rage, by attachment to pleasure, in a place of danger, without bodyguards, surrounded by flatterers, or lacking a loyal minister — in these eight difficult circumstances, even a wise minister should not address him. The mind itself is just the same: at the wrong time, one should not teach.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi shares two illustrative stories for these eight difficult times. For grief, he recalls a distressed student whom no amount of direct dharma-counsel could reach, until a simple walk by the lake and a bowl of ice cream finally softened her enough to admit she had been mistaken — comfort, not doctrine, being what grief actually needs first. For attachment to pleasure, he recounts a devoted lay follower of Venerable Miao Jing who longed for ordination for years but could never bring himself to leave his wife — not because she argued or scolded him, but because a single silent tear from her was enough to break his resolve completely, illustrating just how powerfully entrenched desire can block even a genuine spiritual longing.

He treats the king-and-minister image as capturing something universal about timing in teaching: the message itself might be entirely correct, yet delivered into a mind gripped by grief, illness, anger, desire, danger, or bad company, it simply cannot land — wisdom in teaching means recognizing these eight conditions and waiting, however correct one's own words might otherwise be. Having explained these eight wrong times for teaching, he notes, the treatise turns next to the remaining conditions completing the great teacher's own full twenty qualities.

104. Milestone: The Remaining Qualities — Correct Posture, Unbroken Sequence, Free of Envy, and Five Qualities of the Teacher's Own Purified Heart

The commentary continues through the remaining conditions completing the great teacher's own twenty qualities. Not teaching at the wrong time, and not teaching without sincere respect present, whether through arrogance or through treating the dharma as mere intellectual play; correct posture, such as never teaching while seated below a listener who remains standing, exactly as the monastic precepts themselves specify; giving freely, holding back nothing from genuine seekers out of any fear of being surpassed by one's own student; continuing without interruption whenever genuine interest remains, setting aside all envy toward how accomplished a student might become; teaching in proper sequence, one point building on the last; keeping to a single topic without straying, so that listeners are never left confused about what is actually being taught; opening up whatever should genuinely be opened; rejoicing together with a listener's own genuine joy in the teaching; and exhorting listeners toward bodhicitta itself, capable of transforming timidity into genuine courage — the teacher, in turn, personally embodying present wisdom and inferential wisdom alike, transmitting the correct view faithfully, teaching in ways that never undermine the teaching itself, staying focused rather than scattered like a tangled thicket, teaching correctly in full accordance with the four noble truths and the six perfections, and adapting fittingly to whatever kind of assembly is actually present. Finally, five qualities concern the teacher's own inward purity: a mind of loving-kindness extended equally even to those with whom one has some private grievance; a mind of peace, willing to speak even at personal risk if a single needed word could prevent someone from committing wrong; a mind of compassion for the suffering and of genuine concern for the complacent; a mind entirely unattached to gain or reputation; and a mind that neither praises itself nor disparages others, rejoicing instead whenever any other teacher, anywhere, succeeds in spreading the dharma.

Chinese Source

（十地經論卷11所說二十種能作法師事，節錄）非時不應說；正意乃說；頓（不慳法）；相續（不休息，捨嫉妒意）；漸；次；句義漸次；示；喜；勸（勸發菩提心）；具德者（現智、比智、阿含、所證）；不毀；不亂；如法；隨眾。又：慈心、安隱心、憐愍心、不著利養名聞、不自讚毀他。

Ru-Yi Translation

Not the wrong time; teaching only with sincere respect present; giving freely, without any stinginess toward the dharma; continuing without interruption, setting aside envy; teaching in proper sequence; one point building on the last; keeping to a single topic; opening what should be opened; rejoicing together with genuine joy; exhorting toward bodhicitta. A teacher possessing virtue: present wisdom, inferential wisdom, faithful transmission, and personal realization. Never undermining the teaching; never scattered like a thicket; correctly according with the true dharma; adapting to whatever assembly is present. Furthermore: a mind of loving-kindness; a mind of peace; a mind of compassion; a mind unattached to gain and reputation; a mind that neither praises itself nor disparages others.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats "giving freely, without stinginess" as addressing a very human temptation — a teacher who withholds some crucial piece of knowledge out of fear a student might eventually surpass them, the way a martial arts master in old stories sometimes kept a secret technique in reserve; genuine teaching means holding nothing back that a student is genuinely ready to receive. He treats "continuing without interruption, setting aside envy" the same way — never fearing that a student's own eventual excellence might somehow diminish the teacher.

He explains present wisdom and inferential wisdom as the two registers a good teacher draws on together: present wisdom speaking from direct realization and lived experience of ordinary and ultimate truth alike, inferential wisdom reasoning skillfully toward conclusions not yet personally verified but nonetheless soundly derived — a teacher ideally possesses both, though even inferential wisdom alone, correctly applied, genuinely helps. He treats the closing five qualities of inward purity as the necessary complement to everything already listed: technical skill in speech and timing means little without a heart genuinely free of favoritism, self-interest, and rivalry underneath — precisely summed up in the maxim that when one monastic praises another, the entire dharma flourishes, whereas envy between teachers only diminishes it for everyone. With all twenty qualities of a great dharma-teacher now explained in full, he notes, the treatise turns next to closing this entire fourth aspect with a final restatement and pointer to the sutra.

105. Milestone: Closing the Fourth Aspect — Guiding, Comforting, and Settling Every Sentient Being Into Wholesome Practice

The treatise now closes the fourth aspect across two final parts. First, listing the resulting capacity: within this unobstructed understanding, in whatever place, proclaiming the true dharma exhaustively through every possible gateway, thereby guiding, comforting, and settling sentient beings into wholesome undertakings — every one of these capacities now genuinely accomplished. Second, pointing to the sutra's own fuller treatment: all of this, exactly as the sutra explains at length, should be understood accordingly — the commentary specifying that the Ten Grounds Sutra itself elaborates ten further distinctions of the fourfold unobstructed understanding's own function: with reference to a dharma's own specific characteristic; with reference to shared characteristics; with reference to conduct; with reference to teaching; with reference to wisdom; with reference to freedom from pride; with reference to the lesser and greater vehicles alike; with reference to the bodhisattva grounds; with reference to the Tathāgata's own ground; and with reference to sustaining and establishing the teaching itself.

Chinese Source

戌二、列　若於是中、若於是處宣說正法盡所有門，若由此故，於諸有情勸導、慰喻、安處事業，此等堪能皆悉成就。

戌三、指　如是一切，廣說如經，應知其相。

《披》……十地經中廣說四無礙解所有作業，是名盡所有門……依自相、依同相、行相、說相、智相、無我慢相、小乘大乘相、菩薩地相、如來地相、作住持相。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, listing the capacity. Within this, and in whatever place, proclaiming the true dharma exhaustively through every possible gateway; by this, guiding, comforting, and settling sentient beings into wholesome undertakings — all such capacities are genuinely accomplished.

Third, pointing onward. All of this, exactly as the sutra explains at length, should be understood accordingly.

Commentary: The Ten Grounds Sutra explains at length every function of the fourfold unobstructed understanding — with reference to specific characteristic, shared characteristic, conduct, teaching, wisdom, freedom from pride, the lesser and greater vehicles, the bodhisattva grounds, the Tathāgata's own ground, and sustaining the teaching.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats "guiding, comforting, and settling" as a natural threefold sequence any effective teacher follows: first guiding a being away from wrongdoing, then comforting whatever fear or confusion the guidance itself might stir up, and finally settling that being securely into an actual wholesome practice, rather than leaving guidance merely theoretical. He notes the ten further distinctions the sutra itself elaborates as covering everything from a teaching's own most specific technical content up through how the teaching itself must be preserved and sustained across generations — the entire apparatus of skillful teaching now brought to its own complete fulfillment.

With this fourth aspect — becoming a great dharma-teacher through retentive reception and the full display of that capacity — now closed entirely, he notes, the treatise turns next to the fifth and final aspect distinguishing the abode of unobstructed understanding: purified wholesome roots.
